mr president, the car of today is, in some ways, very much the same as that of 100 years ago but, at the same time, it is extremely different regarding security, efficiency and its effect on the environment. this proves one thing: the strength of competition develops technology at a pace we cannot imagine. it also underlines the need for europe to have a competitive car industry if we are to contribute to the global agenda and to better standards for the environment in the global economy, because, if we do not, we will not be able to influence that development. those who call for a policy that would decrease the competitiveness of the european car industry do not contribute to a better environment – it is important to state that. it is also important to state that the target of 125 g, as stated in the report, is acceptable and a step forward. but the important thing is not to discuss emissions from individual cars – big or small – because we need big cars and we will need small cars: they play a different role in different parts of our union, in our countries. what we need is an overall development that decreases emissions in all sorts of cars. therefore, it is important to support new cars, because they are better, to support better road systems, better transport systems, better fuels and better engines. that can only be done by a competitive european car industry and that can only be achieved if we have the right policies to balance the environment and competitiveness, and this report makes a good contribution towards that.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur for a good job on an important and sensitive issue. we all know how important the car industry is in the eu. our car industry, especially in my own country, is at the forefront of development, research, performance, the environment and safety. a successful automotive industry is important for the union. it is about jobs and growth, but it is also about a responsibility for our environment, for the global climate. the commission is proposing far-reaching legislation aimed at mandatory co2 emission reductions in terms of emissions and improved vehicle technology and the use of biofuels. the rapporteur is concerned about setting targets which are so difficult for industry to achieve that they risk the competitiveness of companies, as we have heard earlier in this debate. i've heard similar arguments at home. i understand and share this concern, but i think it would be unfortunate at this stage to lower the level of ambition on the part of parliament. i have great confidence that the automotive industry in the eu can achieve high-level objectives through research and development. it's been shown before. our citizens want their cars at a reasonable cost, but our citizens also know that climate change must be tackled, and that it must be done quickly.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank our colleague, mrs piia-noora kauppi, for a very good report. in a balanced way, she describes the problems and difficulties that arise in the internal market when we have 27 different tax systems. globalisation, as mentioned, has further increased the need for a common approach to taxation within the eu, so that obstacles to competition can be avoided. different rules and bureaucracy also make companies lose economic strength and we lose jobs within the eu. we need clearer rules and an approach that favours entrepreneurship, as kauppi proposes. perhaps some of us need to remove some of our shadow patches. as you know, taxes are a very sensitive subject. the sensitive word is tax competition and the autonomy of nations to set tax rates. the commission's long-term objective is a consolidated corporate tax base. in the absence of such a possibility, targeted action should be sought in those areas which favour the efficiency of the internal market. the report sets out the various options and shows that individual countries have chosen different paths, which is good but not enough. it is in principle reasonable for companies to be able to offset losses within a company or between companies in a consortium even across borders. to facilitate such a system, a common view of what is to be taxed is needed, i.e. a consolidated corporate tax base. we in the alde group think that this is the right way. the introduction of the ccctb does not in itself prevent tax competition, rather the opposite. the base will be shared and the visibility improved. this new system will improve the possibilities for smes in particular to offset their losses. worried finance ministers - there are many of them - can feel reassured. your power to tax remains. as regards amendment no 1, our group would like to see a split vote where the first part, which concerns excise duties, can be dealt with separately. we can also consider abstaining on the vote on mrs gottardi's amendments to recitals e and f in order to allow for a broad consensus and support here in the house for this excellent report.
mr president, with this new form of debate i could of course use this minute to ask the commissioner what the current state of play is concerning ccctb in the council, because we know that some ministers of finance are not very happy. could you please, mr commissioner, tell us how the debate is going on within the council and what the 27 member states are proposing today?
for the verts/ale group. madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr blokland for the excellent cooperation we have had throughout this process. it is ten years since the rotterdam convention was signed, which said that prior written authorization would be required for the export of dangerous chemicals. after all, it is not just any chemical that is on the list. the list of chemicals includes the most dangerous chemicals that man uses on earth today. so it's been ten years. one reason for this is the old commission's stubborn attitude that it was a purely trade issue. i thank stavros dimas for giving us the appropriate legal basis to move forward. nor has it always been easy to negotiate with the council. i was shocked when the presidency thought that for a long time, life-threatening chemicals, which are virtually banned in the eu, should be exported to other countries without prior consent. the reason for this was that the council did not want european industry to suffer from competitive disadvantages. the real competitive disadvantage for our industry, however, would be if it had to risk health, the environment and international law to survive. it is absurd to say that we must break international conventions just because others do not follow rules and standards. then you might as well say that the eu should export illegal drugs, because otherwise the mafia does. i'm glad we finally came to an agreement and finally plugged the loophole of unauthorized exports. we now have legislation that not only covers conventional chemicals but also includes requirements for other chemicals that are severely restricted in the eu. these now have to be evaluated by the competent authorities from a health and environmental point of view before export. it is good that the time for exemption has been reduced from 36 to 12 months. it is also good that chemicals in products are covered. finally, we have legislation which is acceptable and improves on the commission's proposal, but which could have been even better if public health and the environment had been allowed to prevail over the interests of business, which is the only sustainable economic model.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. when regulating the form of an agreement between two different parties, it is obvious that account must be taken of whether the parties are equivalent or whether one party has an advantage from the outset. if one party has an advantage, the contract rules must take this into account and find a framework which strengthens the weaker party. in the case of consumer credit, which we are discussing now, it is the person who needs the credit who is in the minority. unfortunately, neither the rapporteur nor the compromise proposal takes sufficient account of this responsibility, of consumer rights and protection. this is particularly serious, since it is those with the weakest financial resources who are the ones who are actually asking for credit when they make purchases. i would also like to say that, despite the fact that we have a decision to apply gender mainstreaming in all our work here in parliament, no gender analysis has been carried out on this directive, and despite the fact that we know that many women, especially those with the lowest wages, are often the ones who fall into the debt trap. i am arguing that there must be a ceiling on the level of compensation for early repayment of loans. i also argue that three days' notice is far too short a time. i would also like to see a minimum harmonization, not a maximum harmonization.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking roberta angelilli and all the shadow rapporteurs, but also all the members who have contributed to the fact that we will soon have the first decision of the european parliament on an eu strategy on the rights of the child. the most important issue is the participation and influence the kids. in my home country, sweden, where childbearing is forbidden, every preschooler and every young person knows that adults are not allowed to hit children. the fact that we are now also making clear that we need to work together to stop all abuse of children means that we need to work more closely with actors such as banks, travel agencies, credit companies and currency exchange companies to stop child pornography, sex tourism and the exploitation of children, and to make the internet safer from paedophiles. in addition, illegal websites must be able to be closed. member states must also ensure that children are not a commodity through a sex-purchase law. the most difficult issue we have had in committee has been adoption. i am very pleased that we have now agreed that children interests of the adults. we all remember what happened in orphanages in romania and in guatemala recently, with the kidnappings of children for adoption that we have on our web page. children are not commodities. it is now up to the commission to listen to the wisdom that we in parliament have found and then to come forward with concrete proposals on how to enshrine the rights of the child, which now, with the new lisbon treaty, will become an objective and a law in the eu. with the new lisbon treaty, the eu must listen and ensure that children's rights are also included in all its work. this should of course also apply to the global perspective, to development work, to culture and to all areas. child poverty will of course be a central issue, but also the plight of children in war and all health issues. i am proud to be in this parliament which will be taking this decision tomorrow.
the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion. - mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur for an excellent report. it is linked to the whole strategy we have within the eu and the whole lisbon process that education is something central, not only to how we will cope with growth and employment in the future but also to how we will be able to combat social exclusion. education and training, not just more jobs but also better jobs and highly skilled people, play a very important role in enabling eu countries to compete globally. that is why we welcome this report. i would like to make a few further comments. it is important that we use all the opportunities available to enable education for different groups. one example is child care. it must be possible for both men and women who are parents of young children to participate in education. therefore, there must also be a strategy for childcare. it's important that we look at special groups, like the elderly. there are very few older people in the workforce today. this is largely due to the lack of access to continuing training and adult education. we need to focus on that group. another group is already lowly educated. if we look at education today, continuing education and adult education, we see that those who already have higher education are the ones who get the most education. it is important that we have an equal aspect in education as well. finally, as my speaking time is short, i would just like to point out the importance of good cooperation and the involvement of the social partners in this process.
in writing. the commission proposal contains a wide range of detailed rules on how airport charges are to be regulated. the commission is competent to monitor compliance with community competition law. the proposals in question, however, involve excessive bureaucracy and detailed regulation, which is detrimental to the member states which have opted to deregulate the aviation sector. recommends that the common principles on airport charges apply only to airports with more than five million passenger movements per year, or where the annual traffic exceeds 15% of the number of passenger movements in the member state concerned; this position is preferable to the commission's proposal, which also includes smaller regional airports. we are often faced with the balancing act of supporting common eu rules in order to achieve equal treatment for all actors operating in the internal market. in this case, it is clear that the commission's approach is an unwarranted bureaucratization. on the basis of the above arguments, we voted in favour of the european parliament's proposal, but rejected that legislative resolution in the final vote.
in writing. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the labelling, presentation and advertising of foodstuffs for sale to the ultimate consumer. the commission considers that the measure is imputable to the state and that it is imputable to the state. however, the civil aviation authority and the smaller airlines have been hesitant about a new legislation. for the smaller airlines, new legislation and harmonisation is more of a threat, as they often choose to fly at competing airports with lower fares. i voted against the directive for a number of reasons. airports are already regulated by national authorities and their actions are restricted by existing eu competition law. the commission notes that the airport services agreement with ryanair was signed on 29 june 2012 and that the agreement was signed on 30 june 2012. it is then up to the member states to assess whether their charges are competitive or not. the european parliament has reduced the scope of the directive, i voted in favour of the amendments which limit the directive to 67 airports (compared with the 150 or so airports that would have been affected before). however, i cannot support price regulation at eu level.
in writing. − the single market is the eu's greatest contribution to democracy and prosperity in europe. the aim of this report is to improve the functioning of the internal market by achieving harmonisation of tax bases, in this case by harmonising the way in which losses in companies are to be managed when several countries are involved. the report is, however, based on an ill-conceived premise. the harmonization of tax bases varies greatly between countries, depending on the different structures of the economy. some countries have large elements of heavy basic industry with huge long-term investments, while others are dominated by service companies and light manufacturing. we therefore believe that the question of the harmonization of tax bases should be investigated more thoroughly before a report such as this is discussed in parliament.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. it is important that targets are now set for the number of professional inspectors in europe. the number of occupational inspectors should be one per 10 000 workers, as recommended by the ilo. it is regrettable, however, that after the cuts made by the government, sweden will no longer meet the requirements of the ilo and now the european parliament. after the government's cuts, sweden will have only 0.7 inspectors per 10,000 employees. compared with, for example, denmark, where the figure is just over two.
in writing. - the junilist considers that osh is essentially a national concern. obviously, everything from asbestos cleaning in workplaces to the protection of health care workers working with sharp tools are important issues. however, we have full confidence in the competence of the member states to devise adequate national osh strategies. we therefore question the concrete added value of the eu's views in this area. on the basis of the above arguments, we voted in favour of the amendments aimed at restricting the osh strategy and against the final vote.

in writing. − the rights of the child are undoubtedly a universal and inviolable right and junilistan is pleased to note that all eu member states have ratified the un convention on the rights of the child. this means that there is already binding international legal legislation protecting children from child labour, trafficking, violence and a range of other interferences in their lives. in addition, child rights is an area of swedish law that uncompromisingly puts the best interests of the child first. we have chosen to abstain from voting on the report for the simple reason that the rapporteur seems to have completely lost sight of the best interests of children in his report. the report focuses almost exclusively on the specific social model that member states should introduce in their respective countries to achieve what the european parliament considers to be the best solution. the report covers everything from the prohibition of violence on television and the sale of violent computer games to forced marriages, illegal adoptions and illegal activities. we have of course voted in favour of the amendments which stress the importance of fully respecting the un convention on the rights of the child as a guarantee of the legal security of children and their universal human rights.
in writing. - one point was of concern to all the groups. in paragraph 127 of the original report, there was a proposal calling on all eu member states to ban children from wearing the headscarf in school. as private individuals, we sympathize with the basic idea of protecting the rights of children to play freely, to participate in school gymnastics and to enjoy a certain protection against parental coercion even as minors. however, such a complex and sensitive issue can hardly be solved at eu level. the states of europe weigh the rights and obligations of children, parents and the state in the light of their own history and political situation. we fully agree on the importance of the eu creating a good framework. paragraph 127 is reasonably outside such a framework.

madam president, i would like to thank you. we have created a single market with free movement of goods, services, capital and people. almost all of them have joined schengen. in practice, all border controls within the eu have been abolished. this is a giant leap forward from a liberal point of view. we no longer have to stand with our hats in the face of bureaucrats when we want to study, work, tour or seek medical treatment in another eu country. freedom, however, has a downside. with the disappearance of border controls, the scope for organized crime increased enormously. this has facilitated drug trafficking, dealing and smuggling. sometimes this expansion of organised crime is used as proof of the need for the eu. this is grotesque. the expansion depends on the eu, a price we are forced to pay for increased freedom and prosperity. how much is that? yes, police resources may have to increase by 1-2 percent of gdp at the national level to roll back this organized crime. such a national increase in resources is the most important and perhaps the only means of restoring a decent europe. the rapporteur wants to transfer the power of the police to brussels. europol shall become an eu body financed from the eu budget. he takes issues of integrity and democratic legitimacy lightly. we should therefore reject this report, invest in the development of national police resources and let europol remain a body of cooperation between states.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. when we in the committee on employment usually discuss the lisbon strategy, we talk about high growth, a high level of employment, good social conditions, good health care, and we also say that the education system and the development of skills, among other things, should be a driving force in order to maintain this. if we apply all these objectives to the roma minority in europe, we see an extremely high level of unemployment. we don't see very good social conditions, not very good health care, not very good housing, a segregated society for them, and that the education system is segregated as well. there are special education programmes for roma which do not give them the right to adequate education. yet the lisbon strategy is for everyone. you had some good suggestions. we support them, but it is important that the strategy be followed up by an action plan. it is important that we have mainstreaming, but at the same time there must be coordination within the commission. it is important that we review our funding so that it targets the roma minority. it is important that when we make strategies in the field of employment, education and other areas, we always take account of the situation of the roma so that they are integrated into the different strategies.

member of the commission. − madam president, the idea of an internet governance forum (igf) was born at the world summit on the information society in tunis in november 2005 and defined as a non-binding forum for multi-stakeholder policy discussion. the commission supports the forum in this form. two meetings have been held so far: in 2006 in athens and 2007 in rio. the commission welcomes the forum in each form. the commission also welcomes the participation of parliament at the forum and is grateful for the excellent cooperation on both meetings. as in the previous year, the meeting in rio provided an excellent framework for enriching exchanges which allowed the gathering of a range of views on the relevant issues. therefore, the commission sees value in upholding the main characteristics of the forum, notably the participation of the various stakeholders and its non-binding nature. i would like also to thank you very much for giving me the opportunity to say something more concrete on the igf (we have adopted this abbreviation) which took place in november in rio de janeiro. firstly i would like to thank very much ms trautmann, mr harbour, mr badia i cutchet and mr hökmark, who represented parliament at this meeting, for their active participation and for the continuation of their excellent cooperation. this allowed us – this year again – together to highlight a number of european priorities such as freedom of speech, bridging the digital divide, security and child protection on-line. it is important for europe to bring such issues to the forefront of the international agenda on a constant basis. the commission supports the igf. this concept, elaborated during the world summit on the information society, is still very valuable, and all the issues which have been mentioned are very much in favour of what we are trying to step up as actions in this particular field. in fact, on a broad range of internet-relevant subjects, the number of participants – around 3 100 – clearly demonstrates that there is indeed interest in having such a place for discussions. the fact that there is no negotiated text as an outcome of the forum should be seen as a strength rather than as a weakness of the process. the forum allows for open exchange, without the pressure of defending a particular outcome in a binding document. this can serve as a basis for improving understanding of each other’s concerns and can pave the way for finding common solutions. dynamic coalitions stemming from the forum are, in fact, one example that the igf can work as a platform where people who share common interests can further cooperate. thus, the rio internet governance forum meeting again provided an excellent framework for enriching exchanges, which allowed a range of views on relevant issues to be gathered. i would also like to thank once again members and hope we will continue our excellent cooperation, because this is one of the most promising areas for our future work.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – madam president, it is important to underline the rapid change that use of the internet and ict has brought about, not only for individual societies, but also for the world. in all sectors of societies, the impact of the internet has changed patterns and opportunities. this is important in order to keep a perspective for the future, because the strength of the internet has been its independent nature, its opportunities and its ability to adapt to different opportunities and demands in societies, based on a number of different actors and operators. it is important because, when we look to the future, we must ensure that it can be used in order to bridge rifts between countries and people, and help to fight poverty and support development – it is of crucial importance for the millennium goals, for example. the emergence of new technology should also be used to ensure that it is easier for more people to use the internet, in more societies. we in the european union have three important things to focus on. first of all, we should ensure we are committed to, and support, the igf process and its independent and non-binding character. that is of deep importance, because it is a good process which is much more dynamic when it is independent and non-binding. secondly, we should contribute to the preparations for the next igf in new delhi, and ensure that we hold discussions with parliamentarians and civil society in order to underline its independence, but also the opportunities. thirdly, we should ensure that the internet is guaranteed freedom and freedom of expression.

madam president, finally, we move from targets to tools! i agree that this can be wisely managed – a win-win situation, where we can create a better environment, better opportunities for our children and more jobs in europe. but it needs coherence in our management of different political areas – research policy, development aid, the common agricultural policy, energy policy and so on – and the first test of this will be at the spring summit with the lisbon agenda. will the lisbon agenda at the council in march reflect the energy and climate package? that will be the first great test. there is also another test. we have only 13 months to bring along the decisions on this very important – maybe the most important – proposal: this mandate for the european parliament, and the commission, and the council. thirteen months to be able to deliver a european standpoint before the european elections and before the extremely important meeting in copenhagen in december 2009. we all know that it is really important, we all know that it is imperative that we get there, and it is up to all of us individually to make it possible – and not impossible – to get a decision in april so that, unlike in the old danish fairy tale, the emperor does not stand naked when he goes to copenhagen! we also have to address energy poverty – it is not there enough – technology neutrality and the forestry issues, which have to be improved. this will be delivered, i think, in the parliamentary procedure in a very transparent and open way, as it is in parliament. finally, from tools to targets: it is our responsibility, and the alde group will stand by you when it comes to a decision and deliver value for voters – finally!
madam president, first of all i would like to congratulate the commission and its president on the presentation and the proposal here and to welcome it. i come from a country where we have proved those goals are achievable – although we do not have very much sun anyway – but it is possible to achieve those goals. and now we are, as has been said today, making the overall goals into concrete actions and measures – that is good. i have three points. first, competitiveness needs sustainability – but sustainability needs competitiveness. if we are to be a competitive economy in the future, we need to have competitive energy sources, and that also goes for the renewables. we need to have competition between different renewables, not a regulated economy with subsidies for different renewables. we need to have fair and open competition, because then we will see the second and third generation of renewables emerge, and we will have good use of them. the second point is that high charges for some countries must not lead to less responsibility for other countries. i must say on that point i feel a little bit uncomfortable because, maybe, some countries are getting away a little bit too easily. we need to underline this because everyone needs to make those efforts. thirdly, this is about renewables, but i would still like to underline the importance of nuclear power, not because i look upon nuclear power as one of the renewables, but it is my belief that, if we are to take full advantage and make full use of renewables, we must not get into a situation where the phasing-out of nuclear power consumes the achievements we can make in the fight against carbon dioxide emissions. we need both of them, and they will support each other. those are my three points today. i welcome the proposal and presentation, and wish us all good luck.

for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. there is a broad consensus in the eu that we must reduce greenhouse gas emissions to stop climate change, but this must not lead to a bureaucratic society without freedom and dynamism. two basic principles apply: we will set emission targets for each country and then leave it to those countries and to the market to meet the targets under free competition and we will internalise the costs of emissions with sufficiently expensive emission allowances. then these will automatically be included in all the billions of economic decisions made every day around the world by households and businesses. so households are choosing to self-power low-energy light bulbs, car manufacturers are making fuel-efficient vehicles, property owners are building passive houses, energy producers are producing low-emission energy. then research and development in this area will pay off. but the eu should not impose prohibitions and detailed regulations. that's why we're going to vote no on the ban on patio heaters. that's why we're going to say no to tax breaks for demolishing houses and no to public funding for energy conservation.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? i thank the shadow rapporteur for the fact that we were able to produce a good report. i also want to thank commissioner borg for finally - finally! - proposing powerful means to tackle the dumping of fish and unwanted by-catches. today we have a fisheries policy that is depleting the world's oceans, leaving behind destroyed bottoms, destroyed ecosystems and depleted seas. even seabirds are being killed by today's fishing policy. if this destruction had happened on land, if we had treated our forests the way we treat our oceans, we would have had riots in the streets, but now the destruction of the oceans is happening in secret, in silence. rachel carson's book our silence became a concept in 1962 and a wake-up call for many to get involved in environmental and nature conservation. now we face a time of calm seas. in fact, last year journalist isabella lövin published a book with that very title. let us now get our lives in order to save the future generation of fishermen. the commission's proposal is in fact a total break with the kind of quota and detailed regulation where fishermen have in practice been encouraged to empty the sea and then throw back dead fish that are not profitable and where the development of gear has been mainly about getting more and more out of the sea. the threat of being forced to fill their vessels with unprofitable fish gives eu fishermen a whip to fish more selectively. but for a successful policy, you also need carrots. for example, we can allow more fishing days for vessels with selective measures or give them access to areas that are otherwise closed to vessels without selective gear. it is important that we set annual by-catch and dumping reduction targets for each type of fishery and that we have a dialogue with the parties involved for the best results. positive examples are the bay of biscay and kattegat and skagerrak. there, french and swedish fishermen have used sorting grates with great success in the fishing for sea trout (nephrop). these have virtually eliminated by-catches. with a little more freedom and responsibility for the fishing fleet, perhaps greater cooperation between scientists and fishermen could lead to a positive development. this is combined with better data on what fish are caught. we need to look at electronic logging systems and any video surveillance if we can find a good solution for preserving personal privacy. another important aspect is what we are going to do with the fish in the form of by-catches that return to ports in the event of a dumping ban. it is important that it can be used in some way, but at the same time that the level of compensation is kept so low that it does not create incentives to hunt by-catch. i hope and believe that the commission will quickly come up with a proposal that can be implemented. this can then be an important part of the fight against overfishing and sustainable fishing. but of course that is not enough. we also need general reductions in fishing effort for endangered species today, but perhaps we will have to debate that another day.
the rapporteur. - thank you, madam president. yes, commissioner, it is clear that there are many reasons for throwing fish overboard, but i still believe that this has been exacerbated by elements of our fisheries policy. let me ask you something. i do not really understand why you are against amendment no 4 when it is going in the direction you yourself are aiming for. today's report states that decisions to prohibit dumping should only be taken after other measures have been tried. the amendment provides that the implementation of a decision to prohibit dumping shall be subject to review in the light of other negative incentives. we are assuming the same logic if amendment no 4 is adopted, that we have a policy of no discarding but that implementation will depend on the conditions of each individual fishery. i do not really understand why you said you were against amendment no 4, but perhaps we can talk more about that later. what makes me optimistic, i suppose, is that we do feel some sort of consensus. i've spoken to scientists and fishermen alike. there is a good deal of consensus here about what needs to be done. the commission and parliament are on the same page, and fishermen and environmental organisations are also on board. it makes me a little optimistic. maybe we can get all the players to work together and we'll get results. the vote will be taken at the next voting time. we can therefore take account of what mr fraga said about voting for the first part and against the second part. thank you and thank you to all who have taken part in the debate and the work. it has been a pleasure working on this report.

in writing. the european parliament voted today in favour of the compromise with the council which provides for the liberalisation of europe's postal market by the end of december 2010 (with the exception of countries with particularly difficult topography, which are given two more years). i have focused my work on the principle of reciprocity, i.e. that there is a mutual agreement that countries which are waiting to liberalise should not be able to enter and compete with those which are beginning to liberalise. i am therefore very pleased to note that my amendment was accepted first by the rapporteur (ferber) and then by the council and that it is now part of the compromise which has now been accepted.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. the resolution contains many good ideas on how to make this objective a reality and that is why i have chosen to vote in favour of the resolution. the objectives set can be summarised in the term 20-20-20, which means that emissions should be reduced by 20%, the use of renewable energy sources should be increased by 20%, and this should be done by the year 2020. i believe, however, that the emphasis should be on reducing emissions and that it should then be up to each member state to decide how this is to be done. the eu should have been able to do without the need to specify how countries should change their use of renewable energy sources. in view of the above, it is regrettable that nuclear energy is not included in the concept of renewable energy. the iaea has stated that nuclear power is a necessary component for reducing carbon dioxide emissions. it is regrettable that this resolution chooses to ignore this by insisting that emissions must not be reduced by nuclear energy. in implementing this resolution it will become clear that the eu is indirectly condemning the role of nuclear energy in improving the climate situation. this is a very strange position, in my view, and it should be noted.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass exceeding 10 tonnes.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'climate change' in the definition of the term 'climate change'. we therefore support the main conclusion that all countries must take comprehensive action to reduce these emissions and that eu countries should take the lead. at the same time, we are opposed to the commission and the president of parliament, who always try to use the great challenges to centralize political power and create an eu without freedom and dynamism. the eu's role in energy policy should be limited to:- setting emission targets for each country and then leaving it to those countries and to the market to achieve the targets under free competition - with sufficiently expensive emission allowances internalize the costs of emissions. (then these are automatically taken into account in all the billions of economic decisions made every day around the world by households and businesses. then households opt for self-powered low-energy light bulbs, car manufacturers make fuel-efficient vehicles, and property owners build passive houses. then r & d in this area pays off.) - allocate money for research and legislate on product labelling eu politicians should not regulate in detail. no one today knows how this gigantic transformation will best take place. that is why we reject such things as bans on patio heaters and tax breaks for demolishing houses. we have voted no, in order to support a rethinking of the eu's energy policy.
the report by mrs fiona hall deals with a very important subject. it is not enough for the eu to set tough rules on co2 emissions. people must be able and willing to change their own consumption and behavioural patterns if real change is to be achieved. as a wise paragraph 61 puts it: the fight must begin at home. for our credibility, therefore, the european parliament must set a good example in its own activities. here we call on eu citizens to do away with everything from water heaters to heated outdoor cafeterias, while the energy consumption of the second seat of the european parliament alone costs 5322 tonnes of co2 emissions per year! a study commissioned by mep caroline lucas points to the dramatic environmental consequences of parliament's monthly visits to france. in total, travel to strasbourg, where parliament holds its votes, is estimated to result in 18 901 tonnes of co2 emissions per year. that's the equivalent of over 10,000 trips to new york from europe. the report went through was good. it would be better to stop going to strasbourg for votes that could just as easily be held at home.

in writing. the fight against terrorism must be conducted only in a manner appropriate to an open, democratic and rule of law society. it is fundamental that threats to the open society are tackled with the methods of the open society. in balancing our efforts and actions, we must safeguard human rights and the rule of law and not fall short of privacy measures. i therefore choose to vote against the recommendation to the council by mr deprezza on the factors contributing to increased support for terrorism and increased recruitment of terrorists.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. reading the commission's communication, and in particular the report of parliament, one might think that the poor little eu is being discriminated against by evil foreign powers. this is particularly evident in paragraph 4 of the report, which says that we call on the commission to ensure that our trade interests are protected against illegal or unfair trade practices by third countries. and then: the eu should act swiftly and firmly when third countries unfairly restrict access to their markets for eu companies. we should be careful with that kind of language towards former colonies. they remember the last time europe acted firmly to defend its interests. that language can do us more harm than any restrictions on trade. the commission has a point, of course, in that there are technical barriers to trade and unfair rules. of course, we will discuss this with our partners calmly and rationally and resolve any problems. in that case, invite not only companies but also ngos, including from third countries. then we may be able to understand the reason behind the rules and to see whether they are reasonable or not. i'm worried about the ten-point list. restrictive export practices on raw materials, caps on foreign ownership in services, barriers to foreign direct investment, public procurement procedures and the use of state aid must be capable of being considered as justified barriers to trade resulting from legitimate legislation and constituting administrative measures of public authorities. they cannot be removed without public consultation and deliberation without threatening the stability of those countries. countries must have the right to protect health and the environment and to have a procurement system that preserves the ability of their local companies not to be completely knocked out of international competition and create mass unemployment. nor can we make unreasonable demands on countries that have little to no resources to protect human rights to protect our intellectual property. we'd rather propose than trap. we will put an end to the rules that favour foreign companies operating in their countries and that act against other foreign players. that's where we can remove the rules. rules which are directly or indirectly designed to protect the public or local businesses must be allowed to slip through our fingers at times. we have protected our industry ourselves until it was strong enough to compete internationally. others must have that opportunity.
creating market access is about eliminating tariffs and non-tariff barriers to trade around the world. it is a hugely important priority for the union's trade policy. it not only creates business opportunities for european companies but also access to better and cheaper products in other parts of the world. it creates wealth around the world. this is also an issue on which i feel we are unusually united in the european parliament. this is a welcome development at a time when protectionism is on the rise globally. that is why i also think that the commission's communication and the report by mr guardano cambó are more welcome than ever. they're really very good. in this discussion i would like to point out another issue that we must not forget - the other side of openness. i think they're connected. european companies also need openness to europe in order to be globally competitive and to take advantage of the market access we can create in other countries. in our globalised world, with more and more companies with global supply chains, tariffs on our own can be as damaging to our successful companies as the very tariffs we are fighting in other countries. i have a concrete proposal for the commissioner, one which may help to solve this problem somewhat. when companies have problems with intra-eu trade, they report them to solvit, which is a great tool to increase mobility in the single market. if the company has problems exporting to a non-eu country, it is reported to the market access database. that's also very important. both these systems then form the basis for negotiations on trade facilitation. hopefully, either the market access database or solvit can be developed to also address problems faced by eu importers. if we do that, we can continue to push both ourselves and the rest of the world in a more free-trade-friendly direction. we can then show that europe is leading the way in opening up markets, both abroad and at home.
member of the commission. − mr president, it is my good fortune to be able to respond happily to two subjects in parliament this evening. i am very pleased that i made it all the way back from kiev in order to do so. so let me start by thanking parliament for the invitation and the opportunity to make this statement on trade defence. i would like to thank parliament for its close interest in this dossier, evidenced by our discussions both in plenary and in the committee on international trade. in 2006, the commission and the council agreed on the need for a review of europe’s trade defence instruments. the intention of this review was to ensure that europe’s vital tools were defending its workers and businesses against unfair trade and were continuing to work as effectively as possible, especially taking into account the dramatic changes in the global economy, where european businesses operate global supply chains, and where the mix of economic interests amongst european businesses is inevitably becoming more and more complex and difficult to judge and to call. following our review and public consultation, and with the backing of the college, i worked with my officials on a set of proposals for adjustments to the eu’s trade defence rules that would offer real improvements in accessibility, transparency and speed and clarity for businesses. they would include, for example, greater access to documents, greater assistance for small businesses using the trade defence system and faster provisional measures. we would also propose to clarify, through the drafting of guidelines, the application of eu rules in two important areas. first, the considerations for determining what degree of production outside of europe disqualifies a company from being treated as european for the purposes of our investigations and trade defence assessments. second, regarding the considerations that should be used in the community interest test, which allows us to determine that trade defence action is indeed in the wider economic interest of the european union – something which, as i say, is becoming increasingly complex and difficult to analyse, where situations are not quite as straightforward and not quite as black and white as they perhaps might appear on the surface, or indeed as was the case in the classic operation of trade defence machinery in the past. the intention in both these areas is largely to codify established practice, which would create clarity and predictability for businesses and decision-making in contentious cases, which we have had experience of in recent years and which, i suggest, are going to increase, not decrease, in number in the coming years. whatever your position on the merits or demerits of anti-dumping, those, i think, are useful things to improve upon and to clarify and to issue guidelines upon, in order to give greater certainty and predictability to those who are appealing for the use of these instruments. a set of proposals along these lines would be balanced. it would reflect a middle ground of the debate among all the interested parties and the member states. there is no point and no advantage in trying to push the centre of gravity, in the design and operation of our trade defence instruments, to one side or the other in the range of opinions in the european union that inhabit the subject of trade defence. it is necessary to devise and to identify a route that takes you, broadly speaking, down the middle. although our attempts have often been portrayed by people as an attempt to go to one side or the other, these impressions can only have been formed by people who cannot have seen our preliminary conclusions and proposals, because there is nothing in such a package that weakens our ability to tackle unfair trade, and nothing which fundamentally alters the basis of the operation of our trade defence instruments. however, it is clear from consultation that this issue is politically sensitive and continues to attract vigorous debate and, i have to say, some disagreement. a package that balances the different concerns in the debate will not currently, in my view, attract the necessary support, and it is better to build on consensus than seek to reconcile presently irreconcilable viewpoints on what clarification and what reform should take place. the political environment in which this subject has been discussed is not easy. some argue that, at a time when we are pressing partners like china to trade fairly, we must not risk even the perception that the eu will lower its guard on trade defence. no such motivation, no such intention is present, but, nonetheless, some have argued that even risking the perception would be unwise at this moment. furthermore, the rules negotiations in the doha round, in particular the chairs’ text, have also put the international tdi environment into considerable flux, because of the text’s surprising and, frankly, unacceptable content. i do not think there has been a chairs’ text in the history of chairs’ texts in the doha round that has attracted less support from right across the membership of the wto than the current chairs’ text on rules. but, above all, our objective has been to strengthen unity on the operation of trade defence instruments in order to make them more workable, and it is not my job as commissioner to weaken that unity. the reality is that, as of today, this unity that i would like to see is not sufficiently in evidence amongst our member states. that does not mean one side of this debate is right and that the other is wrong, just that both sides need to continue working towards a greater consensus and, frankly, a greater sense of solidarity in the operation of these instruments. that is what i propose to encourage. we will continue to consult on the ideas that we have floated and to build agreement by drawing on our experience. i would finish by making this observation. the pressures that global economic change is exerting on our trade defence system are here to stay. they are not going to go away; they are actually going to grow. the pressures are going to become greater. our ability to operate the traditional consensus and solidarity is going to become harder, not easier, which makes it all the more important that the role of the commission grows in navigating our way through the different and competing interests and views that businesses and member states have on this subject. i believe that future cases will demonstrate the need for clarity and will demonstrate the need for the sort of guidelines that we have originally drafted. the questions with which we started this review will still be present in six months, in a year and in two years, and i firmly believe that, in due course, they are going to have to be addressed. our challenge is together to find economically and politically credible answers, and the commission will continue to work with that in mind.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. thank you peter mandelson. when you were here last you spoke of the need for a reform of the trade defence instruments. you spoke passionately about how globalisation and global supply chains have changed the conditions for trade defence instruments and that they therefore needed to be updated to remain effective and take account of the community interest. everything you said back then is still true. we need a reform of the instruments. since then, however, reform has proved very difficult to implement. conflicts between european companies and between member states are high. but that does not diminish the need for reform. the fact that everything is so infected and that in case after case you see the same lines of conflict following the same predictable tracks, shows in my opinion that we need change. we need a new unity around the instrument and a consensus that unites. now the reform is postponed and seems rather distant. i'm sure more people than me are sorry. but it is no use arguing about whose fault it was, whether it is the fault of the member states or the commission and which member states have put the brakes on the wheel. the blame game is not good for anyone. because even though the bill is currently stalled, the ideas cannot be buried. we must not forget the question. we must continue to discuss and to establish what we actually agree on. i think there's a lot. it's about transparency and openness. clear rules on how we apply the community interest are also likely to apply. perhaps even how we modernise the definition of community industry. but that requires that we keep the debate alive and continue to debate and compromise. i hope we will do this in the european parliament. i also hope that the member states and the commission will keep the debate alive.
for the verts/ale group. i thank the commissioner. debating with you is always fun! i am also always reinforced in my view that fair trade is better than free trade and, of course, that trade in solidarity with a weaker party is more important than trade that is formally fair and reciprocal. but now for the tdi, the trade and development index. by the way, maybe we should study each other's bookshelves and maybe figure out the background of a cup of rooibos tea sometime? if nothing else, you can come with me to the dump where i became politically active and meet my street children in brazil. back to the tdi. what is a european company? it's hard to define now, and therefore it might be hard to use it. which interest is a genuine community interest. the consumers? the voluntary organisations? small businesses? the big companies? it is not at all easy to define, which means that it is difficult to use the instrument today, except in certain obvious cases. there is a dimension to this which is completely missing and which i think would be very interesting if you could come back to when you come up with new proposals. could not ecological and social dumping be equated with direct dumping? what is the difference between a company being spared a billion euros worth of environmental and social regulations, or receiving a billion euros in state subsidies? it would be interesting if you could bring up a discussion with the wto and see if we can get any further on that issue.
thank you, mr president. i would also like to thank the rapporteurs very much for a very good job. it has been very exciting to follow them along the way. although i am in favour of more intergovernmental cooperation, i realise that there are times when we in the union must have as much equality of laws as possible. the legislative package on the marketing of products in the internal market is one such package, because what we produce in the eu and import into the eu flows freely in the internal market. for me, the safety of consumers in the market is of the utmost importance. i therefore welcome the proposals of the rapporteurs in the legislative package, which in some respects sharpens the commission's proposal. for example, my colleague mr schaldemose wants importers to be responsible for ensuring that the products they import comply with eu rules. i think this is particularly important now that we are also going to review the toy directive in view of the problems which have been present on the toy market recently. i think it is obvious that importers should be able to be held accountable and risk punishment if they import dangerous products. who else would it be? we must also change and strengthen the ce marking. although this is the most sensitive issue, i think the rapporteurs have made good progress. as my colleague mr schaldemose said, ce marking is not the solution to all our problems of internal market security. however, many consumers today believe that the ce mark means that products are safe, not least when it comes to toys and gadgets. we saw my colleague mccarthy demonstrate here with a coffee pot and a toy. it is our responsibility to ensure that the labelling is strengthened and that market surveillance really works in all member states. finally, i would like to emphasize once again that our consumers must come first, because without safe and secure consumers we cannot have a thriving market.

in writing. - the need to combat terrorism across borders with common means; however, the fight against terrorism must always be fought by legal and proportionate means. the cia's flights in europe, the use of torture as a form of execution and the drowning of prisoners, which the cia now admits to having done, and the establishment of special secret prisons must be strongly condemned. the eu should have acted much more forcefully than it has. but one thing is clear to me: our values should be the basis of our common legislation. we must also ensure that common legislation does not disturb or crowd out important constitutions, including freedom of expression. the report calls for the incorporation of a new definition in the framework decision, 'the prosecution of terrorism'. i think that would be unfortunate. not because there is no reason to ensure that all member states have good laws against incitement, but because it is difficult - if not impossible - to find a definition which would apply uniformly and which would not give rise to difficult problems of interpretation. on the one hand, there is the important task of finding ways to combat terrorism and save lives. on the other hand, there is the principle of freedom of expression and the ambition to maintain a high level of legal certainty in europe. it's about finding a reasonable balance.

mr president, i would like to thank you. it is important that the eu grows, that growth is strong and that jobs are created. this creates the conditions for our union to become more legitimate. in recent years, the eu has also done better than most. the eu is also doing better than, say, the united states. we know that the single market and the euro have created completely different conditions for a growing europe. there is still a lot to do, as many have said. let me give you a concrete example. it may be a little chauvinistic, but still. in my home country, the swedish government has embarked on a growth policy which has created up to 100 000 new jobs, including through targeted measures such as reducing taxes and employer contributions. one proposal was just to grant reduced employer's contributions to service companies in a number of sectors which are not competitive on the international market. the hope was to create 17 000 new jobs in this way. the commission has now effectively put a stop to this by requiring a limitation in the proposal which makes it so cumbersome that the swedish government will probably have to withdraw the proposal in its entirety. i find it difficult to understand the commission's actions for two reasons. in the shadow of a looming global recession, most governments are of course responding with various types of stimulus packages to safeguard employment and purchasing power. this proposal was intended to give a much-needed boost to the unusually underdeveloped swedish service sector, which had hopefully had a good stabilising effect on our economy as a whole. i think this is very much contrary to the general spirit of the lisbon strategy. if we are to be this competitive economy, the most competitive economy in the world by 2010, then we must also dare to try new ways. i would like to ask you directly, commissioners, why are you preventing new jobs from being created in this way?
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner verheugen, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the lisbon strategy is now emerging from a sluggish initial phase and is beginning to generate the optimism and will that we need. the authors have managed to keep their focus on the important things and not fall victim to the parliamentary mania of drawing everything in. there are different ways of meeting the challenges of globalisation. some people stick their heads in the sand like ostriches and think everything's gonna be all right. others are wiser and follow the lead of the authors of the report, seeing opportunities and improving their own abilities. the immediate and most important thing now is to at least ensure that the member states live up to their own commitments. that is, to monitor the internal market, to ensure that member states implement and enforce decisions taken, to ensure that the rules are justified, not too narrow and not too expensive for smes. we should also stick to key guidelines, such as simplification, benchmarking, comparisons and competition. this will require increased cooperation between authorities at local and regional level as well. this is good for citizens, it is good for businesses and it contributes to integration. the systematic monitoring of free movement is necessary if we are to make the labour market flourish. measuring performance is a step on the way forward. the eu gains its legitimacy by delivering, not least, quality of life for its citizens. this requires a stimulating business climate, to which the lisbon strategy can contribute if it is implemented in a conscious way. the commission is responsible for putting pressure on the member states. finally, i am convinced that the commission will find a reasonable solution to the swedish problem of employers' contributions.

in writing. the european parliament has already adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. we are in favour of the new treaty. however, we recognize that the fact that two member states rejected the draft constitution meant that we had to make changes. we therefore do not share the view that it is regrettable that the flag and the anthem were removed from the treaty. unlike the majority in this parliament, we believe that it is good that 'emergency brakes' are introduced in decision-making in areas of national sovereignty. we are supporters of the principle of subsidiarity and therefore reject the european parliament's demands for referendums in the member states. this is a matter for each member state to decide. finally, we would like to comment on the vote on amendment no 36 concerning the laval case. we voted against the first part, because we believe that there are differences between the old treaty and the lisbon treaty, for example in the charter of rights, which among other things establishes the right to strike in accordance with national practice. we support the wording of the bill of rights, and we also support the second part of the amendment which protects the right to conflict. we would like to point out that this view is shared by our political group in this parliament, which voted against, because the report is about the treaty and not the laval case.
