in writing. - as in the past on the new eu treaties, i chose to depart from my group and voted against the report on the lisbon treaty. there are certainly a number of improvements in the proposal, but i am against the union's current direction towards greater supranationality. i want european cooperation at the intergovernmental level. i strongly support the idea of international cooperation to solve common problems. there are important tasks which we must carry out jointly, not least in the field of the environment, human trafficking and social dumping. but i think that the proposal strengthens the primacy of eu law over national law, that it takes more power away from the member states and that it gives more power to the larger countries than to the smaller ones. this is something i'm against. i voted against the amendment which called for the eu to decide on a union-wide referendum because i believe that it is up to each member state to decide this for itself.
in writing. − the lisbon treaty is a crucial step towards ensuring a more efficient and well-functioning european union. i therefore welcome the fact that we in the european parliament voted jointly on the report on the lisbon treaty. it means a clearer decision-making structure and more power for the eu's only elected assembly, the european parliament, a strengthened common foreign policy and new posts, such as the high representative for foreign affairs and the president of the european council. the treaty is a profound change and i therefore supported the proposal for a pan-european referendum, an interesting alternative to a swedish referendum which i believe is necessary to give citizens the opportunity to express their views on matters of importance to them.
in writing. - the constitution should be rejected. we need a new constitution, not just a new name. a fundamental rewrite is needed, which, like a constitution in the usual sense, limits and clarifies the power of the court of justice of the european union and the role of the eu. it is important to define what issues are of a transnational nature, i.e. issues such as free trade, climate change, the fight against international crime, terrorism and integration. it is noteworthy that in all these areas england has been driving the development of the eu. in my view, sweden should demand the same exemption as england has received, both its opt-in and opt-out clauses. there is no reason to treat sweden differently. the citizens of sweden have voted no to the euro, and it would be reasonable for the next treaty to make it clear that sweden is not obliged to participate in monetary cooperation. none of this is in the present proposal, which is why i have voted against the report.
in writing. - this report recommends that the respective national population and housing censuses be harmonised to a level that is easily absurd. we believe that it is up to the member states to decide whether they wish to register everything from the generational composition of the private household to the distance between the permanent residence and the green and recreational areas. we believe that both the council regulation and the parliament's report deal with proposals which are too detailed and which infringe on privacy. we have therefore voted against both the amended parliament's proposal and the final vote.
in writing. - we have voted today on the report by mrs juknevičienė on the population and housing censuses. we chose to vote no in the final vote. the member states are capable of dealing with census and housing issues themselves, and this is an area where the eu does not need to intervene.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. parliament adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. the proposal is motivated by good intentions to make it easier to compare population and housing census statistics. however, the final results are extremely far-reaching in the information sought. the commission proposed that each member state collect data on its citizens, such as sexual orientation, the date of women's first marriage and current marriage, ethnicity, religion and the number of live births. the fact that our commission is presenting a proposal which contains such privacy-infringing issues and which in practice involves the registration of citizens is highly significant. parliament clarified some of the most far-reaching issues today. i have voted against the motion as a whole, however, because it still goes too far.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the eu to be more involved in the process of european integration. this is also reflected in the final result. the report does, however, contain a lot of important and necessary points, which i was happy to support. this applies in particular to the more offensive elements which show that parliament is keeping up with the times, such as environmental issues and the link with growth. the debate on flexicurity, which has now gathered momentum, also puts the finger on the important - perhaps the most important - question of how competitiveness and security can be combined. as the report states, we are not building the future on protectionism and bureaucracy but on openness, transparency and good conditions for workers and businesses alike. however, i would like to make it clear that my vote in favour of amendment no 12 is in no way to be interpreted as supporting the establishment of minimum wages in the eu. it was, however, a clarification that collective agreements have a place in the european model. i also made this clear today in amendment no 32 to the report on the integrated guidelines for growth and employment, which dealt specifically with this issue.
in writing. - the lisbon strategy has now been in operation since 2000, and the member states have not yet seen any clear success. the current proposal sets out a new plan which will run until 2013. i believe in institutional competition. there are ambitions in the lisbon strategy that are good and worth pursuing, such as the proposals for increased investment in research and development. the parts dealing with 'social europe' do not belong at eu level and should be left to the individual member states to decide. the resolution also contains a number of vague texts which allow the commission to interpret and justify measures which we have not asked for. my basic view is that the countries themselves should design the growth strategy that they democratically choose. some take a social democratic line, others a very liberal and market-oriented line. we have to respect both. the main thing is that the decision should be taken at national level. i have therefore decided to reject the proposal in its entirety.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the context of the directive. however, we voted against amendment no 32 by the green group on minimum wages as part of the european model. we understand that the issue of minimum wages is important in many of the eu member states. however, we believe that each member state should decide how to deal with this issue. in sweden, we solve it best through collective agreements negotiated by the social partners.
in writing. − this own-initiative report addresses several challenges facing the future of europe, such as increased globalisation, an ageing population and climate change. several of the proposed measures appear to be reasonable and could be a possible path for a member state to take. the junilist is against complicated tax systems, social exclusion and environmental destruction. however, it is up to each member state to decide how it wants to shape its policies to meet the challenges ahead. the most successful solutions emerge through institutional competition, examples which can inspire and stimulate voluntary action in other member states. we have voted against this report because it deals with areas which are the political responsibility of the member states.
in writing. - today i voted in favour of a report setting out the integrated guidelines for growth and jobs 20082010. on the one hand, i was pleased to find amendments from the socialist group which stated the need to create a business-friendly europe, especially for small businesses. on the other hand, the same group also proposes much stricter regulations in the financial field, rules and conditions which are supposed to protect the consumer but which, in too large doses, lead to a reduction in the consumer's returns. that attitude is likely to be more of a hindrance than a help, and i have therefore consistently voted against proposals of that kind. if europe's vision of being a world leader in competitiveness is ever to become a reality, much more aggressive efforts will also have to be made to modernise the labour market, to promote research and training and to exploit the opportunities offered by a 'greener' economy. that is why i voted with the greens in favour of environmental taxes at eu level, even though i defended the independence of the european central bank against their recurrent attacks. if the recent financial turbulence has shown anything, it is the benefit of a strong central bank that can withstand short-term solutions, not to mention the stabilizing effect of participating in a large currency area.
for the pse group. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur who has done a very good groundwork but has also managed the negotiations between the various political groups in an excellent way. this is, as someone said earlier, a great challenge. not a threat, but a challenge to the european union. i shall try to stick to the three main areas which were and are the structure of the rapporteur's report. first, the number of births. we have a low birth rate in europe, and i share the view that this is largely due to a lack of political decision-making. it's about men and women today wanting to be, and needed to be, in the workforce, but at the same time wanting to be parents and have children. we must encourage this combination in the member states so that parents, both men and women, can combine work and parenting. it is about parental leave benefits based on the principle of income loss, linked to working life, at a high level so that you can be at home and not have to suffer financially. secondly, we need a high-quality, extended childcare. we've got a long way to go. we have targets, but few member states are currently meeting the targets for the expansion of childcare. then there are the older people in the labour market. it's a paradox that we enter the workforce later and leave it earlier. we must create the conditions for various measures in the field of working conditions, in terms of the possibility of continuing training and in terms of flexible solutions at the border between work and retirement, to enable parents to remain in the working world. finally, migration: we need people from other parts of the world in our communities to develop and maintain our welfare communities. we must therefore create an integration policy where they are integrated into our societies and not live in exclusion. we must help with this policy, because it is not contrary to our welfare states. on the contrary, it is a prerequisite for their development.
for the gue/ngl group. good afternoon. one of the main issues when we discuss scientific cooperation with africa is how african countries can defend themselves against climate change. as the un panel on climate change has noted, africa may be the continent most vulnerable to climate change. this is actually happening right here and now. crops are burned. the desert is expanding. lakes and streams are drying up, and climate refugees now number in the millions on the african continent. many of the conflicts that exist in africa today are the result of the stress and strain that climate change brings. one of the great ironies of the climate problem is that those who pollute the least are the ones who suffer the most. ethiopia, for example, emits 0.1 tons of carbon dioxide per capita per year, while my own country, sweden, emits over 6 tons per capita per year. the eu average is about 10 tonnes. so sweden is emitting 60 times more, and the eu is emitting 100 times more than ethiopia, just to take a concrete example. from this point of view, we in europe have a huge responsibility. on the one hand we must drastically reduce our own emissions, and on the other we must do everything we can to help developing countries take action against climate change. tonight we are debating how to have a well-functioning scientific cooperation with africa. i think we should ask ourselves, self-critically, here and now: are we really doing all we can to support the african countries? are we really doing everything we can to transfer, for example, modern and environmentally friendly technology and know-how to african countries, and when we negotiate trade agreements, are we taking account first of all of our own companies or of african companies who want to trade with us on fair terms? unfortunately, at least i cannot give a positive answer to these questions. much remains to be done. what, for example, about our intellectual property and patent laws? in november, in our resolution before the bali negotiations, the european parliament stated that 'the european parliament recognises that licensing fees for intellectual property rights in clean technology areas can be an obstacle to the transfer of such technology to developing countries'. let me give you an example. 75 percent of the wind power technology in the world is controlled by four companies. yeah, only four companies in the whole world. three of these companies are european. the fourth is from the united states. now, when many developing countries want to expand wind power, they have to use old and unpatented technology. they simply cannot afford the most modern and environmentally friendly technology. much of modern technology is simply locked into our own legislation. this, of course, benefits a few large companies that control the patents. but what good is this to angola, botswana or rwanda? i want a clear answer from the commission tonight. are you doing anything to make the laws on intellectual property rights and patents more flexible? what are you doing to make technology more easily transferable? finally, another example of these shortcomings is the so-called flexible mechanisms. we in the developed world can shift some of our climate responsibility onto the developing world. the aim, if you take it lightly, is to transfer technology and environmental activities to developing countries. but according to a new report we received last year from the environment committee, less than 2% of the cdm projects go to african countries. the majority of it has gone to projects that have not really been very useful at all. what is the commission's view of the flexible mechanisms and the cdm projects, the clean development mechanism? are you prepared to fundamentally review these mechanisms, as we in the european parliament have called for? how can africa benefit more from technology transfer in the future?
in writing. − we do not agree with the mutual recognition principle as a guarantor for advanced consumer, social or environmental rights. the principle says that if the product is accepted somewhere in europe, it cannot be denied access to another member state unless the authorities can provide evidence that the application of a national technical rule is justified. however, the criteria for exceptions to the principle are already established by eu court rulings. and these apply to all member states, regardless of whether or not we adopt the goods package. in addition to that, consumer rights at eu level are strengthened with the goods package. furthermore, we are against any measures taken in the goods package that may give political support to a future harmonisation of criminal law at eu level. having taking all this into consideration, we have decided to vote in favour of the reports.

in writing. − junilista supports the un human rights council and the important work of this body. however, we oppose the wording of article 34 which insists that each eu member state state state the eu's position on these issues. each eu member state is an independent member of the un and therefore has the right to express its own opinion. we have therefore voted against the resolution.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the commission to be given the task of drawing up a report on the future of the european union. however, we consider that the report is too broad and addresses aspects that go beyond what is relevant to demographic trends. we are also reacting to various proposals for tax relief for companies that provide day care and for people to be able to work from home. we have, however, voted in favour of the report because it also deals with the essential aspects of the challenge facing europe.
in writing. - many parts of europe are facing demographic challenges. however, these should be solved by national measures, not by quadrilateral eu proposals which may be appropriate in individual countries but may be inappropriate in other member states. several eu countries, including sweden, have also largely overcome low birth rates and demographic problems, including through well-developed welfare policies and immigration. the member states who so wish can benefit from studying sweden and the other nordic countries and following the measures taken by them.
in writing. - the demographic challenge facing europe is enormous. as a theme, it is certainly a relevant discussion to have at european level - i myself did so as rapporteur for an opinion on flexicurity. however, a basic prerequisite is that the discussion should take its cue from the fact that most of the measures proposed, such as a delay in the retirement age, a wise family policy, good conditions for parental leave, etc., fall within the scope of the subsidiarity principle. a number of proposals in the broad range of solutions mentioned in the report are being put into practice and have worked well in sweden. however, this does not mean that they would automatically fare as well in other parts of europe. the fundamental problem with the castex report is therefore not its intention, but that most of what is discussed is policy which should be pursued by the member states - sometimes at municipal level. that is why i voted in favour of amendments which stressed the principle of subsidiarity and abstained from the final vote.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the consumer is vulnerable on the internet. when you want to use popular services, you cannot select "you may not record data about me". nor can you find out what happens to your data. you do a search, you buy something. when one company can combine all this information, it can have a huge market advantage and a huge amount of information about everyone who uses the internet. the same is true if you want to download a movie legally on the internet. then you have to use a corporate software, namely windows. we can't protect ourselves from these big companies unless the legislature helps us. data protection, how light-hearted is that when, for example, the fight against copyright infringement must also be included in this? a filesharing company gets their computer searched and all their private information searched. this information is then sent to the media companies to find out what was copyrighted and what was not. when media companies get access to police investigative material, how can you protect consumers? it is time to step up data protection drastically.
member of the commission. − mr president, let me start by extending my gratitude to the committee on fisheries and in particular the rapporteur, mr capoulas santos, for his report. on 23 may 2007, the european community and the republic of guinea-bissau initialled a fisheries partnership agreement for a period of four years. this agreement forms part of the series of ec ‘tuna agreements’ in the atlantic ocean. the financial contribution is fixed at eur 7 million per year. of this financial contribution, 35%, or eur 2.45 million, is earmarked to support the definition and implementation of a fisheries sector policy in guinea-bissau, with a view to introducing responsible and sustainable fishing. in addition, the european union will pay an annual specific contribution of eur 500 000 to improve sanitary conditions in the fisheries sector and to strengthen monitoring, control and surveillance in the waters of guinea-bissau. in addition to the total amount of eur 7.5 million, the protocol also contains a special financial provision for a supplementary allocation of eur 1 million maximum per year, aiming at improving the use of fishing opportunities by ship owners. this new protocol provides fishing possibilities for shrimps and for fish, in particular cephalopods, up to a total tonnage of 4 400 t per year for each of the two categories. furthermore, the agreement covers licences for 19 tuna seiners, 14 pole-and-line vessels and 4 surface longliners. the agreement gives the community a solid legal and political framework for monitoring the activities of the european union fleet in the guinea-bissau fishing area. it also allows the community to support the development of the sector in that country, to the mutual benefit of both parties. turning to the amendments proposed, let me state that, whereas in many instances we share the objectives outlined therein, i am unable to accept them for the following reasons. i fully share the concern that the flow of information provided to the european parliament should be as complete as possible. however, the commission already provides all the requested information, in line with the current interinstitutional arrangements. moreover, every evaluation report, as well as the reports on the results of negotiations and of joint committee meetings, is immediately transmitted to the committee on fisheries. in addition, i myself inform the committee on fisheries regularly of the state of play of international negotiations in special, restricted ‘closed-door’ sessions. the issue of reporting requirements by the fleet is also being dealt with, since we have proposed a legal basis that will allow the commission to refuse licence requests on the grounds of poor reporting from member states. this draft regulation is currently going through the adoption procedure in the institutions. regarding the proposal to reject the fisheries agreements in general on the grounds that they are not sustainable, it should be recalled that the council, in its july 2004 conclusions, indicated that the aim of the new fisheries partnership agreements (fpas) is to contribute to the sustainable management of fisheries resources. as a matter of fact, fpas only provide for access to stocks that are not fully exploited by the national fleet of the particular country in question. the commission believes that the fpas can and do provide a political and legal framework that assists the efforts of partner countries in implementing a policy of sustainable and responsible fishing in their waters. finally, with respect to the proposal that vessels should pay the total cost of the agreement in proportion to their catches, let me just say that we have established a reasonable cost-sharing balance between shipowner contributions and the community compensation. this approach is in line with the july 2004 council conclusions on fisheries partnership agreements, on which parliament gave its opinion. in our system, the shipowner licence fees are indeed calculated on the basis of their respective catches.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the fisheries agreements are only intended to safeguard the short-term economic interests of another of the eu's industries that is not up to par internationally. there are three reasons why we must vote against the fisheries agreements tomorrow. the first reason is the environment. the depletion of west african waters is soon a fact. biodiversity is under serious threat, and the continuation of this insane fisheries policy will have long-term effects on the environment both locally and globally. my second objection is justified on economic grounds. european taxpayers are forced to subsidize the unprofitable fishing fleets by millions of euros every year. where is the economic benefit? where's the sustainability? where is the european added value? if, instead, vessels had to pay their own costs, fishing pressure would be reduced, and both taxpayers and the environment would be the big winners. the third reason is the importance of fisheries for local development in the partner countries. fishing is the only source of income for many. the vacuuming of the seas creates an unsustainable situation for the local population, which cannot compete with the heavily subsidised european vessels. it is time to end these fisheries agreements immediately.

in writing. the european parliament has voted in favour of the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the transport sector. the reason is that the experience of this type of agreement is highly doubtful. low-income countries are offered a lump sum of money and in return their fishing waters are opened to commercial fishing from eu member states, primarily countries in southern europe. the resources transferred are ridiculously small in relation to the fish caught; moreover, the risk of unsustainable fishing is obvious. another argument is that local coastal fisheries are usually adversely affected. we hope that it will be possible to evaluate these fisheries agreements as soon as possible and replace them with sustainable systems within the framework of the eu sustainable development strategy (sds).

the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. - madam president! - what is it? biogas can play an important role in the fight against climate change, not only for producing heat and electricity but also for powering vehicles. biogas can be produced from, for example, manure and household waste. today, there is a great potential for a significant increase in the production of biogas. we in the committee on the environment want to support this possibility and to see much more biogas in the future. however, increasing biogas production must not become an end in itself. as regards waste, the main priority must still be to reduce the total amount of waste. if we're going to reduce waste, we have to -- which is like swearing in church -- reduce our consumption. i would like to take the opportunity to ask a few questions to commissioner mariann fischer boel, who is with us here tonight: does the commission have a strategy to reduce the ever-growing waste mountain in the eu? does the commission also have a strategy for reducing the consumption of a large number of goods which we do not really need? we in the environment committee also want to see a certification for sustainable biogas. such a certification system must cover the whole chain, i.e. from the field to the car. social and environmental factors must be the basis for such certification. i welcome the commission's proposal for a new directive on renewable fuels, but more needs to be done to promote the role of biogas. i have one more question for mariann fischer boel: what do you think of such a certification? this and other environmental issues underline the importance of public accountability. most of us in this room agree that we want to strengthen the role of biogas. more cars and trucks could roll up with biogas in their tanks. biogas reduces greenhouse gas emissions from cars by as much as 90 percent. in addition, particulate emissions are reduced, and cars run more quietly with gas in the tank. however, if we are serious about our environmental efforts, we cannot take responsibility for the market. for example, if more cars are to run on biogas, there must be gas stations where gas can be refuelled. the state must ensure that the mackerel take responsibility through legislation and temporary grants. in my home country, sweden, the last northernmost point is in uppsala, which is in the middle of the country. this means that we have over 100 miles of road without the possibility for forward-thinking motorists to refuel with biogas. of course, that's not sustainable. we all have a responsibility to promote biogas: the eu by setting guidelines, the member states to stimulate national production and, not least, the municipalities which should build more biogas plants.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the united nations climate panel says that humans are emitting greenhouse gases on a scale that is changing the world's climate. the eu has accepted that humanity must therefore reduce these emissions dramatically over the next few decades and has said that the eu should be at the forefront of this global change. so what are we gonna do? it is with horror that i note how the political clout and the ambition to transfer political power to the eu institutions now threaten to be unleashed. we in the european parliament should not demand subsidies for investments in biogas. we have no idea whether biogas should be taxed at the expense of other alternatives. we will have emission targets set in each eu country, as we have done, and then let the countries themselves seek effective ways of achieving these targets. the classic european solution is institutional competition not central control.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. first of all, i would like to thank bart staes for a good report, which we in the gue/ngl of course support. one of the objectives of this report is to reduce the use of pesticides by improving statistics, which is an urgent task. the use of poisons and chemicals in agriculture is increasing in europe. the toxins remain in the food we and our children eat. it happens to all of us. this is something that should concern all who care about their health and our environment. in my country, the environmental movement has a long-term vision of chemical-free agriculture. unfortunately, that goal seems far away today. not only in the eu but also in sweden, the use of chemicals in agriculture is increasing. i have a specific question for commissioner joaquín almunia: if a country wants to ban a chemical in agriculture, shouldn't it be allowed to do so? unfortunately, there are other examples of member states which have wanted to prevent the use of certain pesticides. however, your commission has prevented them from doing so, why? should not environmental and public health considerations take precedence over the need for total harmonization?
mr president, i would like to thank you. the description we have heard from the commission and the council today is a very different reality from that in which the majority of citizens live, a reality which shows that poverty and the gaps between different groups have increased. more people have been employed, we are told, but there is no mention of the type of jobs that have increased. these are usually low-paying jobs that are impossible to support themselves and any children. it's a job with precarious and insecure terms of employment. that is not the model of society that we on the left want or accept. we know that there are other ways to go, ways towards solidarity and justice and a society for all citizens. any idea of a social europe was shattered by the ecj's vaxholm and viking line judgments. the judgments clearly stated that within the eu, the requirements and interests of free movement and the single market take precedence over the interests of employees. the effect of the judgments is social dumping. workers from different countries are pitted against each other. serious entrepreneurs who want to pay decent wages and offer reasonable working conditions are being pushed out. the court leaves no room for doubt. but why are the commission and the council passive on this issue? why approve a treaty that further strengthens the interests of the market against those of the wage-earners? the vaxholm judgment gave three unequivocal answers, namely, first, that member states may not decide on the labour market, and, second, that employees may not take military action which disturbs the internal market; the court thus deprives employees of the only real defence of the principle of equal pay for equal work. thirdly, companies that set up in eu countries with lower wage levels will be allowed to send staff to other countries to work for those lower wages. trade unions, political organisations, ngos and millions of citizens have realised that a people's europe is possible, but when will the commission and the council realise the same?
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is difficult to take responsibility for the future of europe. we will survive globalisation and the competition it brings. we're going to tackle the tough demographic issues of a retirement boom and a shrinking population by 2020, the same year we're going to meet the environmental and climate goals that we've set, the 20-20-20 goals. we must ensure that we have economically sustainable development in europe. i am glad that when we deal with questions of the lisbon strategy, we are dealing with a unified approach to sustainable economic development, economic growth, environmental responsibility and socially responsible policies. we see that concerted efforts bring results. it's fun. we also see that a concerted strategy is of the utmost importance. the cooperation that has been established between the european parliament and the national parliaments is of the utmost importance. i would like to point out the new area included in this year's resolution, namely transport policy, which has a lot to answer for in terms of climate objectives and also growth. we are in a situation where we can create more jobs in europe through an efficient, sustainable and environmentally friendly transport policy. the fact is that the different policy areas are interrelated. it is impossible to express a view on the 20-20-20 climate policy objectives without also having it reflected in the council resolution on the lisbon strategy. i therefore hope that when we see the outcome of the spring summit we can have a comprehensive policy that can cope with both economic growth and climate policy. that, gentlemen, is a challenge!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am pleased, as is the commission, that there have been more jobs and more productivity. however, as many have pointed out, the coin has a reverse. many of the new jobs are precarious and unviable. the opportunities for education are extremely unequally distributed. some regions are not keeping pace. this is why we need a social europe that provides secure jobs with decent wages, that provides equal educational opportunities for all and that also ensures that development in all regions is good and equitable. we've had a debate for a long time about flexicurity. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. it is challenged by saying that freedom of movement is superior to the right to strike. it is also challenged by saying that the principle of equal pay for equal work should not apply. it opens up social dumping, but not only that, it also means that companies that pay decent wages under contract have a hard time competing. this could lead to protectionism. i share the commission's view that we do not need protectionism. it is therefore important that the commission clarifies that equal pay for equal work should apply and that we should have good social conditions throughout europe, so that the currents of protectionism do not spread across the eu.
mr president, is it not a little odd that when we are discussing how to make europe more competitive in order to get more jobs that the discussion is leaning more towards the belief that jobs can be created through new regulations? that is not the case. i should like to disclose a secret to our socialist friends here today: in order for there to be employees, there needs to be employers – there are no employees without employers. their main problem is that, they are so eager to believe that jobs can be created through regulations, they forget that the preconditions and opportunities for new enterprises and new jobs need to be created, because there is no bigger social failure than unemployment and there is no bigger social success than every new job we can achieve in europe and in our member states. i would like to underline what the president said about achieving results through the lisbon agenda. let us not forget that the world is moving faster and we need to be more competitive; we need to be more entrepreneurial. there are three things i would like him to consider at the european council and later on. firstly, concerning red tape and better regulation: i would like him to report annually on the achievements of reducing red tape in order to show that he will reach the goal by 2012 of 25% less red tape than exists today. i would like him to also ensure that the services directive is implemented fully in each member state as soon as possible. i would like him to ensure that we are facilitating new businesses through a more offensive and more active broadband policy. i think our success in making europe more competitive lies in making small and medium-sized enterprises able to act all over the internal market. that is a job we need to do and he should take the first steps, together with the slovenian presidency, at the end of this week.

in writing. - the current cap health check has given the commission and the european parliament an opportunity to take an ambitious and forceful approach to the further development of the cap. it is crucial that the reform of the cap continues in a market-oriented direction, which means that consumer choice is at the heart of farmers' production decisions, while reducing the overall cost of the agricultural policy. with the current strong growth in global demand for agricultural products, there are very good conditions for accelerating the deregulation of agricultural policy. the moderate delegation regrets that neither the commission nor the european parliament took the opportunity to do so, and we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. − in sweden, both the previous and the current government have positively described the upcoming 'health check' of the eu's long-term budget and the cap in the coming years and how it will lead to very big changes and reforms. the federalist majority in the european parliament now considers that the heads of state or government agreed in 2002 to maintain agricultural subsidies under the first pillar at the same level until 2013 and that this agreement should be accepted. how can the 2002 agreement be interpreted in such different ways? the christian-democratic/conservative and social democratic majorities in the european parliament believe that reforms should be implemented only after 2013. the social democrats and the swedish alliance of liberals and democrats have told swedish voters that reforms should be introduced on the basis of the 'health check' for 2008-2009 directly in the long-term budget. which side is right about the 2002 agreement? the junilista group is absolutely of the opinion that reforms of the cap and a reduction in its costs in the eu budget should take place immediately after the completion of the health check. all other solutions would be to deceive the voters of the six member states that in 2005 did not want to expand the eu's long-term budget.
in writing. - there are two things that an eu friend has to explain (if not defend) when he comes home to talk about his work in the eu. one is the parliament's travelling circus to strasbourg. the second is agricultural aid. in both cases, the present system is equally impossible to defend and to explain. there are no good reasons for us to go to strasbourg. nor is there any reason for the eu taxpayers to be fattening the british royal family, which is today one of the main beneficiaries of the system while african producers are being knocked out. the goepel report went in the wrong direction, and that is why i voted against it. i did so mainly because i believe that the direction of the will is wrong: more direct aid, less reform, less use of the market. the commission has made good progress in moving towards a more modern system of agricultural aid. it is sad that parliament seems to want to turn back the clock.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the conflict in chad cannot be seen in isolation from the conflict in darfur, sudan. it is quite obvious that the government of chad is supporting the rebels in darfur. the chadian opposition announces that darfur rebels are fighting alongside the chadian government army against chadian rebels. my country sweden is supporting the eufor force with a few hundred soldiers. the aim is to guarantee the safety of the civilian population, but unfortunately there is much to suggest that the force is being used as a pawn in the internal game in chad. in a statement, chad's president idriss deby called for the eufor force to be deployed to eastern chad as soon as possible. deby clearly wants to use eufor to protect the survival of his own regime. a question to the commission and the council: how can you ensure that eufor acts completely independently of such pressures? what do you think of the government of debbie's cooperation with the rebels in darfur? am i wrong? then prove it to me! as regards the prisoners of the civilian opposition in chad, it is good that some releases have been made, but much more needs to be done. what is the eu doing to ensure the immediate release of all political prisoners?
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. the fact that taxation at eu level is a sensitive subject is shown by the work on this report. now, i thought that the broad understanding that we had to do something radical to meet the climate demands would make it easier to get the really sharp proposals heard. it didn't really happen. i can certainly understand mr rühle's criticism of the level of ambition, but at the same time the work of our parliament is about finding reasonable compromises, which the commissioner was also keen to see, which can be implemented throughout our union. i have had to give way to arguments both as regards the scope of tax increases and the time taken to implement them. excellent cooperation with colleagues becsey and rosati made me realise that if we were to get a report through at all, i would have to adjust the level without completely missing the purpose of my original proposals. the commission proposal was approved in its entirety. i didn't win everything, but i didn't lose everything, either. navigating between the shores of luxembourg and the greens has not been easy. emissions from transport are increasing significantly. we have to do something about that. the fact that eu member states are competing with different levels of diesel taxes is therefore neither reasonable nor right, as the commissioner very rightly points out. no one can think that it is good for the internal market for giant lorries to make long detours to fill up cheap diesel, in luxembourg for example. the so-called 'tank tourism' is bad for the environment and distorts competition. it also leads to a loss of tax revenue. moreover, there is no reason to have separate taxes on unleaded petrol and diesel. this is what happened to the compromise, which was almost unanimously supported by the committee. the minimum rates on diesel are being raised more slowly than the commission proposes, from the current €302 per 1 000 litres to the same level as the tax on unleaded petrol, €359 per 1 000 litres by 2015. the commission wanted to see an increase as early as 2012. latvia, lithuania, poland, bulgaria and romania shall have until 1 january 2016 to introduce the higher minimum tax. the commission wants to raise the minimum price to eur 380 per 1 000 litres by 2014. the committee also calls for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's energy policy is not distorted by the use of fossil fuels. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. this is of course a very sensitive issue, particularly in the context of subsidiarity. however, this is a possible way of achieving an increasing convergence of tax levels. at the same time, we know that the necessary environmental requirements will be weighed in future assessments of the use of economic instruments. to those who don't think i'm tough enough in my demands and that i've been playing around with the ambition level, i want to say: i agree with you on the merits. we should have been able to agree on higher levels and shorter implementation periods, which the commission and the commissioner were keen to do, but the eu today is not the same eu as it was just five years ago - fortunately, i would add. the economic conditions for tax harmonization are not the same as before. if we are to tackle transboundary emissions, we must find reasonable compromises that everyone can live with. we all have to realize that. i would also add that several of the old member states deserve criticism for not following up on previous decisions on tax increases. the committee also welcomes the commission's proposal to introduce a new system of taxation of capital. oil prices are at record highs today. last i heard on the news, it was closer to $110 a barrel. there is now a broad consensus on gradual fiscal convergence, which i hope the commissioner will see - at least in the committee - and i hope for the support of my colleagues and the house tomorrow, a broad support, so that we have a report on which we can work further.

in writing. - some member states have strong self-interest in promoting increased arms exports. the creation of a common code of conduct for arms exports is therefore likely to force some member states with a more restrictive policy to compromise. we believe that the monitoring of arms exports should be carried out by the member states in accordance with their national legislation. sweden shall retain the right to restrict arms exports if it so wishes. cooperation is desirable to advance global disarmament efforts, but this is best done internationally, within the framework of the united nations, given the experience, expertise and global reach of the un.
in writing. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide network of information centres for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. in addition, a gender perspective must be integrated into the criminal justice system and prisons. we do, however, have some objections to some of the contents of the report. we do not want to see a harmonisation of prison conditions in europe and we strongly question the report's proposals on special criminal penalties or alternative punishments for women, pregnant women and women with small children. as regards the child's contact with the parents during the period of incarceration and the time thereafter, it must, with the best interests of the child in mind, concern both parents and not only the mother or one parent.
in writing. - we support the work being done in the member states to modernise and adapt prison policies to better meet the needs of prisoners, and as part of this take into account the specific needs of women. since prison services are not a european competence, we have voted against the report. measures relating to visiting rules, the operation of institutions, training for prison staff, leisure activities for prisoners or social support measures are and should remain the competence of the member states in order to adapt and develop them to national and local needs.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. of course, it is also vital that the child's needs be given the greatest possible consideration in these situations. however, we believe that it is up to the member states to decide how they will comply with this. we particularly emphasize the right of each member state to define its own criminal law and thus also to impose appropriate penalties. the european union will also not intervene in the detailed regulation of whether or not a prison has to have a gym, visiting rules or work during the period of imprisonment. this will be governed by the member states and their respective electors and will be based on public debate in society. we recall that all eu member states are democratic states which meet the copenhagen criteria.

for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the eu is constantly moving into new areas. the problem is that there is no principle for what the eu should do. however, the principle of subsidiarity, which is celebrated in the speeches but rejected in the decision-making process, is a suitable one. it opposes the centralisation of research and development, except for very large-scale projects such as galileo and fusion power. research and development bureaucratized in large organizations yields less for the money, not more. there is seldom any reason for member states to send money to brussels and then have their researchers claim it back. this conclusion is particularly true in the case of research and development for smes. the enthusiasm of scientists and companies is because they are fooled into thinking that there will be more resources in total. there is no indication that this is the case. thank you for speaking.
the cultural conditions have fundamentally changed with the breakthrough and spread of the internet. i would say that it has been almost exclusively positive. culture has been revitalized, democratized and disseminated to more people. despite this, much of the european debate is currently focused on problems allegedly created by the internet for cultural practitioners. it's unfortunate, you know. that is not to say that culture is not facing challenges. copyright infringement and illegal file sharing are examples of such challenges. much of this report also focuses on these problems. right now, there are several proposals circulating around europe on how to deal with them. unfortunately, our basic civil liberties and rights are often not respected. for example, perhaps the hottest proposal right now is to ban those who have committed copyright infringement from accessing the internet. i would like to take this opportunity to ask my colleagues to support the amendment i have tabled to this report. in short, my proposal emphasises the positive role that the internet has played for culture and democracy, but it also rejects the idea of banning individual citizens from accessing the internet. copyright infringement and file sharing must be fought with sanctions that are proportionate and respect our freedoms and rights. however, to prohibit individuals from accessing the internet is, in my view, a serious restriction on freedom of expression and information and anything but proportionate. the internet is more than just a copying machine or a channel for distributing illegal movies or music. it's an arena for free speech, for information and communication. to my little brother, the internet is as natural as television or a conversation in a schoolyard during a break. and should we apply similar restrictions to other copyright infringements? i was not long ago in university, and unfortunately there were many students who illegally copied their course material, but to punish them by forbidding them to read books or write articles would of course be unreasonable. a person who has stolen food from a store is not prohibited from selling milk, and a person who has stolen a bicycle is not prohibited from using the street. so let's not do something as crazy with other crimes just because they're committed on the internet. thank you, thank you!
first of all, i would like to thank the commission and commissioner stavros dimas for the green paper on adapting to climate change. it is a book that every decision-maker and business leader, indeed every european, should read to understand the challenges we face and what we need to do. it is, after all, just as many speakers have already stated: a very large number of people are affected by the climate change we see coming. we have so far spoken a lot in economic terms in the european debate, but climate change will change our entire environment. one subject that we have not yet discussed sufficiently is health. the world health organization has pointed out that 60,000 people died last year that could be linked to climate change. it requires a completely different kind of community planning, a different kind of technology and a very large investment. it may seem daunting to many people, but these are investments we must make to cope with climate change and technological progress and to compete with other parts of the world. as always in an engineering revolution, there will be winners and losers, but throughout the eu system we are working to ensure that european industry and european workers are the winners in this situation. however, to achieve this, large investments, forward-looking decision-making and cross-border cooperation are needed. we cannot decide on transport in this report we call for action to make agriculture take its climate responsibility. agriculture includes the livestock industry, which accounts for 18 percent of global greenhouse gas emissions. eighteen percent of the time. this is stated by the fao in its 2006 report 'livestock's long shadow'. so what can we do to bring down that 18 percent of emissions? when the chairman of the ipcc, rajendra pachauri, spoke for us in the climate committee, he called for a reduction in meat consumption. he's absolutely right. we've never in the history of the world eaten as much meat as we do today. since the 1950's, world meat production has increased more than fivefold. it's not sustainable. i would like to ask commissioner stavros dimas: do you agree with rajenda pachauri that we should eat less meat? i call on the commission to draw up a plan for reducing meat consumption. finally, a few proposals: an information campaign for vegetarian diets, calling on member states to introduce meat taxes so that meat becomes more expensive and therefore cheaper, investing more in the green in our own eu institutions, for example a vegetarian week in the european parliament, the commission and the council would be a good, modern and green start for a more sustainable union.

- is eu action in the urgent fight against cancer more effective than equivalent action within existing international organisations with broad expertise, such as the who? do the eu institutions have a broader competence than the member states in how to fight cancer? the junilista answer to these questions is no. the resolution calls for the establishment of an interinstitutional cancer task force within the eu, the launch of eu-initiated information and education campaigns on cancer, the establishment by the commission of an advisory committee on cancer prevention, eu legislative action to promote and support initiatives on poor sun protection, the commission's action to create smoke-free environments, member states' guaranteeing the availability of multidisciplinary teams of oncologists throughout the country and the recognition of oncology as a medical specialty. the thinking behind these views and demands is certainly commendable. the fundamental question, however, is what is the added value of the eu regulating all this. where is the trust in the member states? what happened to the principle of subsidiarity? do eu regulations really benefit those who need quality legislation and thoughtful action, namely cancer patients? where is the recognition that cancer is a global problem, that it should be dealt with primarily on a global level? on the basis of the above considerations, the junilista has voted against the final vote on the report in question.

in writing. - the junilist considers that cultural issues in general fall within the political responsibility of the member states. we have therefore voted against this report in its entirety. the report is outside the legislative procedure and is nothing more than a suggestion by the federalist majority of the european parliament that the eu should intervene even more in the cultural field. we have voted in favour of the amendment which aims to prevent citizens from accessing the internet, because we are opposed to the french proposal that filters throughout the eu should be able to be blocked from the internet. in principle, however, we believe that the issue of file sharing is a matter for the member states to legislate on.

in writing. - in the european parliament there is a term for reports containing endless political wish lists, whether we have competence in the field or not - they are called christmas trees. the report by mr bono is one of them. however, an important light was shed by the excellent amendment by christofer fjellner 1, which clearly took a political stance to signal that attempts to shut down the internet for pirates should not be recommended. since information has been leaked that president nicolas sarkozy wanted to transfer the french experiment to the eu level, there is every reason to support the expression that this would not be appreciated, to put it mildly. when the amendment was adopted, i therefore voted in favour of a report which i would otherwise have tried to vote against.
in writing. - the cultural reports drawn up by parliament are often difficult to deal with. the intention is of course good and, as the report states, there is every reason to use the pan-european level to support artists in their professional activities, where this is justified. this is not to say that everything has to be done at eu level. on the contrary, the principle of subsidiarity must be strictly observed in this respect, so that the member states do not lose the opportunity to develop their own cultural policies. it was therefore partly a matter of principle that decided to vote against the report. however, in this particular case, the wording of large parts of the report is even more serious. reason has a tone that's frightening. the same insinuation sneaks into several other parts of the text. you have to be careful and nuanced if you're going to discuss concepts like cultural heritage. you didn't have that here.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. as jutta haug said, this is the last budget under the old model, at least if the new treaty is implemented, but it is also a budget that is a prelude to a more long-term budgetary reform. we are also entering what is known as the midterm review. it is therefore natural for us to take a more long-term view of budgetary matters. we believe, and this is important in the resolution, that important new political priorities must also be able to find their way into the budget. for example, we have today a clear lack of funds both in category 1 for growth issues and so on and in category 4. we therefore expect the commission to make a bold proposal for the mid-term review, but we must also start with next year's budget. we have a few points of particular importance. i am thinking first of all of climate and energy policy. i am the rapporteur on the budget for the committee on industry, and we have, for about is the food crisis. we are now in a situation where the evolution of food prices may indeed have to affect the eu budget. we must not be outsiders to this. my conclusion is that as society changes, we must also be prepared to change the eu budget.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the question of organ donation is an ethically sensitive issue and must be governed by national values. cooperation across national borders may be done to achieve quality and safety requirements, but the choice to donate or receive organs is the choice of the individual or the loved ones. a choice that is often rooted in a cultural context. the proposed european donation card is inappropriate because the rules on consent, distribution and organisational structure differ from one member state to another. health care is and will remain a national issue. however, the swedish junilista is in favour of a voluntary exchange of bodies and knowledge within the existing organisations of the member states. the measures proposed by parliament to bring the debate on organ donation, national telephone lines, positive media coverage of organ donation and the training of sports stars may be justified, but these efforts will be decided by the member states themselves, based on the social climate prevailing in each country.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i have therefore been rapporteur for these so-called other institutions, seven of them, which include the council, the court of justice, the court of auditors, the economic and social committee, the committee of the regions, the data protection supervisor and the ombudsman. i have not had any serious objections to anything i have watched. there have been normal remarks about what can and should be improved. there have been no disclosures which could in any way call into question the granting of a discharge to any of these seven institutions. i would like to take this opportunity to remind you that the granting of a discharge, the demanding of responsibility, is the foundation of democracy. i have no confidence in the way this house is handling this task because i had the exact same task of looking at these seven institutions two years ago and then, ladies and gentlemen, found the most appalling interiors in the committee of the regions. i found that fraud of the worst kind had been committed there. i've been able to extract material for you all to read in the secret reading room. there were three separate investigations into this: one in olaf, our anti-fraud agency, and one in the court of auditors, and the committee of the regions itself had an internal investigation under way. they had fired their internal auditors. the list of objections was endless. i therefore naturally suggested that we should not grant discharge in that situation. i told the committee. the committee voted to discharge him anyway. i said the same thing in this house two years ago and you voted overwhelmingly for discharge. this shows that when you do this today on my recommendation, you do it not because i say so, but out of old habit. this congregation is not taking responsibility. it is more concerned with the eu's reputation. you're more concerned with deceiving the citizens, the voters. i don't want them to know what's going on. it is therefore with considerable sadness that i say 'yes, this time these other institutions can be granted a discharge'. i can do this with a clear conscience. most of you can't.

group - i would just like to emphasize that we grant discharge because we think that there is a basis in the court of auditors' declaration for approving the management of the economy by these independent authorities. but there are also problems there which we hope will be worked on. this is particularly important if all the institutions - the council, the commission and the european parliament - are to improve their ability to scrutinise the requests of public authorities. i hope everyone takes responsibility for that. i will come back next year as rapporteur on this matter and hope to discuss it more in depth with the commission.
the rapporteur. - i would like to take this opportunity to make some constructive criticism of the commission and the decentralised authorities, as i still believe that the rapporteur, mr hans-peter martin, has not succeeded in doing so. as rapporteur for the next year's report, i would like to focus on three questions in particular. they are also mentioned in the reports. the first is to evaluate the possibilities of merging the administrations of several of the smaller authorities and thus freeing up resources and expertise to comply with the complex regulatory bodies surrounding the authorities. many of these decentralised authorities have problems with compliance with, for example, the general budgetary principles or the financial regulation, procurement laws, etc. that is the point of view which is being repeated here and which i hope the commission will work on. we also propose that the independent authorities, like the other institutions, should have their staff allocations reduced by a percentage which it is not thought that they will be able to fill. i believe that too can free up considerable resources. last but not least, we propose that the earmarked revenue paid by the authorities to the commission each year and recovered should be set off against the draft budget adopted by the european parliament. there are three things on which i would like to have continued dialogue with the commission and the decentralised authorities during this period until i intend to take over the discharge procedure. last but not least, i would also like to say something about the court of auditors' special report on the european fisheries policy. it is also part of the package of discharge for the commission which we are adopting today, and the court of auditors is delivering a devastating criticism which must lead to far-reaching reforms of the common fisheries policy. three conclusions i have drawn, which are also in the report we are voting on, are firstly that it is unacceptable for member states to set fishing quotas year after year which are much higher than what science says is sustainable, (2) that the commission and the member states must be tougher in fighting crime and fraud with quotas and (3) that we must develop a new fisheries policy which strengthens the incentives and reasons for professional fishermen to care for the stocks. there are three points i would like you to take seriously in the report we are voting on today.

the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. the junilists are in favour of increased cooperation between member states to combat terrorism and other serious crossborder crime. the european parliament and any other supranational body shall not therefore take decisions in this area. minimum provisions on access to legal aid for individuals and data protection are regulated in each member state. in addition, there is already a basic and comprehensive protection in the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, which has been signed by all the member states. a functioning dna exchange already exists with interpol. it is also important to highlight the privacy aspect required by the use of dna. we therefore agree with the report on the request for dna for persons who have been acquitted or whose charges have been dropped. however, the report goes too far in many respects, as it talks about the introduction of framework legislation in several areas, such as the guarantee of justice and the adoption of a general legal act covering all police and judicial cooperation in criminal matters. it also talks about the establishment of joint rapid reaction forces. a country's military resources are strictly a national matter and should not be regulated at eu level. the junilista has therefore voted against the report.
