was. that is why i do not think we need a specific european gambling police. i don't think sports agents make too much money, and that would be an argument for us to create a special training for sports agents. nor should we equalise the income of successful and less successful clubs. attempts to limit what the report calls the overactive schedules of athletes will not make them resist doping. however, we must clarify a number of incorrect claims made about the gambling market in europe. there is no link between private players in the gambling market and match-fixing. on the contrary, some of the biggest scandals we have seen in europe have hit precisely those countries with monopolies. there is no connection between a gambling monopoly and a restrictive gambling policy. on the contrary, many of the state monopolies in many member states are some of the biggest buyers of advertising. it's not countries with competition that have an unregulated gambling market. on the contrary, it is found in many monopoly countries where gambling services are the least regulated and where monopolists are usually free to act as they wish. we must not try to keep the eu out of an area where the eu actually has an important role to play by using inaccurate facts. we have free movement of services in europe, which of course must also apply to gambling services. the member states' false talk about gambling addiction is usually concealed by pure protectionism and the protection of their own revenues from the gambling monopoly. all this hypocrisy about gambling policy reveals, perhaps, that it is the finance ministers of the member states who suffer from the worst kind of gambling addiction. that is why we moderates find it very difficult to support large parts of this report.
in writing. - we believe that the common agricultural policy should be abolished. at the same time, we believe that agreements should be respected. in today's vote we have therefore voted in favour of the commission's proposal to comply with the accession treaties with portugal, greece and spain, where the eu has unfortunately committed itself to guaranteeing support for cotton production, which will also include production aid. we therefore regret that the commission has not been able to present a proposal in this area which is in line with the policy for other agricultural sectors where aid has been decoupled from production. the european parliament's report (a6-0166/2008) proposed the possibility of even less decoupling of aid, and we therefore chose to vote against this report.
in writing. - the junilist supports the important work on human rights taking place worldwide, but considers this annual report to be another attempt by this parliament to promote the eu's competence in foreign policy areas. the situation in several countries is deeply worrying and the international community must of course react. however, this will be done through the un and its various bodies, which, unlike the eu, have legitimacy and broad support among the majority of states. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. - i chose to abstain on the matter, as the cappatos report contains elements in favour of eu decision-making (in particular, on the basis of paragraphs 15, 45 and 141). i believe that it is up to the member states to decide and take decisions on human rights issues.
in writing. the report also calls for the eu to be able to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action to combat terrorism. the european union is a major player in the world economy, and the eu is a major player in the world economy. however, as an election observer, the eu has a role to play alongside these other international bodies. the experience and expertise that the eu has built up is of great importance and should continue to be used. we have therefore decided to support the report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment. the political situation in burma and the release of political prisoners are very pressing issues, but they should not be made part of the eu's supranational trade policy. we see this as another attempt by the eu to expand its powers in foreign policy. on the other hand, junilista sees free trade agreements between the eu and other regions as a natural consequence of having a single market and a common trade policy. we have therefore voted in favour of the report.
in writing. - there should be a framework for the permanent access of lobbyists to the premises of the european parliament; we support the current code of conduct. we support greater transparency in political decision-making. we voted against the report because it leads to a bureaucracy which is counter to its own aims. the full regulation of contacts between politicians makes it difficult for citizens and elected representatives to have natural and open contacts. citizens should be able to contact their representatives without their conversations and views being publicly reported. it is not possible to give a detailed account of how perceptions and opinions are formed. the arguments and responsibility for decisions should be open, not the dialogue between individuals. it is important to protect the privacy of individual members and citizens. the transparency advocated in the report risks being illusory and misleading. transparency must be based on politicians taking responsibility for their positions and explaining their reasons for doing so. furthermore, a joint register of lobbyists between the european parliament and the commission would be to give up parliament's independence. in conclusion, political decision-making should be based on the integrity of the elected representatives and be presumed to be so.
in writing. - a member of parliament always needs to be given relevant information in order to make good decisions. it goes without saying that one person cannot be an expert on everything. in this sense, lobbying can be a valuable addition to information gathering by providing expertise and relevant impact assessments. however, citizens have the right to know which special interests are active. the influence of a lobby group does not necessarily depend on financial strength animal rights activists and other social movements can be at least as successful as industrial interests. of course, registering interests is not straightforward, not least in terms of defining who or which groups are engaged in lobbying. as a policy-maker, however, i think it is relevant to continuously identify those who are spending time and energy on influencing legislation. if you have nothing to hide, you have nothing to worry about. in the sun, the trolls burst.
in writing. - i voted for the lobby report in its final form because it represents a microscopic advance compared to the present situation. the fact that it did not succeed in doing so is in itself a testament to the influence that lobbyists have on the eu system.
in writing. − sport is part of the lisbon treaty and will therefore, with the entry into force of the treaty, play a more prominent role in eu policy. we social democrats would like to stress that sport is already a transnational activity, but that this does not mean that european institutions such as the council, the commission and the parliament should be involved in the management of sport in detail, but that sport should be self-regulating as much as possible in order to maintain its independence and autonomy.
in the case of the commission, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. firstly, the report refers to the treaty of lisbon, which has not yet been ratified and will be the subject of a referendum in ireland in a month's time. the federalist majority in the european parliament is thus violating fundamental constitutional principles. the lisbon treaty is also 96% identical to the constitutional treaty already rejected in referendums in france and the netherlands. it is therefore completely unprincipled to refer to the treaty of lisbon in this report. secondly, this report means that the eu is entering another policy area which the junilists consider to be a matter for the member states to manage. we therefore vote against this report.
in writing. - i voted against the report because i think it is too closely related to the lisbon treaty. i don't want to make sports supranational. i think it's good the way it is. i do think, however, that the amendments on women and sport were good.
in writing. - the report on sport contained a lot of interesting and sensible information. no one can reasonably object to the eu member states taking strong action against doping and xenophobia in sport. more controversially, but in my view more logically, it was to support the commission's idea of a common european police force to monitor sporting events. that is as far as i was prepared to go in supporting the report. unfortunately, the really big question - the question of the liberalisation of the gambling monopoly in europe - did not land quite right. i myself believe that liberalization, in controlled forms and with licensed gambling and lottery operators, could satisfy both the consumer's interest in a functioning gambling offer and the sports movement's need for funds. that's why i put my vote down.
in writing. − we recognise that sport has a special role in society as an instrument of social inclusion and integration, and as a promoter of local democracy. we acknowledge the considerable importance of sport in health terms as a preventive measure for obesity and chronic conditions. we fully support the notion of strengthening the role of women in sport; the importance of protecting athletes of all ages during and after their sports career; the promotion of sports in society, including physical activities in the school curriculum; the need for redistributive measures in sports financing; and the citizens right to information and wide access to broadcasts of sports events. however, we do not support the enhanced role of eu-level policy in sport. we therefore cannot support the steps taken to further increase eu policy in this area. specifically, we cannot support the idea of setting up a european police force for sport.
for the gue/ngl group. there is no doubt that those who break environmental laws should be punished. the penalties must be substantial and dissuasive so as to avoid environmental offences in the future. but should we harmonize the criminal law? should the eu decide on penalties prison sentences, fines and other things? no, that is for the member states to decide. the risk of harmonization legislation is always that progressive countries will have to lower the level of their legislation. this is not acceptable. if we leave the decisionmaking in this area to the eu, what is the next step? the eu is obviously committed to improving the way member states work on environmental legislation. good examples can and should be set and help and support can be given to those member states which are lagging behind for various reasons. the establishment of tables of concrete indicators for comparing member states is a good example. we will then be creating a system that has great legitimacy and is democratically anchored. it's also the best way to protect the environment.
the rapporteur. - my fellow members! first of all, a big thank you to all the shadow rapporteurs for the good cooperation. we have all seen the images of the recent famine. in a short time, agricultural prices, especially food prices, have skyrocketed. this is a blow to many developing countries that are heavily dependent on food imports and have previously lived on the margins. it is hoped that this report on international trade in raw materials will help to remedy some of these problems. the eradication of hunger and poverty are commitments we have made since the signing of the so-called millennium development goals. un secretary-general ban ki-moon recently warned that rising food prices are jeopardizing the most important millennium development goal - to halve world poverty. so it is high time for swift action. the recent food crisis, in particular, has shown that food is not just any commodity. if even the most basic demand for people to be fed cannot be met, the rhetoric of development is of little value. therefore, caution should be exercised in making food an international commodity like any other. yet, the developing countries of the world have been forced to deregulate their food policies and move from production for domestic needs to export production. this is the policy of the imf, the world bank, the united states and also the eu, which have pushed these demands. by eliminating protective tariffs and subsidies, many developing countries have become extremely vulnerable to sharp fluctuations in the price of food and raw materials. for example, haiti, which used to be self-sufficient in rice, was forced by the international monetary fund in 1995 to lower its rice tariffs. state-subsidized rice from the united states flooded in, and local production was destroyed. today, three quarters of all the rice consumed in haiti is imported from the united states. i hope that this report will give us the tools to face the problems and challenges posed by the uncertain trade in raw materials. some of the things we are focusing on in this report are helping developing countries to diversify their economies so that they move from a situation where they only export one or two commodities to allowing more advanced production with more refined products. the importance of policy space in developing countries is also discussed. this must mean that these countries actually have the possibility and the economic policy tools to support, for example, the development of domestic agriculture. we also call on the european commission to review the flex financing scheme which aims to stabilise commodity prices. we also raise the gender dimension in trade in raw materials, or rather the lack of it. it is extremely important that we always take the gender dimension into account when negotiating international trade agreements. this report also deals with fair trade. fair trade can be used as a tool precisely to support small-scale producers in developing countries and to increase pressure for higher social and environmental standards in these countries. we demand that all eu institutions apply the principle of fair trade in their public procurement and other purchasing. we also raise the problem of the growing consumption of animals, i.e. the increasing demand for meat and dairy products, which leads to an increased demand for cereals for animal husbandry instead of for human food. there are a lot of other things that we're also looking at. finally, i would like to say that this work has revealed the true face of the european union. while we have tried to speak of the needs and conditions of the developing countries, european industry, with the help of its representatives in the european parliament, has tried to focus on the short-term commercial interests of companies in having a stable supply of cheap raw materials. i would like to call on everyone to support the amendments tabled by the left group, the socialists and the greens. amendments nos 20 and 21 are particularly important. we can then balance this report so that we have a stronger development dimension.
the rapporteur. - commissioner janez potočnik, we look forward to the communication from the commission and the conference. it is good that you want to emphasize the food safety, the environment and the development dimension! francisco assis, i agree with you completely that we need to rethink our economic model. zbigniew zaleski, i agree that trade must not be a form of neo-colonial theft of raw materials from developing countries. i therefore call on mr zaleski and all the others to support amendments nos 20 and 21 in particular. take a good look at them. look also at amendment no 26, which deals with biofuels. we are calling for environmental and social standards for biofuels. the problem is that more and more cereals are used to produce meat, that is to say, to raise animals on cereals, but also that more and more cereals are used to produce fuel for our cars. it is a call to the commission we want measures to reduce private consumption of meat and to reduce private consumption of meat so that we have more grain to feed people! the report calls for urgent action to combat climate change. trade must also take its responsibility. we need to reduce all unnecessary emissions and reduce transport. a large part of the trade is in products that could just as easily be produced locally. we need to do more business with green technology and smart environmental technology. if we do not do this and if trade does not take its responsibility, then we will soon have no raw materials left to trade and then we will have no food left to eat. it's incredibly important that we actually take our climate responsibility to its fullest!
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the subsidised tobacco industry is a clear example of the double standard of the eu and the european parliament. the eu has proudly proclaimed the fight against disease to be a priority and spends a few million swedish crowns on information campaigns every year, while the eu covertly subsidises unprofitable tobacco growers with several billion crowns every year. the report extends the phase-out of these aids until 2013. the rapporteur is trying to clean his hands of the negative effects of tobacco consumption. it is simply claimed that because the eu's tobacco production is so small, only 4%, the impact on final consumption is non-existent. what kind of reasoning is this? would that justify continued subsidies? i believe that the eu's tobacco production is 4% too high. moreover, european tobacco is so bad that only a fraction of it is sold on the european market. one third of it burns up. the tobacco interests have been allowed to rule for long enough. our citizens demand that we take our political responsibility. fellas, this last blow is bad! it's time to get rid of the double standards! i therefore urge you to vote against the report tomorrow.
the committee on women's rights and gender equality has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to thank commissioner meglena kuneva and the rapporteur, mr lasse lehtinen. i welcome the strategy and am very pleased that it sees consumers as an important group to protect. we know that we will never have a thriving single market unless we have satisfied and secure consumers. but i share the rapporteur's view that continuous monitoring of the strategy is needed if we are to really develop it to the benefit of consumers. however, as rapporteur for strategy in the committee on equality, i am disappointed that the committee responsible did not take account of more than one of our 13 amendments. there are many important and good suggestions. we'd like to see a more gender-specific perspective. consumer policy is definitely not gender neutral. gender should be taken into account as well as children, the elderly and the disabled. i hope we can come back to this in the next follow-up!
for the gue/ngl group. thank you, mr president! consumer rights are often weakly supported and therefore need to be strengthened. i would therefore like to thank the rapporteur and his colleagues in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection for their success in strengthening consumer support for the groups most in need of protection: children and the elderly. strong consumer support requires active consumer organisations. alone is not strong. it is through these organisations that consumers become stronger and gain more knowledge and information. therefore, these organisations must have a secure and secure funding. but the uncertainty of today's cross-border purchases must be resolved by the possibility of bringing class actions against companies that break the law. the main problem facing consumers today is the difficulty of adjudicating a dispute between different countries, as there are no bodies to resolve such crossborder disputes. the eu should primarily develop bodies to resolve disputes, not create more eu legislation. the proposal to set up an eu consumer ombudsman is extremely dubious in my view. why create more services to be financed at eu level and thus risk reducing the funding to consumer organisations? it's not good for the consumer. finally, i regret that the amendments from the committee on women's rights and gender equality - as usual, i regret to say - were voted down in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. they deserved a better fate.
mr president, in the internal market consumers and providers, as well as goods and services, should be able to move freely. consumers must not be less protected in other member states than at home if we want to ensure and enhance cross-border activities. therefore, i welcome the horizontal approach in the consumer policy and the better protection it will lead to. it means empowered consumers and strong consumer protection as well as a business-friendly internal market legislation. however, we are concerned about the hasty approach concerning collective redress. it is too early for parliament to take a position. we need thorough studies of the existing systems in the member states and an assessment of the need of a european collective redress system. we do not have enough knowledge at this stage of the consequences in terms of risk abuse, higher costs for consumers and businesses and more regulation. i do not believe that anyone supports the introduction of a european system in the eu knowing all the drawbacks and disproportionate consequences it would bring. for now, we encourage other means of redress which are more rapid and at lower costs and leave the regulation to the member states to be decided nationally. existing instruments and non-legislative tools can be used at eu level. we call on the commission to carry out the study and present its results to parliament and the member states. why go further than what is needed in order to achieve the objectives of the treaty? why take away the possibility of choice for member states keeping different dispute-solving mechanisms? there are many questions to be answered. there is no clear indication so far that an eu-collective redress system brings added value to the internal market and its consumers and businesses. the eu should not legislate without strong reasons.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. when anne van lancker began work on the european parliament's opinion, she had the ambition to concentrate it on a few key points because we knew that both the commission and the council would propose that the guidelines should not be changed at all. our tactic was to concentrate on a few points so that they might listen to us to some extent. it hasn't happened. although the basic approach is the same, we now have a lot of amendments, not just a few. i think it would have been better to concentrate on what anne van lancker said - that we are clearly integrating the social dimension, a policy for all those who are outside the labour market and do not share in the wealth. despite the good employment performance, we can see that many of the new jobs are insecure and unprofitable. there's no safety in the workplace. and the discussion we have had on flexicurity should be reflected more clearly in the guidelines because it has been debated for several years. the same applies to gender issues. the council is not here, i think, unfortunately, because it will not listen to parliament at all. they will do exactly as they have decided before. i think that we in the european parliament, at the next triennial review, must seriously consider changing the tactics and the way we work so that parliament has a real say in the future shape of the guidelines.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this report reflects the appreciation of the values which should characterize a free labour market. but there is a major objection: it is wrong to establish such rules at eu level. they then become part of the eu's common acquis, the acquis communataire part of the holy scriptures. the possibility of future reforms is disappearing across the eu. if germany and france had pushed through a common labour market policy for the eu in the 1970s, based on the political ideas that prevailed then, europe's economy would be on the verge of collapse today. the buzzword of the day, flexicurity, is constantly being used in the report. this is because there has been no common labour market policy. denmark has therefore been able to step up and develop something that today looks very promising. the report can be usefully reduced to one sentence: the european parliament recommends that member states take a look at the danish flexicurity model to see if they have anything to learn. that's it.
in writing. - the content of the report is generally positive, but it is hypocritical to talk about social responsibility, local economy and gender equality while at the same time trying to forbid the procurement rules to take these into account. i don't want to contribute to that. the report gives a false picture of reality and i therefore abstain.
in writing. − the report dealing with trade in raw materials and commodities expresses many important issues from a development perspective. the report emphasises the problematic occurrence of speculation in inflating prices and increasing volatility on the markets, which needs to be regulated. it also calls for more support for diversification in developing countries, and highlights the importance of policy space for these countries to enable the development of not least their agricultural sector. the report also criticises the increase of meat consumption, and asks for ways of tackling that. however, the report also expresses points, which we find deeply problematic. this refers foremost to the repeated emphasis on the international competitiveness and the urge for the european industry to secure cheap access to raw materials. we do not support this focus, and can only conclude that the neoliberal face of the eu once more has been articulated. in general the report is a step in the right direction compared with earlier positions of parliament on trade policy.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. i am, however, against the idea of harmonising consumer protection in the eu and calling for more countries to join emu.
in writing. − the report proposes to establish an eu consumer ombudsman. we are sceptical towards this idea for several reasons: this post may create significant expenses which risk underming the funding of consumer organisations. moreover, the creation of another post at european level risks being too remote from citizens. having said this, we still support the report since its strengthens consumers access to collective redress, which is fundamental for consumers’ trust in making safe cross-border purchases.
in writing. - we have chosen to vote in favour of this own-initiative report because it contains a number of good papers on the creation of a sustainable ecological and economic management of the eu's marine areas. we have also chosen to support the idea of a european harbour day. there is reason to be sceptical of major campaigning by the eu institutions, but in this case we chose to support this because the environmental situation in the oceans is a pressing issue. we do, however, consider that the report contains some texts which can be interpreted as being too favourable to commercial fishing. the eu fleet is currently overcapacity and needs to be reduced in the face of dwindling fish stocks. it is wrong to guarantee the employment of professional fishermen in fishing. active labour market training is one of several measures that can be used to empower workers and regions that depend on the fishing industry.
in writing. - we fully share some of the views expressed in this report, such as the urgency of combating sulphur and nitrogen oxide emissions from ships and the over-bureaucratized and centralised nature of the common fisheries policy. however, most of the proposals put forward are negative. we find it difficult to see the added value of the eu establishing an annual day for the sea. we also question the value of eu funding for maritime research, the establishment of an eu-level action plan to map shipwrecks and underwater archaeological sites, and we are critical of the eu institutions' views on spatial planning in coastal areas. the report thus provides another example of the european parliament's attempt to gain influence in an increasing number of policy areas. the respect for the principle of subsidiarity, which is often celebrated but rarely applied, is completely absent. we cannot accept that. we have therefore voted against the final vote.
for the gue/ngl group. thanks to the rapporteur britta thomsen who highlights the problem of gender inequality in science and research. the lack of equality permeates the whole of society. however, through the involvement and opinion-forming of women's organisations and others, awareness of the lack of equality in many areas has increased and more people have realised the negative consequences for society as a whole. but in science itself, it's been quiet for too long. that is why this report is particularly important. while the eu emphasises the role of research in economic development, research is still often reserved for one gender. in the public sector, 35 per cent of researchers are women, in the private sector only 18 per cent. the scientific council of the european research council has 22 members, 5 of whom are women. this report focuses on these problems, but in my view it is not enough. we need to raise the ambition level even further. a comment on the gender equality debate: we still have to defend the demand for gender equality by saying that it is necessary for economic growth and development. when will we be able to say that gender equality is an end in itself that does not need to be defended in economic terms? equal rights are human rights!
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank dimitrios papadimoulis for a great collaboration. i also think that cooperation with the council went well, even though we had some disagreements about the content. first, we must establish that mercury is a toxic and dangerous heavy metal. i'll start with the criticism. i think it is strange that, while we are prohibiting the export of hazardous waste and are almost ending the use of mercury ourselves, we want to delay the export ban so that industry can empty its stocks before it is defined as waste. it is a dangerous chemical which we know will be released into the wild by gold-miners and which will destroy the wildlife of other countries. i really wish we had a much earlier end date. but a compromise is a compromise. i am glad that the industry's voluntary commitments are now incorporated in the legislation so that they are actually implemented and that all the pieces that were essential are included in the export ban, such as cinnamomers, oxides and calomel. in this way, we have at least obtained a comprehensive legislation. when it comes to how we deal with waste, we're talking about fairly small amounts. think of 10,000 tons of liquid mercury. it's actually no more than it would fit here in front of the podium -- it's 10 by 10 by 10 meters. that's all it is. but it's still a very large amount of mercury. a lot of molecules, if you will. we can't release a single one of them into the wild. the only way to effectively implement eu waste legislation is not to permanently deposit mercury in liquid form. for my group, therefore, article 7 was extremely important for a settlement, that is to say the article in which we return to the question of solidifying waste. we cannot have any permanent waste without having reverted to that method. i am glad to say that i recently received an e-mail from the swedish waste company saying that a pilot plant has now been built that can already today handle 500 kg of waste and convert it into insoluble solid form. when we come back to the issue in 2010, i can guarantee that the fixed form is a safe method that complies with eu legislation.
for the gue/ngl group. the content of this report is correct, but i would like to see more concrete action on climate change. nevertheless, the report is supported by the left group. in the report we note that global emissions increased by 70% between 1970 and 2004, that the last decade was the warmest ever and that we are facing several tipping points such as the melting of the greenland ice sheet. that is why we are calling for a reduction in eu emissions of 60-80% by 2050. we are calling for the climate labelling of consumer products and for action to be taken against the large emissions the eu is causing through imports from other countries. we also call for changes in lifestyle patterns. that is absolutely right, but we must also make sure that we make it easier for citizens to live in a climate-friendly way. for example, we must eat less meat and we must travel less by car and by air. unfortunately, the eu subsidizes the meat industry with gigantic sums. and unfortunately, the eu subsidizes the construction of highways that only lead to increased car traffic. our measures against the plane are also far from sufficient. unless we politicians create sustainable systems, we have no credibility in calling on people to change their lifestyles. like i said, we should go further. we need more concrete measures to change and we need to have higher ambitions for reduction. we must also be self-critical and question the economic order that prevails in the eu. the eu is built on the completion of the single market and on continuous growth. it only leads to increased transport and it's not sustainable.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to congratulate karl-heinz florenz on an excellent report. i would point out that it has a very strong political and symbolic significance, which is manifested not least by the fact that the leaders of the three major political groups opened the debate. we agree with the content of the florenz report. it is also written in such a way that all european citizens can actually read the text and understand what we mean. the fact that the design is so educational and clear is another positive point i would like to highlight. however, we still have issues that we need to discuss. i would like to call for support for the amendments dealing with the sea and the 1.5 degrees rise in sea level. we also need to look at public health issues. we shall deal with that in the next report. i expect solid writings in that area by then. we members now have the opportunity to show how determined we are and whether we are serious about our commitment to these issues. avril doyle and i are co-sponsors of the emissions trading act. if everyone is serious about what is being said today before the vote, we also expect strong support for the proposals we will put forward in our respective committees. finally, i would like to point out that we are in the midst of preparing for the copenhagen conference. this preparation must be done in a completely different way, in cooperation between parliament, the council and the commission and with the developing countries. it's only 18 months away.
madam president, first of all i would like to thank mrs oomen-ruijten for this report. it underlines that turkey in many areas already is part of european projects, but it also underlines that there is development, there is a movement going on, regarding reform of turkish society. at the same time it underlines that those reforms and those changes are being made far too slowly, that there is much more to do. but that leaves us with a fundamental question: is the european union and are europe and european values better off with a turkey that is fulfilling all the requirements and has made all the reforms that are underlined in the report, or are we better off with a turkey that will maybe lean in the future more to other parts of the world, to other values? i think the answer to this question is quite obvious, and that underlines that we must of course keep the pressure on regarding all the changes that need to be made in turkey with regard to freedom of expression, reform of the article 301 paragraph, freedom of religion, equal rights for women and men, not only in legislation but also in reality, and of course the need for a solution to the cyprus issue and a number of other issues. but with that perspective, if the accession negotiations deliver all these results, it is very important that the european union is open for the membership of turkey because this would strengthen european values, europe and the european union, and i think that is the necessary and obvious conclusion of this debate.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the european parliament and the council. we have therefore voted in favour of the proposals that the monday and thursday afternoon sessions of the strasbourg sessions should be closed with the hope that the strasbourg sessions will be closed altogether. the european parliament shall have a single seat and a single place of work. it is regrettable that there are member states whose political leaders consider themselves strong supporters of the idea of europe but at the same time do not want to let go of their own interests.
in writing. − the report on selection and authorisation of systems providing mobile satellite services is about how we will provide a common european satellite service, an important part of strengthening europe's continued competitiveness in high-tech research and industry. the report is good and focuses on how to improve this work. however, a crucial issue of the coverage of this service has become problematic from the swedish point of view, as the compromise was 60 per cent of the eu area. this means that parts of sweden will not be covered, which is negative from the point of view of technological and research development. i therefore chose to abstain.
in writing. - as the rapporteur points out, improving access for women to science is essential. how we can get there in each country, however, varies according to the culture and other characteristics of the country. the problem is different in the 27 eu member states and therefore the solutions must also vary. it is not possible to make a general statement about the situation of women in all 27 member states. the junilista is convinced that the path to real equality must be tackled at national level. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of the term 'competent authority'. they have less financial security and are much more involved in their careers. this is serious, both in principle and in terms of practical consequences. modern economies - and democracies - cannot afford to discriminate against academic leaders. that is why i voted in favour of the report. i would point out, however, that there were parts of the report which were not put to the vote and where i find it difficult to see the reasonableness. paragraph 7 states that age should be considered as a quality criterion along with family situation, including the number of persons for whom the researcher is responsible. i think that is difficult to achieve and possibly even counterproductive. there is always a risk in simplifying gender roles and talking about "qualities that tend to be more prominent in female researchers" or creating different measures of researchers' performance in general. however, i wholeheartedly support the call to introduce a non-binding target of at least 40% representation of both sexes on different types of scientific councils. i also agree with the criticism that the eu sometimes sets ambitions too low when it comes to gender equality. the politics of presence should not be underestimated - even if it cannot be made into a religion.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the interim report on the scientific facts on climate change. a high degree of commitment and determination is required to achieve the best possible outcome in addressing the political challenges of climate change. in this context, we would like to stress the importance of research being free and that a prerequisite for all research progress and development is precisely criticism and questioning. to restrict this possibility is not only a threat to research itself but also a restriction on the right of every individual to express his views. poverty is the biggest polluter and the ambition to reduce climate change is not in contradiction with growth and modernisation. a necessary condition for poor countries to prosper, and thus to have the resources and the opportunity to invest in more modern and cleaner technologies, is that they can trade freely in their goods. in view of this, any co2 duties or equivalent in relation to imports are an inappropriate solution that risks defeating their purpose.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr van hecke for a very good and important report. i once went into politics to make sure that my own country, sweden, would achieve the so-called one per cent target. the liberal people's party - my party at home - is and has always been a force for change in this area, and sweden is indeed meeting this objective. today, nearly 40 years later, i must admit, however, that the disappointments have been many. mismanagement, corruption and poor projects have unfortunately been common. but progress has also often been in the right direction. millions of people around the world have been given a better life and democracies have become more numerous. surely there is room for optimism! but if the legitimacy and support of eu citizens is to remain strong, we must improve our ability to act quickly and effectively. aid must be used in a way that ensures quality and long-term sustainability so that we can meet the millennium development goals. moreover, aid policy must be formulated more from below. the countries receiving aid must be given greater responsibility for what might be called the problem-setting privilege of aid work. the commissioner said that there was a lot of evidence that poor countries were being forced to create more expensive bureaucracies to cope with the administrative burden that is a condition for aid payments. that's not how it was supposed to go! mr president, i would like to thank you. today's debate was a foretaste of what will happen if the treaty of lisbon is adopted. increased militarisation, increased supranationality and increased costs for the eu at the expense of the independence of the member states. the eu is now representing all of us. twenty-seven wills to become one. i am therefore surprised to see how this european parliament, which claims to be the defender of democracy, chooses to go the federalist route without respecting either the irish referendum or intergovernmental cooperation. the treaty of lisbon is another step towards a common foreign minister, a common military and intelligence service, in short a common defence and foreign policy. citizens of ireland, you are the only ones who have been given the opportunity to stop this dangerous development. i urge you to vote no on 12 june, as we in parliament should do tomorrow.
i should like to begin by saying that i think the gsp, the generalised system of preferences, is a very good instrument for development, because it links trade and development in a very clear way and gives those countries which are making the effort tariff concessions and therefore access to european markets. it is also important that we use it as a development tool. now this is a technical review and it is important that we do not weaken the development dimension for those who need development aid the most, but rather strengthen it. i think in many ways this review does that very well and it takes the tool in the right direction, but it doesn't quite go all the way. i think of vietnam, for example. vietnam is very dependent on one product group, namely shoes. as you probably know, there is a threshold in the gsp system which says that you have to be over 50 per cent of the value of the exports you have to be guaranteed gsp status. right now, vietnam is just under 50 percent. this is in turn due - we should be aware of this - to us, to europe and to the fact that we have punished vietnam with tariffs on the export of shoes. nevertheless, they are still very much dependent on their shoe exports, especially poor women in vietnam. so i wonder: why can't they keep their preference? it's only 3.5%. it is not that they lose, that they get a completely duty-free entry, but they get a little lower duty. in the future, we would also like to have a free trade agreement with vietnam. so i wonder: why throw them out now when we want to take them in and take in this export through a free trade agreement? i therefore hope that you will look at this during the next regulatory period, so that no country will end up in this situation, in this limbo that i think we are placing vietnam in within the gsp system.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the regulation under discussion today is both extensive and complex, and i should like to stress a few points. first of all: the proposal that fishermen caught fishing for blackfish should repay the subsidies they have received from the eu is very good. we cannot allow the taxpayers to be forced to subsidize criminal activity. however, all types of boats are used for blackfish fishing. the proposal should therefore cover all vessels, both eu vessels and third country vessels. secondly: the proposed catch certificate is positive. the certification process plays a key role in the control of blackfish. however, i am more skeptical about maximum fines imposed jointly. member states should be able to meet their responsibilities through dissuasive charges. in conclusion, i believe that the cfp has proved to be a failure and must be reformed. to combat illegal fishing effectively, we need to cooperate internationally, both with regional fisheries organisations and with other international bodies.

on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, i would firstly like to congratulate mrs morgan on her efficient work on the report. i would just like to say one thing in response to her intervention earlier today: do not let member states run away from their responsibility to fight energy poverty and secure the well-being of their citizens; do not let any government abdicate that responsibility. having said that, i would like to emphasise what i think is the most fundamental question in this discussion, namely: are the european energy markets good enough? are they functioning well enough? is the legislation good enough regarding consumers’ well-being, low prices, industry and competitiveness, and the opportunities to make the best use of all energy sources, not least renewables? i think it is fair to say that the answer is ‘no’ if we are to respond to the challenges of climate change, to decrease energy dependency and to connect the european energy markets with each other. that is why we need reform and why it is important to get a level playing field opening up for new entrants, ensuring that we have open and fair competition. it is in this perspective that we need to look at the discussion about ownership unbundling. i think the council has a responsibility to make a compromise among its members. parliament also has a responsibility to try to contribute to the best possible solution and we have a common responsibility. i ask both the current presidency and the incoming presidency to take this question seriously. the energy markets of today are not good enough. we have a common responsibility to achieve the best possible solution and that is why i hope you will listen to parliament and we will listen to you.
the electricity market has been more or less monopolized in all the industrialized countries. economic research has shown the major socioeconomic benefits of making the electricity market a competitive market, but it is also important to break up vertical ties. electricity producers should not also manage the distribution of electricity to customers, a so-called unbundling of the electricity industry is required. few eu countries are implementing such reforms. this is the case for the nordic countries, the united kingdom, the netherlands and spain. the eu institutions have not been needed. countries do so in their own self-evident interests. opposition is strong in many important countries, such as france and germany, which are more interested in the eu state than in the prosperity of a free internal market. there is a strong incentive for the eu to pursue them. efficiency and prosperity for all europeans will increase if competition in the electricity sector covers the entire vast eu market. what is needed is political pressure on those member states which do not want competition.
thank you, mr president. efsa's job is to assess whether something is dangerous. the commission and parliament have a duty to assess whether something is in accordance with general policy principles such as good animal husbandry, not misleading the consumer, maintaining a high level of environmental protection in accordance with the precautionary principle. then this proposal is unethical and inappropriate and an undesirable method of treating chicken. in this case, moreover, the committee on veterinary affairs is almost unanimously opposed to the method. i would like to add a dimension: fraudsters have long used chlorination of chicken to make it look more pink and fresh. we are not going to give the impression that we are going to support fraudsters in favour of farmers who have invested millions, not least in my own country, to improve the situation. some labelling rules will not work, especially in restaurants, for example. turn this into a victory now, withdraw the motion and go with the support of unanimity in parliament and unanimity in the council and say to the united states: no, we don't accept your methods.
mr president, allow yourself to think: if the irish had voted ‘yes’, does anyone here believe that our green t-shirts would have had the text ‘respect the irish vote’? the truth is that they neither respect the irish, nor democracy. they only respect a ‘no’, and that is quite unique. when sweden entered the european union, there were 12 member states. it was 1995. today there are 27 member states. that is quite a change, and i think very few can question that europe has become much, much better because of this enlargement and this magnitude of development. we can say today, when we are discussing the irish referendum, that those who were saying ‘no’ all the time were wrong all the time, and we who advocated a future development of the european union have been proved to be right. so let us proceed in the same way in which we have achieved those results, with persistence, vision, democracy and respect for each member state. let us proceed with democracy in each member state with a ratification process, but also respect the decision of each member state, while also remembering persistence. we need to go further regarding the energy market, the internal market and how to achieve better results in all areas, but also to understand that the nice treaty is not enough if we are to proceed with the same development and the same visions with which we have achieved so much. so let us proceed and let us be the europe that says ‘yes’. let us remember that the ‘no’ people have achieved nothing in this europe. (applause)
i fully share the view that each member state has the right to express its views. it's a democratic right. i also believe that the lisbon treaty is in many respects better than the current treaties. this concerns the possibilities for further enlargement and greater openness, but also the rights of workers. when i visited ireland and met with trade union representatives during the campaign, there was a legitimate concern that the balance between market and social rights is not the right one today. the court of justice's judgments in the laval and rüffert cases were also cited in ireland. the two most serious consequences are: first, there is no equal treatment after the judges. those from member states with lower wage conditions have to be content with the minimum wage and not with equal pay. secondly, the right to strike was restricted in a way that is totally unacceptable. these are questions which the council and the commission must address. it is a question of balancing the social europe with the market europe. if we do not achieve this balance, the people will turn their backs on the eu. we all have a responsibility here, all three institutions, and we must do it soon.
in writing. the committee also welcomes the fact that the commission has taken the initiative to present a proposal for a regulation on the european union's financial contribution to the european union. that is why we are voting against the motion.
in writing. - the junilist is strongly critical of this report and of the compromise supported by certain political parties; a country's refugee policy is a matter of strictly national character and should be decided in accordance with a country's legal culture. the report is a major step forward in the eu's attempt to enforce a european immigration policy with a strong offensive against illegal immigrants. it calls for the arbitrary and consistent detention of people who have committed no crime but have merely crossed a national border. the report proposes to totally exclude people from european soil for five years, regardless of their situation, which means a fortress europe. the eu should be a union of values that upholds human rights and human values, but this proposal goes against that. organisations such as the unhcr and the united nations high commissioner for refugees already exist to respect the human rights of vulnerable people and to set humanly acceptable rules and standards. the junilista rejects this report and hopes that other members will do the same.
in writing. - we welcome this resolution, as we support the common commercial policy and customs legislation as a prerequisite for the functioning of the internal market. we would point out, however, that we are deeply critical of many of the trade agreements that have actually been concluded between the eu and third countries.
in writing. − chicken slaughter has not been shown to be harmful to health or otherwise, and our views should always be evidence-based. i have therefore voted against this report in the name of free trade, which i regard as being motivated by protectionism. however, given the widespread public concern in the eu about chlorine-dried chicken, it is of the utmost importance that we have legal labelling requirements so that every individual can choose to avoid this product, for example in the retail trade or in restaurants.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. - madam president! - what is it? first of all, a big thank you to jutta haug. it has taken account of several of the views of the committee on industry. for example, she has pointed out what we think is important, namely that there is a certain difference between the commission's annual policy statement and what is actually proposed in the budget in terms of, for example, climate and energy policy. last year, the debate on the budget in the committee on industry focused on two main areas, namely galileo and the european institute of technology. we can now see that implementation in these areas has started well, although we are still monitoring implementation. this year, therefore, we can concentrate on energy and climate policy instead. we have had a lot of views on this in the committee on industry. we are particularly concerned about the insufficient margin, 82 some administrative cuts. it is optional unless it means that we are making it more difficult to implement the seventh framework programme, for example, but it is not likely to be enough. just to give you an idea of the scale of the problem: the rapporteur, mr buzek, for the strategic energy technology plan, is talking about eur 2 billion per year from 2009 onwards for the so-called set plan alone. i do not know if this is realistic, but it does give the impression that energy and climate policy require substantial new resources. the seventh framework programme, like the sip innovation programme, has some money for energy and climate policy, but remember that those levels were set back in 2005, i.e. long before we had any idea of what the 2008 climate and energy policy would look like. we are also about to launch several so-called joint ventures, including the development of fuel cells and hydrogen technology. the commission is now proposing 30 million as an initial resource for this purpose. however, it is proposed that the money should be taken from the seventh framework programme's cooperation programme and its energy component. it is not a real increase, but a redistribution. we want a comprehensive picture of the whole energy and climate policy effort because it is almost impossible to get one today. this must start to be substantially re-resourced as early as 2009. we are also convinced that the energy and climate issues are the strongest argument for a substantial mid-term review of the long-term budget starting next year.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to begin by thanking the commission, commissioner vassiliou and her always helpful staff, the slovenian presidency, the council of ministers and my colleagues the rapporteurs avril doyle, mojca drčar murko and of course also the shadow rapporteurs from all the parties for a very good cooperation during the years we have been working on these issues. i am glad that we have now found a compromise which both simplifies business and strengthens consumer protection in a number of areas. for example, in the past, allergy sufferers were not even mentioned in legislation. they will if we agree to the compromise tomorrow, and then it will be easier in the future to take account of the way allergy sufferers are affected by additives. i am also proud that we have got three crucial changes in the new rules through parliament. the first change concerns so-called azo dyes. for example, they are found in soft drinks and candy, even though it has been scientifically proven that certain azo dyes can make children hyperactive. that is why it has been important for me and the environment committee to ban these substances. we have not succeeded in doing so because the member states are strongly opposed to this and to a specific labelling. despite this opposition, we have now at least been told that food containing these azo dyes must be accompanied by a clear warning. the second change concerns the environmental impact of additives. an example of the environmental impact of additives is the sweetener sucralose. sucralose has been shown to penetrate the body and not be purified in our wastewater treatment plants. if the compromise is accepted, it will now be clear that environmental considerations should be taken into account when deciding on additives. the third change is about nanotechnology. the compromise means that if an already authorised additive is modified by nanotechnology, it will be considered a new additive and will have to go through a new authorisation procedure. as regards the regulation on the common procedure, i welcome, firstly, the fact that we now have such a common procedure. secondly, it has been important to ensure that efsa is given sufficient time for its evaluations of different substances. the green group has tabled amendments to ban azo dyes, which are therefore contrary to the compromise with the council on additives. in doing so, they put at risk all the improvements mentioned above, including a specific warning label for azo dyes. i would have liked to see a ban, but it's not possible to get through to the council of ministers. i therefore intend to vote against a ban, because otherwise we risk not receiving either a ban or a warning text, which would certainly not be in the interests of the children. in conclusion, i would therefore urge all members in this house to support the compromise tomorrow and to vote against all the other amendments.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. our constituents want good, safe and secure food. do these regulations give them that? we have been able to push through some improvements on the part of parliament. what i am personally very happy about is that nanotechnology will finally be regulated and that it will no longer be possible to smuggle it onto the market without proper controls. the inclusion of the environment as one of the criteria for the approval of additives is an improvement. the inclusion of azo dyes on the labelling is an improvement. so there is something to rejoice about. the commission has also been asked to present a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. i am surprised, however, that the council is fighting against the will of its constituents. how can our governments actively counteract the fact that a text which says additives should not mislead the consumer into thinking that the food contains other ingredients than are actually present? how can they actively counteract it? ministers actively oppose the use of colouring agents as a specific reason for using colouring agents for otherwise colourless substances. the council of ministers actively opposed a ban on azo dyes. they actively opposed the inclusion of pesticides used as preservatives on the labelling to inform consumers. i believe that if we approve the green group's proposal to ban azo dyes in products for children, we will force the council to defend its position in the public debate. it is easy for the council to say such things when it is negotiating with us behind closed doors, but what government wants to stand there and approve additives which we know cause overactivity in children and which we have good reason to believe cause allergies in children? i wonder if the commission could not go straight to a ban. after all, dyes are hardly indispensable necessary additives. in addition, there are other colourants than azo dyes. if we are to fight for the precautionary principle, once the efsa has shown that there are allergic and allergic reinforcements, then we must simply ban it. we don't need to continue discussing azo dyes after that.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to thank commissioner vassiliou again for her support on the azo dyes. we really appreciate that this was one of the first questions you addressed when you took up your post. it also showed that you are ready to take up your responsibility as a commission, that you have not only to follow the recommendations of efsa but also a responsibility to make an independent evaluation of all the aspects of the supplementary legislation. you've certainly shown that you're willing to take that responsibility. i think that the debate here tonight underlines that the european parliament will of course also be following closely the efsa review of the allergenic effects of azo dyes and also the commission's position on this evaluation. we will of course also follow other studies which will be presented on the effects of azo dyes on, for example, the behaviour of children, in order to possibly return to this question. finally, i would like to say that it is one thing what the legislation looks like, but then it must also be applied. i think many consumers share my view that in fact you are often cheated in the shop today. i would like - and i think it is a legitimate request - for the member states to tighten up their supervision and, together with the commission, really ensure that the legislation we have on the table to vote on tomorrow is actually followed and complied with, so that consumers do not come home with, for example, fruit and yoghurt which they think contains a lot of fruit or berries, but which they only discover to be colourings if they read the packaging carefully.

for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. this summer could be the first arctic summer without ice. some of the world's largest glaciers are now moving across the north atlantic from the greenland ice sheet. never before have so much ice and snow melted as in recent years. i saw it with my own eyes when i visited greenland last year. even the experts on the un's climate panel are amazed at the melting that is taking place. of course, this frightening development is directly linked to our emissions and global warming. it is we in the rich world -- the united states, australia, japan and of course the european union -- who are to blame for this. that is why we must tackle all sectors that contribute to the emission of carbon dioxide and greenhouse gases. so far, the aviation industry has increased its emissions by about 100 percent since 1990. the airline industry has so far gotten away with it. aviation fuel is exempt from taxation, and municipalities and regions often subsidize airports in europe. here in the eu we fly to an ever-increasing extent. the compromise we are now about to adopt is unfortunately far from the necessary measures that we in the gue/ngl group wanted. the reduction requirements for aviation should have been higher, emissions other than co2 should have been included, the emission allowances should not have been given out for free. this is at least an agreement which is a small step in the right direction. the airline will be forced to reduce its emissions, and a small part of the emission allowances will have to be auctioned. the auction will also provide us with revenue that will be used for climate action in europe and in developing countries. in conclusion, i would like to put a question to the commission: can you promise that countries which want to take parallel measures to reduce aviation emissions, such as the uk's air passenger duty, can continue to do so?

mr president, whatever we talk about, whether environmental or security issues, climate change or european competitiveness, the energy markets and how they function in the future are crucial. this also applies to the discussion about the next phase of our legislation regarding carbon dioxide emissions: how to share the burdens and how to develop a scheme of european emissions trading. that is why i would like to turn to the presidency and stress that, when we have debated here today and voted tomorrow on the second part of the energy package, it is of the utmost importance that the presidency start the discussions between parliament and council. there must be no delays, because we need to have these markets functioning with a new legislation in place. we can ask ourselves whether the european energy markets are functioning as well as they should. the answer is very easy: they are not. there is a difference between the electricity market and the gas market. electricity is dependent on a number of different sources of energy and different producers, while gas tends to be more dependent on distributors. that is why the difference in the legislation which we are talking about – namely that we have full ownership unbundling on electricity and this scheme that the committee on industry, research and energy decided on in parliament – is acceptable. but what is important is to ensure that this is discussed and negotiated as soon as possible. it is important to make sure that the legislation on gas allows for a separation between production and distribution that also gives real strength to the third-country clause of the energy package. once again i would like to underline to the presidency that negotiations should start as soon as possible. do not delay until october. we need to start now.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the european energy market is in very serious trouble. we have an import requirement that will go up to 65 percent by 2030. we have consumers who cannot choose the kind of energy they want, and who are suffering from rising energy prices and the effects of greenhouse gases and climate change. the paparizov report under discussion today states that we need to have more cooperation between system operators and that we need to have more investment in the gas market. that's good, but it's not enough. the buzek report on research and innovation states that we are investing far too little. i would remind you that parliament wanted to increase investment in research and that we are about to enter into new budgetary negotiations. the la russa report is about breaking up the monopoly and improving the functioning of the gas market. those who oppose this view argue that breaking the monopoly between owners and distribution would lead to higher energy prices. i have to ask the following question: when we have rising energy prices and the climate effect, do we solve it through multiple monopolies? that's not true! the result of the votes in the committee gives us some of what we need, but we still have a lot of steps to take to have a functioning, environmentally friendly energy market in europe.

the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? mr trichet, please! the commission is not the european central bank is the subject of all eyes. the current uncertain economic environment with financial uncertainties is putting the ecb under great pressure. i'm convinced that last week's interest rate hike was the right decision. inflation is an abomination that unfairly redistributes wealth. europe's political leaders should be grateful to have an independent central bank ready to act to prevent europe from falling into stagflation, low growth and rising inflation. ten years after its introduction, the euro is a world currency. the security and stability that the euro has brought to the euro area and the union as a whole, including my own country, and to the global economy, is something that no one could have dreamed of. ireland's 'no' in the referendum did not give a reaction to the strength of the euro. asymmetrical economic developments among euro area countries may pose a risk, but this can be remedied by adhering to the stability pact's requirements for sound public finances and continued structural transformation in the member states. at the same time, there is a point in reviewing the ecb's work after ten years; transparency, openness, decision-making and the international role of the euro could be improved. the committee therefore proposes that the ecb should present a new proposal on how to make decision-making more clear and efficient as the eurogroup grows. the ecb should explain the discussion between the members of the governing council when deciding on the key policy rate in order to increase transparency and further emphasise predictability. the role of the president of the eurogroup should be strengthened to better reflect the importance of the euro also in international contexts. the provision of better information to the market on the ecb's interest rate decisions has long been a key issue for parliament, as has the publication of the minutes and the result of the vote. however, the ecb has refused to do so on the grounds that it would create national divisions within the ecb's management. mr trichet, we have heard your views and the committee is now putting forward an amended proposal. the ecb's management shall be more clear in the information provided after the interest rate decision, i.e. whether agreement was reached without discussion or whether there were difficulties in reaching a common position. this would be an important step towards improving the dialogue between the market, us politicians and the ecb. inflation is at record highs, now at around 4%, well above the medium-term inflation target of around 2%. not only the dollar but also other currencies have weakened considerably against the euro, which has revived the exchange rate debate. the enlargement of the euro area gives the monetary area more weight, but also brings more challenges, as decision-making becomes more cumbersome and differences in economic development between members increase. the crisis in the mortgage market has shown that financial stability is a global issue, as crises are no longer confined to one country or region. the coordinated efforts of the federal reserve and the bank of england have done much to keep the financial system afloat but have not solved the crisis. this has also highlighted the need for better cooperation between central banks and other institutions. the fact that the ecb and the fed both warned against underestimating the risk of the mortgage crisis without much success shows the increasing vulnerability of the world's financial markets. there is a need to act here, as parliament is now doing, for example, by following up the lamfalussy process to modernise the european supervisory structure. the single monetary policy and the ecb face major challenges in the coming years. i am convinced that the eu leaders and the ecb will prove themselves to be mature. but then, too, all eu leaders must understand that price stability and sound public finances are the cornerstones of growth and jobs. it is therefore remarkable that the french president, not least in his current role as president of the council, is questioning the ecb's stability objectives. in my view, europe's leaders should instead explain the aims and objectives of monetary policy in an open dialogue with the citizens. rampant prices and compensatory wage increases are the worst enemies of prosperity.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? i would like to thank you for an extremely interesting and stimulating debate. it shows that there is a great deal of support for the ideas and conclusions we draw in the report. i would also like to thank mr juncker and mr trichet for the good answers they have given. you are responding in a way that will also make you accept the views and opinions that we have expressed here. let me conclude by sharing my own experience as a politician in the somewhat smaller country of sweden up in the north. i was a member of the swedish parliament and its finance committee in the 1990s, when sweden entered the economic wall. political experience is good, my friends. you who believe that inflation and an unstable monetary policy will help the people who need our support the most are wrong. you're wrong! as a politician in the finance committee, i was able to see the swedish interest rate reaching heights that no one could have imagined at 500 percent. we had, in the 1990s, as mr juncker says, mass unemployment, rising inflation and stagflation. i have these experiences with me, and they have made me hope that my own country, sweden, will join the euro area and participate fully in european cooperation. as our colleague mr kauppi said, and as mr trichet always says, nobody thought the euro would be the success it has been. i think that shows the good cooperation that exists in europe. mr juncker, you said that the ecb should handle its determination with elegance. i think that's a good expression. i want to thank you for a good debate. i would also like to thank you for allowing me, who is outside euro-cooperation, to take responsibility for this report.
madam president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteurs. jean lambert has been working on these issues for as long as i can remember and has a great deal of experience. emine bozkurt came in a little later, but both of them have done a fantastic job and, above all, worked very well together with the shadow rapporteurs from the different parties. a little general first, because it's repeatable. this is not a question of harmonization. we know that social security systems in the eu differ. it is about the right of citizens to be able to use the internal market to look for jobs, to be able to stay elsewhere in the internal market. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we have a coordination of social security systems. it is a question of coordinating pension rights. it is about the possibility for the unemployed to take advantage of the internal market. it's about sick people who can seek treatment in other countries. this is essential for the internal market. without such coordination, the internal market would not function satisfactorily. we've had this coordination before. it's had its flaws. improvements are now being made, partly by the presence of more people, not just professionals, and partly by the inclusion of more areas of concern, such as early retirement, which we welcome. i would like to point out a few points that have been raised in our speeches. jean lambert has mentioned electronic exchange and is positive about it, because it brings many improvements. however, it is also a matter of protecting the individual when such an exchange is made. that's why we're going along with the data controller. it is important that this issue be included in the
