in writing. − tax fraud causes major economic losses for member states and reduces the ability to maintain and develop the quality of the public services we jointly finance through the tax bill. however, we abstained in the final vote because of several amendments which see tax competition between member states as a positive thing and downplay the harmful effects of tax havens on the economies of the member states. we also voted against the wording of the second part of paragraph 3, which gives a very positive picture of tax harmonization between member states.
in writing. - the fight against tax fraud is certainly worth all the support. we have therefore voted in favour of the motion for a resolution in its entirety, despite the fact that it contains several unintended and unjustified elements. paragraph 3 states that 'for a vat system based on the 'principle of origin' to work in practice, taxes between countries must be harmonised in order to avoid tax competition'. we do not want to support such a writing. the harmonization of vat and tax systems between member states is a very dangerous step away from national self-determination in one of the most fundamental areas of policy. the european parliament should not be making such a sweeping statement on such an important issue. tax competition also has advantages in that it allows countries to take the lead and develop more efficient taxes or other solutions to finance public expenditure if they are free from unthinking eu rules.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. − madam president, europe’s ability to be the leader in the field of telecoms and mobile internet is dependent on our ability to open up to new innovations and new services. from that perspective, it would be dangerous to try and defend old structures and old players. we need to open up, and that is why it is so important to use the digital dividend in a way that frees up the whole of the spectrum for new services and new opportunities, while at the same time defending old broadcasters and their services of today. the possibility of developing different services in all parts of the spectrum must be freed up. where infrastructure competition is not there already, we must ensure that there is real competition via a functional separation.

the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to thank my colleagues on the femm committee, and in particular the shadow rapporteur, for their very constructive and good contribution to the report. the presidency of the council has highlighted the importance of combating gender stereotypes. it is one of the six priority areas of the presidency. i therefore believe it is important that we in this parliament also give our views on the problems of gender stereotypes and how they affect equality. in order to avoid any misunderstanding, i would like to stress and be very clear that in this report i am definitely not proposing any new legislation in this area, either at eu or national level. the measures i propose concern national self-regulatory bodies which already exist and which represent producers, advertisers and consumers. these bodies should work to raise awareness of the importance of gender stereotyped advertising. member states should also ensure that there are bodies to which the public can address complaints. i also think that the gender equality institute should conduct research on the impact of this type of advertising on gender equality. we need to know more. why is this important? because advertising is everywhere, in our homes, in public spaces, in newspapers, in the media, and so on. of course, whether we are aware of it or not, we are affected. companies wouldn't spend billions on advertising if it didn't have an impact. advertising and marketing today are not just about trying to sell a product or a product, but about selling a whole way of life, a form of culture. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union's external relations, in particular the future of the union's external relations, and on the future of the union's external relations. i want all people to have the ability to choose without being consciously or unconsciously influenced by various gender stereotypical norms. today, it is impossible for an advertiser to advertise a product that would increase environmental problems or accelerate climate change. i have a vision that in the future it will be equally impossible to sell products that have a gender-discriminatory or gender-stereotyped message. i am also convinced that with increased awareness of the importance of advertising, consumers, both men and women, will refuse to buy products with such advertising. i am equally convinced that consumers will favour companies, producers, which have a conscious advertising policy and which do not allow gender stereotyped advertising. it will be an important success factor for companies and advertisers who demonstrate their responsibility by influencing and contributing to greater gender equality. that's the goal we all say we have. this is one factor out of many, but it's a significant factor.
the rapporteur. - thank you very much for the debate! i would like to make one thing clear - the report comes from the european united left and nothing else. as regards the amendments, i would remind you that 53 amendments from the various political groups have been adopted in committee, including four from esther herranz garcía. freedom of speech has been mentioned. of course, freedom of speech is sacred. there is nothing in the report about any new legislation, either at eu level or in the member states. this is a national issue. what we are doing in the report is emphasizing the bodies that already exist, self-regulating and inter-party bodies where producers, advertisers and consumers together find the best forms. the commission says more basic research is needed on the impact of gender equality. i agree with that and that is why i am putting it in the report. we need more research. we need to wake up the critical thinking! that is precisely what the report is about! increase awareness and thus critical thinking!
for the verts/ale group. we talk a lot about taking action on climate change, but if we had taken a holistic view of the environment, we would have copied the un legislation here as well. so now we're going to remove the acute toxic category number five. and why do we do that? yes, for the bizarre reason that otherwise the number of substances to be classified and labelled would have increased. so the reason is not some kind of environmental or health logic. no, we do not want to copy category five because the number of substances would then have increased, although the point of the whole thing is that we would have a common global standard that would facilitate both trade and, in this case, also the environment and information to consumers. i fought to get us to reintroduce acute toxic category five, but unfortunately, it didn't work out. another thing we pushed and actually succeeded in getting through with the council was to stop the proposal to reduce the classification limit to 10 kg. i want to thank the council for standing up for themselves. the commission also made positive efforts. if the bill had passed, the reach would have been undermined. reach was about examining chemicals, seeing if they were dangerous and registering them. but reach only processes and registers the 30,000 most commonly used chemicals, the ones with the highest volume. the other 70,000 chemicals we use would be dealt with by this legislation. then it's very good that we kept it and that there was no lower limit to when to classify. if the bill had passed, reach wouldn't have had his complement in the ghs. i'm very glad that it didn't happen and that's why i can support this. i am also glad that we were able to stop unnecessary categorisation of animal tests, such as eye being brought through the un system because we failed to take the first step here. what happens to consumers? they are not as well informed as they could have been about the dangers of chemicals and that is a pity. but at least they are given a decent standard and basic protection against dangerous chemicals. so in the end, it was a compromise that no one is happy with, and maybe that's how it usually is.
for the gue/ngl group. those working in the chemical industry are particularly affected. according to a finnish study, some 32 million eu citizens are exposed to carcinogenic chemicals at their workplaces every day. it is our duty to ban the most dangerous chemicals and to take control of all chemicals. that was the purpose of the reach chemical law that we passed last year. we can discuss the outcome of reach. i thought to myself that the end result was watered down and broken down by industry, but now it's there anyway, the world's most comprehensive chemical law. the ghs, the global harmonised system for the classification and labelling of chemicals, is a continuation and complement to reach. the ghs aims to bring information on chemicals to workers and consumers. the aim is to classify and label the thousands of substances and mixtures that surround us. the ghs is actually a un convention that we are now going to implement at eu level. once the ghs has been passed unfortunately not until 2010 for substances and as late as 2015 for mixtures all chemicals will be easy to identify. it is about better information for the millions of workers who come into contact with chemicals every day, for consumers who should know what they are buying. the labelling will also lead to the strengthening of public health over law and for the environment. industry can look forward to more consistent legislation that will facilitate international trade. it is a great step forward and that is why we in the gue/ngl group support this compromise. but it's a pity we didn't make it all the way. chemicals that are toxic, persistent and bioaccumulative, so-called pbts, will not be labelled. instead, this compromise means that the issue of pbt will be raised at the un level. that's a serious breach of the agreement. why not make sure that pbt chemicals have the same status as all the others and are also labelled? now we can hope that other countries will press the issue of pbt chemicals. i also believe that this will be the case in the long term. nevertheless, this is a sensible compromise which will lead to millions of europeans, workers and consumers, being better informed about the tens of thousands of chemicals that are around us. for anyone suffering from cancer, allergies or skin diseases from chemicals, this is a big and important decision!
madam president, i would like to thank you. before i started working on environmental issues, i must admit that my knowledge of chemicals was relatively limited. however, the more i have learned, the more skeptical i have become about the use of such a large amount of chemicals in society today. for example, not long ago i read that the consumption of chemicals used for cleaning in ordinary households has increased dramatically in my home country of sweden. no one knows exactly how it affects our health. what we do know is that already today, in sweden alone, several children are forced to seek emergency care every year because they have ingested household chemicals that are acutely toxic, even though the chemicals today have a warning label that says that they are just that. this warning will now, through our decision later today, unfortunately disappear from many products. i have tried, together with some colleagues, to change this and to ensure that this marking of ordinary household products should be allowed to remain. that's not gonna happen this time. i deeply regret this, because it means that important consumer information is lost. nevertheless, i welcome the fact that the eu is now getting a harmonised classification, labelling and packaging legislation to complement reach and that it has been drawn up on the basis of the voluntary international system that already exists. it makes it easier for consumers and businesses alike. after we have taken this decision today, it is of the utmost importance that all the national authorities take their responsibility to ensure that information on the new labelling system is effectively communicated not only to businesses but also to a wide range of consumer groups. i would therefore like to ask the commission and mr verheugen to follow up on how and with what success this is actually happening so that we know whether consumers in europe understand what the label means from now on.
i would just like to respond to what my colleague graham booth said here that the environmental movement wants to protect people, but not animals. i think he's got this all wrong. it's about classification. the texts referred to by graham booth state that you cannot keep mixing a chemical mixture for classification purposes only to just get below a certain limit, that you cannot do a lot of animal testing to avoid labelling. it would lead to chemical mixtures containing dangerous substances of different kinds and therefore not exactly reaching the labelling limit. in these cases, the aim would be to carry out animal testing solely to avoid labelling. that's why we need these texts. here, animal health and human health go hand in hand. animal testing is avoided, but people are properly informed about the chemicals so that they can take concrete measures to protect themselves against them. graham booth has really misunderstood this!
in writing. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide system of classification, labelling and packaging of chemicals. this can contribute to safer chemical handling, which is beneficial to the environment and health. however, we would have liked to see a labelling of chemicals in the so-called category five. these chemicals are commonly found in households and are a common cause of poisoning in children.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment. the junist position is that neither the european parliament nor any other eu institution should speak in such meetings, as foreign policy is to be conducted at national level, not by the european union. not surprisingly, the european parliament is taking the opportunity to advocate a strengthened common foreign and security policy and, even worse, the implementation of the lisbon treaty. we are already seeing how different member states have different views on the question of georgia. it is not desirable, therefore, for the eu to speak with one voice, since that voice must be able to speak against the positions of many member states. the frequent references to nato are also very problematic, since there are countries which are members of the eu but not of nato. the situation in georgia is very serious, not least in view of all the civilian casualties in the conflict. however, the eu is not to conduct foreign policy and that is why we have voted against this resolution.
in writing. - junilista believes that working time should be regulated at national level. this report should therefore not be dealt with in the european parliament, even though it is formally a commission's handling of an infringement case. the principle of subsidiarity, which is celebrated in every conceivable festive context, is fundamental here. when the majority of the european parliament goes into the details, it is the opposite, nothing can really be left in the member states. the working time directive is in itself a clear breach of the principle of subsidiarity. the structure of the economy differs between countries. some have heavy manufacturing, others have light industry, others have a lot of tourism and seasonal industries and the public sector is structured in different ways. it is therefore extremely inappropriate and unnecessary to try to regulate working hours for the whole of the eu. those who argue for this say that we will otherwise have problems of social dumping in the eu. this is a very serious accusation against the countries we have accepted as members of the eu, all of which meet the copenhagen criteria and are all states of law with the right to free association. this report is another attempt by the eu to intervene in the question of working time, which is the responsibility of the member states. we have voted against, citing the principle of subsidiarity.
in writing. - junilistan strongly rejects all forms of discrimination. the eu is a union of values and member states must treat all groups in society fairly and equally. however, the report contains a proposal which we strongly oppose, namely that the european parliament should call on the commission and the council to decide on a full transfer of competence to the community on issues such as immigration and asylum. these issues are to be dealt with by the respective member states. the report also contains many comments on how to achieve gender equality. the commission is proposing measures to combat discrimination in the labour market, information campaigns, public consultations, quotas, wage equalization, measures to combat occupational segregation in education and improvements to maternity leave for self-employed women. the report also welcomes the establishment of the european institute for gender equality and advocates that the eu institutions and member states should establish a specific international day for equal pay. gender equality must be an objective for all member states. however, the policy measures used to achieve the objectives are to be decided at national level. the international coordination that is desirable should be at global level, preferably within the un. we have therefore voted against this report.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the report on gender equality in 2008 was a good one. there were a number of important issues, not least the possibility for women (and men!) to combine work and family life and the importance of generous parental insurance. i could live with the fact that there was some hot air and repetition. what was more difficult was paragraph 4, which sought to create a legal basis at eu level for 'all forms of violence against women'. there is nothing wrong with ambition, and if it were about trafficking in human beings, which is transnational, there would be no problem. but here the call for a 'complete transfer of the political action programmes to community competence' in an area which is primarily a matter of national competence is more worrying. the reason why i finally abstained was the second sentence of paragraph 6 which calls for quotas. this is something i would not like to see at national level, and certainly not introduced as a dictate from brussels.
in writing. - the draft report contains a wide range of opinions and requests. we would like to stress, however, that the european parliament cannot solve any problems in this area and that legislation at eu level is not the right way to go either. finally, we believe that it is through the formation of public opinion and debate in the member states that advertisements and advertising of sexual services in the newspapers can be eliminated. threats of consumer boycotts can force newspapers to clean up their advertising and hotels to become porn-free. but it requires opinion to be built from below. not by action at eu level. we have voted yes to the report as a whole, after some agony, but i would like to stress that we are doing so because we consider many of the values and demands contained in it to be so urgent, but there is a difference of opinion on the way to achieve these objectives.
in writing. the report by eva-britt svensson created a lot of headaches before the vote. in its original form, the report was full of sweeping generalizations and - in my view - exaggerations. it was a mixed bag of media and advertising, codes of conduct and bills, self-censorship and new governance. the report that remained after the vote was, however, quite different. the worst excesses were gone, and a fairly reasonable problem remained that advertising is sometimes, though not always, caricatured and gender stereotyped. i do not think it is at all difficult to express concern both about the impression children and young girls make, and especially about pictures of extremely thin women. the report was not entirely free of socialist undertones, but the problem is real - not ideological. that's why i finally voted yes.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the un declaration on the millennium development goals truly deserves all the support of us rich europeans. it is both a tragedy and a scandal that so many in this world live in extreme poverty, that so many women die during pregnancy and childbirth, that so many newborns die in childbirth, that so many lack access to safe contraception and that so many are affected by hiv/aids and do not have access to stop-gap medicines. the reasons for these appalling conditions are not a lack of resources, technology, or medical knowledge. we know how to solve these problems. this is evident from the fact that many countries have long ago solved them. what is needed is to get poor countries to change their social institutions so that development in these areas is really possible. progress has also been made in some parts of poor countries, such as egypt and bangladesh. the un member states have committed themselves to these goals after careful analysis and in-depth political debate. but these are global issues and they belong at the un level. why do they appear here in the eu? global issues are to be dealt with at the global level, in the un, of which all eu countries are members. the eu is to deal with the issues that are common to its member states, i.e. cross-border issues within europe. the eu can and should do more to reduce poverty and therefore maternal mortality by abolishing its agricultural policy as soon as possible.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? trade in services has become a necessity for all economies today. it is impossible for any country to achieve economic success with an expensive and inefficient service infrastructure. i would therefore like to thank syed kamall very much for a good and important report. producers and exporters of textiles, tomatoes and other goods will not be competitive unless they have access to an efficient banking system, effective insurance companies, audit agencies, telecommunications and transport systems. the econ opinion emphasises that access to financial services such as microcredit, access to basic banking services and international bank transfers are essential for individuals in developing countries to be able to engage in economic activities and start businesses. the committee also stressed that the specific nature of the financial sector required well-balanced solutions in a globalised world. it is also something that commissioner peter mandelson himself was in favour of. if our negotiating partners, especially the developing countries, refuse to open up their services markets, their chances of economic development will be diminished. to those of you in this assembly who doubt, i say: look at how your own countries have developed! the development of trade in goods has gone hand in hand with the development of trade in services. to the boat, mr president, to all the citizens!
syed kamall calls for a far-reaching opening of the services markets of developing countries. unfortunately, this is entirely in line with the eu's current trade policy. kamall, for example, wants what is currently run by local businesses or the public sector in developing countries to be competed for by multinational european companies. this is a policy that has already been tried in europe. my home country sweden was one of the first countries to liberalise the markets for energy, electricity, trains and mail. today we see the result: higher prices, poorer maintenance, and often poorer service. a few monopoly firms can now reap unprecedented profits on what was formerly common property. is this a development model we should sell to the third world? - i know. thank god, there are countries that do things differently! norway, which is free from the eu, has withdrawn all gats requirements on the liberalisation of the services sectors of poor countries. norway has raised its voice against the laissez-faire policy pursued by the eu in the wto. it's hopeful. the eu should put the needs of poor countries first, rather than calling for deregulation and privatisation.
madam president, i would like to thank you. when the port directive was being considered in 2006, junilista voted clearly against it. the reason why we oppose the directive is that europe's ports are already functioning well and are competitive also in an international perspective. the report we are now debating contains several elements which were already included in the port directive which we voted against. the report also contains many proposals which are both worrying and undesirable. for example, it is proposed that community-declared consignments should be exempted from customs controls. this is a major problem, especially when it comes to the smuggling of drugs, for example. it should be for each member state to decide whether or not to open a container. this is a report that reeks of unnecessary regulation, hidden protectionism and high costs for the taxpayer. that's why i'm urging you all to vote against it tomorrow.
for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. i often say that the eu must be narrower but sharper, that is to say, we must focus our action while respecting subsidiarity. this strategy is exactly such an area. i would like to mention a few points that our rapporteur, frédérique ries, has dealt with in her report. her criticism of the lack of measurable targets and indicators is very heavy. we need to complete the strategy. the criticism by mr ries and several other members that there are not enough preventive measures is serious. it is directed at this material and we must take it into account in the work that follows. i would like to highlight three areas in particular: vulnerable groups, population diseases and the link between climate and health. we need to get much better at seeing the difference in treatment and medication of adults, children and women and men. it is scandalous that this is still not self-evident and has already been implemented in medical research and treatment! the work started on patient mobility in the internal market is of great importance for different groups of patients, such as neck injuries, where different standards of treatment exist in different member states. i welcome the french presidency's focus on alzheimer's, one of our major diseases, but we also need a united approach to diabetes, asthma and rheumatism, to name just a few. this also applies to occupational injuries. we are now witnessing a development in europe and the world with more pandemics and greater spread of viruses, bacteria and parasites in a way that we have not seen for a very long time. much of this, of course, is related to the climate effect. antibiotic-resistant bacteria, which cause drugs and treatments to fail, is one of our most pressing health problems. it is regrettable that the development of new antibiotics has largely stalled within the big pharmaceutical companies. i expect the presidency and the commission to tackle this very serious and major problem at once! we also need an analysis of the different scenarios for the climate effect. how would a two-degree, four-degree or even more global temperature increase affect health in europe? no such material exists today. if we are to make concrete and good decisions on the climate package, we also need solid material on the different climate scenarios we have to deal with in the future.
in writing. - advocating for human rights in our world is part of the union's political mission as a union of values. however, in the junist's view, this cannot be used to pursue foreign policy at eu level and thereby infringe on the foreign policy sovereignty of the member states. we therefore appreciate the eib's priority in lending to promote democratic development and stability in central asia, but we oppose a development which would make the eib an instrument for the eu's foreign policy endeavours. after careful consideration, we have voted in favour of the amendments proposed by the european parliament to the commission's proposal, even though some of the amendments do not quite align with this principled approach.
in writing. the situation for israel and palestine is complicated. for israel, dealing with the enormous uncertainty of its surroundings is a daunting task. as a warm friend of israel, i am well aware of this. however, it is always important to uphold international law. i therefore chose to take part in the negotiations on the european parliament's joint resolution on the situation of palestinian prisoners in israeli prisons. through these negotiations the final result was much more balanced, which led me to endorse the resolution. in my view, it is important not to condemn israel in the way that hélène flautre's report on eu sanctions in the field of human rights did, where the facts had not been studied. that is why i voted against it.
in writing. - it is absolutely appalling that so much of the world's population lives in extreme poverty, that women in these countries and regions die during pregnancy or childbirth, and that so many lack both information and access to safe contraceptives. this is a matter of human dignity and inviolable universal human rights, not least for women living in poverty. this resolution contains positive - and necessary - proposals, but also addresses issues which are not within the eu's competence. we have chosen to support proposals calling for improved conditions for women, particularly in the area of sexual and reproductive health. however, the resolution also deals with matters which are partly of a foreign policy nature. we have therefore abstained from the final vote.
in writing. - trade in services has become a necessity for all economies today. it is impossible for any country to achieve economic success with an expensive and inefficient service infrastructure. producers and exporters of textiles, tomatoes and other goods will not be competitive without access to an efficient banking system, effective insurance companies, audit agencies, and telecommunications and transport systems. but the ability to provide public services in a private way is also crucial. competition also means that we get better services in areas such as health care, schools and public transport. i therefore chose to support the idea of not making a categorical distinction between services for private and public use, because i believe that competition also in the public sector contributes to better efficiency and service. i take this for granted, whether it is our internal market or trade in services in other countries, outside the eu.
madam president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. a big thank you to mr nyrup rasmussen and mr lehne for their important reports. as many have pointed out, they could not have been presented at a more opportune time. the impact of the us mortgage market over the past year has had major implications for the entire global market. bad loans have been packaged in batches and sent on. the short-term nature and the difficulty and complexity of many of the new market instruments have led to a more difficult and fast-moving market. in addition, excessive remuneration for company directors has seriously damaged confidence - i think we can agree on that. the world's financial inspectorates have also failed to strengthen their cooperation and competence to a sufficient degree. new instruments have been introduced, and there has been a lack of openness and transparency. the starting point should now be equal treatment of all financial market participants, i.e. the introduction of stricter capital requirements and regulations, also for hedge funds and private equity. in the area of supervision, the debate has touched on the need for new authorities, many of whom - as i saw today, gordon brown - have been pushing for a common european, even a global, financial supervision. i think there is reason to reflect on this, given the still different nature of the markets. it's the american way. however, markets are globally interconnected. our committee also said that the existing committees for cooperation in financial, banking and insurance matters within europe should be substantially strengthened. there is much to be done here - and globally. achieving transparency and clearer supervision is necessary to restore confidence in the financial markets. i think there is complete agreement on this too. the market economy must have clear rules and game rules. as a liberal, i have no difficulty arguing for clearer rules and more effective legislation. however, we should beware of introducing laws and regulations that do not solve today's problems and that do not take into account the importance of a global financial market. the global availability of capital is driving growth and creating new jobs, let us not forget that. europe needs an open and efficient financial market which also allows for self-regulation and self-reorganisation. the challenge is not to lock up our systems by over-regulation so that we can neither cope with growth nor with new crises. this week, the financial world was gripped by panic. do not let this panic dictate the legislation, at least not in this house.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the financial crisis is shining a frightening light on hedge funds and private equity right now. as well as other financial innovations such as structured investment vehicles, conduits and money market funds. they've all been invented precisely to avoid the capital subscription and transparency requirements that we place on banks. there is a lot of money to be made in borrowing short-term liquidity, investing heavily in illiquid assets and investing with minimal equity, but it is a dangerous business. both liquidity and solvency can seemingly disappear quickly. hence these strict international banking regulations. now the international system outside the banking sector is collapsing. hedge funds and private equity are next. they have very little equity, they're highly leveraged. private equity contains huge amounts of leveraged buyouts, lbos, that have been conducted with almost no equity. we are not going to be able to do that. i don't think it was.
in writing. the european parliament's committee on culture and education seems to suffer from employment problems and in its eagerness to find issues to address it writes reports that the world would have done without. the report on media concentration and diversity in the eu is one such example. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment. there are many objections to such an idea, both in terms of feasibility but also, and more importantly, its possible implications for freedom of expression. the draft report has certainly been modified during the committee procedure, but it still contains a lot of unnecessary and harmful information.
in writing. – the report concerns an important subject, media diversity, which is an important element in safeguarding democracy and the right of citizens to have access to reliable and independent media. countries where economic, religious or political interests control the media have difficulties offering their citizens a variety of means of gathering information, which creates large gaps in their ability to absorb information and guarantee free and impartial elections. now we in the european parliament are voting on a report concerning these important issues. we swedish social democrats had really hoped to be able to vote for this report. but as it stands today, we will not do this. there is great purpose in the compromise proposal reached, but there remain several ambiguities, mainly around issues that affect web-blogger status. we do not want to contribute to the european parliament equating web-blogging with traditional media by questioning and proposing discussions on web-blogs status, as proposed. this is not required for other groups, so why the web-bloggers? however, we share the view, with many web-bloggers, that violations and slander are equally punishable on web-blogs as in other media. therefore, we will vote against this report.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i am very pleased to note that great progress has been made since we voted our internal market report in committee. the new scoreboard presented in july showed that member states had achieved their best ever record in transposing internal market rules into national law. the target of 1% average transposition deficit set by the heads of state or government has been achieved and we have a winner with 0% delayed transposition, bulgaria. chapeau, take off your hat! let us hope that this positive trend will continue and that our citizens will be allowed to take full advantage of our single market in the future. we must place greater emphasis on proper implementation. in our report we call on members of the european parliament and the commission to include new and refined tools and more and deeper parameters to give citizens the means to measure and recover from the losses suffered by them and the business community because member states do not always do what they are supposed to do in time or in the right way. in particular, we must develop the control and the implementation of the measures, and do so in direct connection with national implementation. this is a win-win situation for all concerned, since it avoids legal proceedings. we must find ways to avoid, in the first place, and to speed up the process of infringement, in the second place. implementation groups and networks can avoid differences in implementation and unnecessary gold plating. a well-functioning solvit and sectoral reviews by the commission can detect remaining problems at an early stage. solvit should and will become an increasingly powerful instrument. one of the major issues in the debate here in parliament has been the relationship with the consumer scoreboard. i would like to take this opportunity to stress that we should not rush into any decision just yet. as we have seen, the single market scoreboard is acting as a lever and there is still a great deal of scope for development. further, it is appropriate to let the two grow separately, each developing its own qualities. then we'll have to look more closely at the value of probably, possibly, merging them. the consumer scoreboard is still a very immature product, while the internal market responsiveness is already very important for harmonised ec legislation. despite its power in this respect, the member states are very positive about it. in other words, it is a very effective instrument which we should preserve but which we may also transfer to other areas in the future. i will also mention the areas which are the most difficult. the environmental, tax, customs, energy and transport sectors are the worst affected. here i think we should try new models and perhaps pick up the services directive model, the implementation of it, in other words very close cooperation with the commission. i recommend that. i hope that there will be more opportunities to discuss this very important subject in the future, at annual internal market forums, enhanced networking, administrative cooperation, perhaps a new internal market council in the council, i would appreciate that. a periodic update is a method that helps to develop this area. i should like to thank all those who have been involved in this matter, because i am convinced that what we have done lays the foundations for future eu cooperation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. mrs lefrançois proposes important changes to protect privacy, freedom of expression and the rule of law. the criminal offense is incitement, not incitement to commit a terrorist act. the protection of privacy will also apply to e-mail and other electronic correspondence and the fundamental principles of all our legislation will be highlighted: proportionality, necessity and non-discrimination. that's great. but, because there's a big "but": where did the subsidiarity issue go? what kind of terrorism should be dealt with at eu level? in spain, a heinous terrorist attack took place last weekend and we all know the spanish people, but this crime is not an eu issue. nor was the terrorism that had been going on for decades in northern ireland. it is clear that the fear of terrorism is being used deliberately to advance the eu's positions in the judicial and police fields at the expense of the member states. the issue of subsidiarity is alive in the party talk but not in the legislation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their excellent work which has substantially improved the commission's proposal. i have had difficulty in the past in supporting reports on terrorism, even though i seriously consider terrorism to be one of the most important tests of the credibility of our union and its capacity for solidarity and joint responsibility. with all due respect to those colleagues who come from member states suffering from the terrorism frenzy, i would say that our first responsibility is to ensure that democracy is never defended by undemocratic means. the rule of law must be upheld, as must respect for the privacy of citizens. the commission's lazy writings on the criminalisation of public solicitation, like other proposals to introduce the excuse of terrorism, are sufficiently broad and open to interpretation to risk seriously undermining the aim of the legislation, which is to achieve a common level of protection within the union. the fight against terrorism must be fought jointly, but with respect for the various legal traditions and conditions of the union and, above all, with respect for democratic traditions and values.
in writing. - we have previously criticised the different campaigns for the 'european year' for things like intercultural dialogue and for creativity and innovation. these 'european years' place a burden on the eu budget and thus on taxpayers, while having little impact on reality. if there is a demand for 'european years', they should be financed through private sponsorship and not by taxpayers. we have therefore voted against the report before us, even though it only dealt with minor amendments to the commission's proposal.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the new regulation to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment. extreme poverty and hunger, infant mortality, poor maternal health, hiv/aids, malaria and other diseases, and the lack of universal basic education for all are just some of the challenges that deserve attention and recognition from all countries of the world. in line with the junistans' position, the report notes that these challenges require international coordination in several cases. however, junilista believes that this type of cooperation should be carried out through organisations with broad international legitimacy and long experience, such as the un, and not through the eu. the junilists are opposed to the report's explicit call for direct control of bilateral aid by individual eu countries. aid is and will remain a national concern. that is why the junilista has voted against the report.
in writing. - we have voted against this report in the final vote. this is a matter of the utmost importance - so important that it must be maintained as a political responsibility of the member states or their subordinate levels. the member states shall have sole responsibility for the organisation of teaching and the content of education systems. here again we see an example of how the european parliament's committee on culture and education wants to go into an area which today does not fall within the union's responsibility but which it is desired that the union should intervene in for the good of all of us. this own-initiative report is a waste of taxpayers' money which the european parliament should not be concerned with.
in writing. − this own-initiative report by the european parliament's committee on culture and education goes beyond its usual remit and brings new ideas on how the eu should become more involved in the field of education. this is an area which is now the responsibility of the member states, and we believe that it should remain so. the report proposes, among other things, the creation of a common student id card in the eu. we find it difficult to believe that these proposals in themselves increase student mobility, but rather increase the bureaucracy surrounding student activities. the rapporteur also states in his explanatory statement that there is a need for a legal framework at eu level for students. these proposals circumvent the eu's agreed policy responsibilities at different levels for different policy areas. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. − hedge funds and private equity are examples of investment instruments that are associated with high risk. the confidence of investors, the public and, in particular, regulators requires that the activities are subject to both transparency and adequate regulation. the junilista group welcomes several of the writings and proposals for action set out in the report. we have nevertheless voted against the report as a whole. we have done so because the report advocates action at eu level, even though it should be clear to all of us by now that solutions to the potential risks associated with, among other things, hedge funds and private equity should be sought primarily at global level.
in writing. − it is regrettable that the report by rasmussen on hedge funds and private equity has been watered down following the compromise negotiations between the three largest groups in parliament. it is also regrettable that the amendments tabled by the greens and the gue/ngl group, which were taken directly from the rasmussen draft report, were not adopted during the plenary vote. for example, one of the paragraphs which has been weakened from the original draft emphasised the need for enhanced appropriate levels of transparency towards the public, investors and supervisory authorities, including, in the future, any new eu supervisory body. in spite of this, we decided to support the report in the final vote. this is due to the urgent need and necessity to counteract harmful financial speculation and instability in the markets. in this respect, the report could be seen as a step in the right direction.
in writing. the european parliament's role in the eu system is to be the voice of the citizens. this voice is strengthened not least by the work of the committee on petitions, which takes up issues raised by citizens' initiatives. a good example is the 'one seat' campaign to move the seat of the european parliament from strasbourg to brussels, which has been brought up for formal discussion by this committee. it is true that some of the calls to the member states are sometimes far-reaching and some of the proposals are not the most well thought-out. however, the work of the committee on petitions is a crucial part of the eu's work for citizens, which was crucial for me in supporting this report.
in writing. - transparency is crucial if we are to restore confidence in the financial markets. the last few months have shown the problems that a complex and fast-moving market will bring if it is not given the opportunity to understand and follow even sophisticated products. there have been problems in the otc market, but transparency must also exist in other parts of the financial market. i therefore decided to abstain on this broad issue, as the amendments which would have widened the report in a necessary way were not adopted.
in writing. − the swedish freedom of the press act represents fundamental values in swedish society. we cannot accept that laws to combat terrorism are contrary to the swedish constitution. there are many other ways and opportunities to achieve the same goal. the proposals we have adopted today do not contain any clear possibility of derogations in order to maintain our swedish legislation. we support the proposals for improvement put forward by the european parliament, but cannot support the proposal as a whole. however, since an agreement in line with the swedish constitution is clear in the council, we choose to abstain rather than vote against the report.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the commission to be given the power to adopt legislative proposals for the future. the reason for this is that we believe that the european parliament should be able to decide its own seat. we also believe that, in view of our climate and our environment, and for economic reasons, the european parliament should have its seat in brussels only.
in writing. - this report is a report on the activities of the committee on petitions of the european parliament. but since this report refers to, welcomes and expresses hope in several of its considerations that the treaty of lisbon will soon be ratified, we have chosen to vote against the report as a whole. we consider the lisbon treaty to be rejected in principle, since the citizens of a member state have voted against it in a referendum. in addition, there are still more member states where a majority of the voters would certainly have voted against the lisbon treaty if they had been given the chance. we cannot support the ignorance of the european parliament's committee on petitions in the report. as regards the question of a single seat for the european parliament, we believe that, in principle, it is up to the member states to decide jointly on the seat of the european parliament, but we also believe that it is reasonable for the european parliament to have a say on the matter.

for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the subject we are debating is of far greater importance than other subjects which are usually discussed in this house. we are talking about something as fundamental as the movement of people within the so-called schengen area. there is no doubt that this system makes travel easier for many individuals, but the downside of the system must be said to outweigh the positive aspects. i am referring to the fact that schengen also leads to the restriction of the mobility of a huge number of people by social systems. schengen is another step towards creating a superstate, a fortress europe. the creation of a control society of enormous proportions. i don't want any part of this. there is certainly no doubt that crossborder crime is one of the biggest problems we face today. cross-border solutions are therefore needed. however, i do not think that schengen, or the eu for that matter, is the right forum for this purpose. today, interpol already exists, a remarkable and well-functioning international police agency in which sovereign states from all over the world participate. instead of building parallel systems, more should be done to strengthen interpol. as we know, crime is not confined to our continent but is made up of worldwide networks. these were some general comments, now to the more specific ones. one aspect which i think is being treated far too lightly in the schengen information system is the issue of privacy. the personal data to be processed and stored are of a very sensitive nature. one of the most important tasks of a state is to be able to provide its citizens with full protection against unauthorised access to personal data. i therefore regard this as a national concern because it is my firm opinion that the eu cannot under any circumstances provide the necessary protection of personal data. i also believe that it is unnecessary and costly to build new structures. after all, it is tax money that is going to finance this system. i have long held the view that the development of the european union, or integration as some people choose to call it, can be likened to the tyranny of small steps. scary as it may seem, the steps aren't so small anymore. instead, it is a matter of taking large, determined and gradual steps towards the creation of a european union state. no true european should accept this.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the destruction of tropical forests continues unabated. 13 million acres of forest each year, or a football field every two seconds, that's how much forest is cleared every year in the world. this is despite the fact that the first hourly agreement was concluded 20 years ago. in 2006, the international tropical timber agreement was signed. although the agreement itself is comprehensive and leaves much to be desired, it is at least a tool we have to deal with the problem. caroline lucas has taken this up and with her much needed amendments we will have better opportunities to protect tropical forests. i have a question directly to the commission. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment is a very important one. when will this legislation on illegal logging come? is it true that the commission voted on this in may of this year? then why haven't we seen anything? you did not mention that in your speech, so we in parliament want to know. what about the bill? tell us about it. otherwise, i'd like to thank lucas for also problematizing the whole idea of trading in timber. is it really reasonable to cut down so much forest and then export it? my own country sweden is the most forested country in the eu. at the same time, we import one sixth of all the timber we consume. why would you do that? well, of course, because it's very cheap to buy timber on the world market. lucas wants to do something about this and calls for the eu to support countries that adopt strategies to protect their tropical forests. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial policy' to cover all aspects of the common agricultural policy. it is in this way that international trade should be used as a tool to promote sustainability and development worldwide. another dimension of deforestation that is under-debated is the meat industry. much of the meat and feed consumed in the world comes from former forest land. meat production is one of the main causes of the deforestation of the amazon. the chairman of the un's climate panel, rajendra pachauri, recently called for a reduction in meat consumption. he is quite right, so here is another question for the commission: when will there be a strategy for reducing meat consumption? as i said earlier, lucas is also right on most of the points in this report. that is why the left group supports this excellent report.

for the gue/ngl group. one third of europeans are overweight or obese. a considerable part of the health budgets of the member states must be devoted to combating the problems of overweight. in addition, the problem of overweight is socially and economically unfair. low-income people are hit hardest when they consume more sugar and saturated fats. worse food, simply put. it is, of course, the task of policy to create the best possible conditions for people to eat more healthily. that is precisely what the rapporteur, mr foglietta, is doing in this report. that's why he has the support of the left. the report calls for the eu to be more flexible in allowing member states to have lower vat rates for healthy food and higher rates for what we should be eating less of. this is an important demand which i hope will be heeded by the eu leadership. what does the commission have to say about this? can the commission come back with a revision of the eu vat directive to allow more flexibility for member states to have lower vat on healthy food, for example? another question for the commission concerns trans fats. we know that trans fats are bad for your health. this has been confirmed by the authorities of the member states, as well as by the eu's own food safety authority, efsa. commissioner androulla vassiliou said in the committee on the environment on 1 april this year during a hearing, and i quote: trans fat is definitely not good for health, there is no doubt about that. in this report we call for a ban on trans fats, but the commission refuses to propose this. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. the commission now has the chance to withdraw from this unhealthy position. when are we going to ban trans fats? can the commission at least guarantee that member states are free to ban trans fats if they so wish? meat is another dimension of the public health problem, as the previous speaker, mr buitenweg, pointed out. meat consumption is increasing like an avalanche around the world. if nothing is done now, warns the food and agriculture organization of the united nations, meat consumption, already high, will double by 2050. meat contains saturated fats and contributes to obesity. in addition, the meat industry is greatly exacerbating climate change. the eu should phase out subsidies to the meat industry, but in the 2007 budget alone, over €45 million was earmarked for pure marketing money for the meat industry. it is counterproductive and, moreover, a bizarre waste of taxpayers' money. a phasing out of these meat subsidies and a strategy to reduce meat consumption should be self-evident measures for better health in the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as many have said, this is an important issue that we are discussing here today. it is a very important issue where we can learn from each other, from the member states, but it is also very important that we respect the principle of subsidiarity when we discuss this issue. there is much that the eu can do to reduce the problem of obesity and that we should focus on here in parliament. advertising and consumer information are areas which we actually decide about here in parliament and which should be our focus. i think we have failed partly there. for example, we have failed to prohibit advertising directed at children, a group who cannot distinguish between advertising and the facts presented to them and who are therefore, by definition, misleading to these very consumers. much of the advertising directed at children is about foods that are high in fat, salt, or sugar. a ban on advertising to children would be effective in reducing the obesity problem in europe. the second thing i wanted to talk about is trans fats. i am very pleased that tomorrow we may be able to call on the commission to propose a ban on trans fats. the most common argument against such a ban is that saturated fats are actually the biggest public health problem in europe. so it's by definition, but why not reason as they do in denmark? now, if we have a huge problem with saturated fats, why do we have to have an extra problem with trans fats, too? i don't understand that logic. you can't get rid of all the saturated fats, but you can actually get rid of this industrialized production of trans fats, which is just a cheap and bad way to cook. i am also very pleased that we are taking up this issue of glutamate in the report. finally, i would like to say that subsidiarity is important. we could have dismissed a lot of the opinions about what different schools should do and serve. i think there are better political levels for taking such decisions than here in the european parliament.
