president of the council. - mr president, madam commissioner, madam vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, i am aware of the attachment you, and in particular you, mr president, have to transparency issues, and the presidency shares this. it is very important that our citizens can understand how european decisions affecting them are taken - this is the case for european legislation - and we must, of course, make progress in this area. your question is welcome because it provides an update on the difficult issue of public access to legal advice. this is the subject of the judgment, but the whole of the transparency policy must be assessed in the light of this judgment. the turco judgment is important because it is the first time that the court of justice has ruled on the specific cases of legal opinions and the conditions for their public access. in its judgment, the court stressed the importance of transparency and openness in the legislative process in order to allow citizens to participate more fully in the decision-making process. it also concludes that regulation no 1049/2007, which provides a framework for public access to documents, imposes in principle an obligation to disclose the opinions of the institution's legal service relating to the legislative process. in answer to your question, mr cappato, the council has taken the necessary steps to implement this court judgment, and has done so since the first days of july 2008. the council has complied with the judgment and has made public the document requested by mr turco. the council then decided to adapt its practice to the court's judgment, taking account of this principle as laid down in the judgment. it is true that there are exceptions, but the court itself defined them in this judgment, in respect of opinions which are particularly sensitive or, on the contrary, of a particularly wide scope. in any case, any refusal by the council, as you know, must be substantiated in detail. the council received a number of specific requests for legal opinions from the council's legal service and applied the principles of the court. however, you are aware that the public disclosure of legal opinions internal to an institution may affect the legitimate interest of the institutions to seek and receive objective and independent opinions. we have to find a balance between the two. this is why the legislator explicitly wished to protect the confidentiality of legal opinions, and this is a requirement that remains present in our opinion. beyond these very technical replies - i agree with them and i apologize for them - but which are official since i have made them on behalf of the presidency, i have thus the opportunity to give an update on the transparency policy. several aspects of this policy of transparency must be distinguished. first, as regards direct access to working documents - that is the subject of your question - i confirm that the council is fully applying the provisions of article 12 of regulation 1049/2001 and annex ii to the council's rules of procedure, which requires the keeping of a public register and specifies the modalities for direct public access to council documents. i do not want to bore you with too much technicality or numbers, but the fact is that the figures are illuminating, since the public access register contains references to over a million documents established since 1999. you'll say, "too much is the enemy of good"... secondly, it seems to me that the important thing is the legibility and the publicity of the decisions taken by the institutions. this is what mrs wallström is doing in particular in the legislative procedure, and the interinstitutional agreement on better lawmaking contains general commitments on transparency. measures have already been taken to implement this agreement. all council deliberations under the codecision procedure are open to the public and the council regularly holds public debates on important issues affecting the interests of the union and its citizens. in order to improve public access to the deliberations, there is also a council website which has created accessible sections so that you can watch the video streams of the deliberations which, you will admit, is still an important and particularly exciting point! the council presidencies also have a role to play. following the example of the websites of previous presidencies, the french presidency has invested heavily in its multilingual website ue2008.fr, which includes a 'web tv' with several channels, similar to the one that the european parliament has fortunately launched. third and last, we must be able to respond to requests for information from the public. to do this, a public information service has also been set up - and this seems to me to be the most important point - because where we are least equipped, it is most certainly in the practical information we have to give to the public. and that's why citizens are legitimately concerned and feel that it's too opaque. this is because we either have insufficient resources or information sites that are not sufficiently coordinated and do not allow the public to be aware of all the legislation. and it is on this last point that the council must concentrate its efforts. these are the elements i wanted to share with you. transparency is absolutely essential. we have reached an important agreement with the commission and the european parliament, also in terms of communication, to increase transparency, and i believe that the interinstitutional negotiations we have had with mrs wallström and the vice-president of the european parliament enable us to look to the future under better conditions. it is a collective task and we must now give it its full meaning. i should like to point out that we have a real political will to make progress in this area of transparency, in the information and communication policy, especially in the practical information which, in the case of legislation, must be given to the citizens. we must therefore make greater use of the new information technologies. but i know that this concern is shared by parliament, the commission and the council.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank the honourable members very much for their questions. of course, as we have already heard, this turco judgement by the court of justice is a very important one. the commission fully agrees with the court when it recalls the great importance of an open legislative process. needless to say, we will respect this judgement and take it fully into account in our daily work. i want to be as clear as possible, but i will have to be rather brief in my replies to your five specific questions. the first one concerns better lawmaking, our interinstitutional relations and regulation 1049/2001. this turco judgement concerns access to documents by the public. there is no direct link to our interinstitutional cooperation. for that we have our framework for enhanced interinstitutional cooperation, which i believe works very well. our two institutions have a good track record of working together in the legislative process to meet the better lawmaking objective. regarding your second question on accessible information on current public consultations, i would like to start by mentioning the prelex database, as we call it in our jargon. the aim behind this database is to facilitate access to pre-legislative documents through a single entry point. in practice, it is a portal containing links to key pre-legislative documents. this database is operated by the official publications office and it is based on information supplied by the commission. more importantly, it is directly accessible to the public on the europa server. regarding public consultations launched by the commission, there is also a single access point on the europa server. that is your voice in europe. this website facilitates access to consultations and provides general information on the commission’s different consultation processes. it also gives comprehensive information on open public consultations and on related consultation documents and questionnaires. in addition, this access point also gives information on the follow-up, such as consultation reports and published contributions. regarding your third question about the trans-jai project, i would simply like to assure you that full public access with dedicated servers – entitled ‘public go live’ – for the trans-jai web portal is planned for march 2010. this leads to your fourth question on the transparency principle and the principle of good administration. of course these principles are closely interlinked. we are always committed to providing as much information as possible to the public. this is particularly the case for procedures which concern citizens and their rights and for the workings of the institutions, which we all know are sometimes not so easy to understand. the commission’s website gives information on its organisation and procedures, and we have an easy-access ‘who is who’ directory of the commission staff and the different directorates-general. your last question, number 5, concentrates on the public register of documents and the ombudsman’s draft recommendation in the ‘statewatch’ complaint. a public register of documents has been up and running since 3 june 2002, as required by regulation 1049/2001. since then, the commission has also put in place a dedicated register for comitology procedures and a register on expert groups. we are doing our utmost to modernise our internal it systems but, as you know, unfortunately these things do not happen overnight. but one thing is clear. this is ongoing work. we always take into account the need to increase the coverage of this public register. more specifically, on the ombudsman’s draft recommendation in this case, the commission has of course submitted a detailed opinion to him. in that opinion we acknowledge that we still have to increase the coverage of our public registers and we confirm our commitment to further develop our public registers in the interests of enhanced transparency. on one point we were unable to share the ombudsman’s view. he concluded that the commission should – and i quote – ‘include references to all the documents within the meaning of article 3(a) in the register’. i can share the aim and the ambition of this conclusion, but unfortunately it is impossible to achieve. it is simply impossible to reconcile the wide and imprecise definition of ‘documents’ in article 3(a) of regulation 1049/2001 with one single, fully comprehensive public register. instead we have to provide the links or make different entry points. i could, of course, mention that i have made the register of my own correspondence directly accessible on the web, and i could mention other examples of what can be done proactively outside the formal legislation, such as improved registers, greater user-friendliness and accessibility, active dissemination and quicker publishing of documents. but all this is not our topic for discussion today. i am sure we will have other opportunities to discuss these important issues in depth.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, a precondition for the credibility and legitimacy of the eu is a set of working rules on transparency and the protection of data and information. transparency in the decision-making process is especially needed when european democracy is questioned. member states have different experiences. more exchange of experience can lead to better administration in the eu, but also in the member states. we have come very far since 2001 when the eu legislation on transparency was decided. most member states already have some kind of freedom of information act – sweden and finland since 1776, other countries like ireland since a few years ago. it takes time – you have to respect that – to find common behaviour and attitudes. one national formula cannot be applied over the whole eu. the cultures are too diverse. open government is essential in a representative democracy. the whole process is influenced by the fact that the digital revolution is transforming our society into an information society. one important point that has to be better developed in regulation no 1049 is the balance between confidentiality and transparency. we need an open decision-making process respecting the overriding public interest without infringing institutions’ or the individual’s rights to confidentiality. judicial interpretation of complex matters, for example competition cases, cannot be left to the judgement of tabloids. the turco judgment can contribute to the development of better lawmaking. which practical consequences will the commission and council draw from the turco case?
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the eu system, the court of justice is at the centre and has the power to interpret political decisions. in interpreting eu law, the court of justice's ruling is the final authority, regardless of the legislator's intention and intentions with the legislation. we saw it in the laval, rüffert and others cases. in these cases the court ruled against the workers. in turco, on the other hand, the court ruled in favour of the applicant. i welcome that, but my basic criticism remains that the court of justice decides eu policy and that in any dispute the court of justice always has the final say. in turco i welcome the court's recognition of the primacy of citizen control over the legislative process. it is a step in the right direction, but i regret to say that there are still many steps to be taken before the eu leaves its closed way of working, where only those on the inside can find their way forward. this is a matter of democracy and participation and openness. it is important to take note of the turco case in the context of the current review of the so-called transparency regulation.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i am a staunch eu sceptic, but there are two areas where the eu should have a central international role: trade policy and environmental policy. now we're talking about trade policy. free world trade is the key to economic prosperity for all the countries of the world and especially for the poorest. the setback at the doha round this summer was therefore profoundly serious and it is now up to the eu, the world's largest trading organisation, to take a new initiative. it is therefore deeply unfortunate that peter mandelson, our most competent commissioner, is leaving his post as head of the eu's trade policy. at the same time, the world economy is threatened by a devastating financial crisis. at this stage, the uk government proposes a replacement who is clearly incompetent for the task. it is now the responsibility of the european parliament to ensure that we have a strong and competent trade commissioner at this dangerous historical juncture. let's take that responsibility!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i was going to talk about two things. the first is how we are going to deal with the over-prescription of antibiotics, which is very well dealt with in the report. it is only to go to ourselves here in the european parliament or i need only go to myself. i don't think i'd be standing here today if we didn't have effective antibiotics. i have been ill a number of times, and as i'm sure many of you in this chamber have been saved by antibiotics. sadly, our children's future access to such help is threatened by our allowing doctors to prescribe antibiotics when they are not needed and even to sell antibiotics without a prescription. we need policies and incentives that discourage doctors from going to patients to make money, so that they don't prescribe antibiotics when they don't need to. i think the eu has a very important role to play here in disseminating ways of working successfully against this excessive prescription. the second issue i wanted to raise is health inequalities, which have also been raised by several previous speakers. there is another aspect to health inequality. it's not just about inequality between countries. it's also about inequality within countries. the who has had a special commission that has looked at class differences in health. it is plainly stated that 'social justice is a matter of life and death' and that this is something we must talk about openly in the union. the fact that there are such great differences within countries is completely unacceptable. there are good examples of success in addressing class differences in health, but there are also very bad examples. i am very concerned about what is happening in my own country's capital. suddenly, a system has been introduced that moves doctors from the socially deprived and poor areas where medical care is most needed to areas where people are healthiest and most well-off. i think the eu should collect statistics and knowledge so that voters, and also those who make decisions about health care, can be clearly informed about which measures lead to greater social equality in health and which do not.
in writing. - we have no objection to informing and educating the citizens of the member states about eu cooperation in an objective and factual manner. but it must not be propaganda for the introduction of even more supranational government in the union. the joint declaration on communication in europe to which this report refers also mentions several different eu programmes, such as 'plan d' and 'citizens for europe', which junilista strongly criticises. the declaration also praises eu parties, and political foundations linked to them, which the junilists believe must be built from below by the national parties of the member states and not from above by the eu bureaucracy. we have therefore voted against this report. we believe that the joint declaration on communication on europe should have been written in a different way, with an emphasis on factual information and education and an open debate in which political forces who want intergovernmental cooperation in europe, not a united states of europe, can also have a say.
in writing. - we, the swedish social democrats, have voted in favour of the report on combating undeclared work (a6-0365/2008). the report contains many important texts, including those on the liability of main contractors for irregularities in subcontracting. the report also addresses the recent interpretation of the posting of workers directive by the court of justice. the report is therefore good on the whole, but we would like to make it clear that we consider tax policy and, above all, the choice of tax levels to be a matter for the member states. however, there was no opportunity to vote specifically on these parts of the report.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of workers against discrimination on grounds of sex, age, disability, age, age of majority, age of the workforce and age of the population. black labour is just one of several serious problems for which the social partners must find solutions. the panzeri report is rich in well-meaning and in some cases far-reaching advice on how the individual member states should organise their respective labour markets in general and tackle the problem of undeclared work in particular. the rapporteur calls on the member states to continue reforming their tax and social security systems and to take joint action to combat undeclared work. the rapporteur also proposes a common approach to immigration in the eu and that 'reforms of economic policies, taxation and social security systems in all member states should be integrated and take account of the main causes of undeclared work'. junilista believes that labour market policy is a national concern and we therefore see with great concern how the eu is trying to increase its influence in these matters at the expense of the self-determination of the individual member states. for the reasons mentioned above, the junilista has decided to vote against the report.
in writing. - we believe that effective measures must be taken to stop undeclared work. however, we cannot accept that a common immigration policy, for example, should be part of the solution to the problems of undeclared work. that is why we abstained in the final vote.
in writing. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. we would, however, like to clarify our position on the minimum wage points. we share the view that the number of working poor in europe must be reduced. all workers in europe must be guaranteed a sustainable income. member states which wish to solve this by means of statutory minimum wages are free to do so. it is equally clear that member states which have transferred pay to the social partners should be able to continue to do so. comparisons between different labour market models and their ability to guarantee workers fair wages are provided in the lisbon strategy. it is important that such an exchange of experience takes place between member states.
in writing. - we have voted against the report on promoting social inclusion and combating poverty in the eu because several of the proposals concern areas which should be dealt with at national level. the content of health care is primarily a national responsibility, as is the actual content of labour market policy. these two areas are best addressed at national level, closer to the citizens. measures such as minimum wages also run counter to the aim of the report by creating more obstacles to the fight against social exclusion.
in the case of the commission, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the commission's proposals are implemented. we need sensible comments on the importance of a reasonable tax burden, with strange statements on the importance of the citizen's wage and the minimum wage set by the eu. whatever one's view of the matter - and in the latter two cases my view is strongly negative - it can be stated that these are not areas where the eu has, or should have, competence. since neither the political substance nor the political level was in line with my fundamental convictions as a liberal and a people's party, i therefore voted against, even though the report also contained some sensible remarks.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the definition of the term 'competition'. we have previously voted in favour of establishing cooperation agreements with ukraine (brok report a6-0023/2004). the zaleski report is based on a commission proposal which aims only at partial renewal of the cooperation agreement with ukraine following its accession to the wto. nevertheless, the report is characterised by interventionist ambitions and far-reaching proposals for how the eu should steer developments in a number of policy areas in ukraine, including energy policy, fiscal policy, telecommunications, water, tourism and education. as junilist is strongly opposed to the eu's foreign policy ambitions, we have voted against the report.
in writing. - junilista believes that the eu is a union of values and should therefore play a role in promoting democracy and human rights in its neighbourhood. you belong to belarus. the parliamentary elections in belarus in september did not appear to meet international standards, as was also noted by international observers. the lack of transparency and democratic elections is very regrettable, both for belarus and for the region and europe. however, the resolution contains several lines of writing that should not be there. the european parliament called on belarus to abolish the early voting procedure. it may be true that part of the electoral fraud is carried out by this type of voting, but we still believe that it is up to each sovereign state to decide how elections are to be conducted, as long as they are democratic. furthermore, there are also views on visas, which we consider to be a national issue. it is up to each sovereign state to decide who will be allowed to stay on its territory. despite the objections we have raised above, we voted in favour of the resolution because we believe it is important to stress that belarus must become democratic.
in writing. - the junilist accepts the eu's central role in trade policy. good relations and a developed world trade are essential to achieving economic prosperity for all the countries of the world, not least the poorest. the junilista therefore supports several of the texts of the resolution. the call for the industrialized and advanced developing countries to offer the least developed countries 100% duty-free and quota-free market access to, inter alia, the single market is welcomed. we also welcome the proposal to reform the wto in order to increase its efficiency, transparency and democratic legitimacy once the doha round is concluded. however, we regret that the resolution contains references to the treaty of lisbon. the entry into force of the proposed treaty should be considered highly uncertain, not least because the irish people voted against the treaty earlier this year. continued references to the lisbon treaty therefore signal a lack of acceptance of the rules of the democratic system. the resolution's texts are, however, broadly based on a sound understanding of the importance of world trade for continued development and prosperity. the junilista has therefore voted in favour of the resolution as a whole.
in writing. - the swedish social democrats have voted against the report by carlos carnero gonzález on the use of the union's symbols by parliament (a6-0347/2008). these symbols already exist and are used today and do not need to be regulated in this way.
in writing. - we can see that there are desperate attempts to construct a european nation with its own national identity. experience shows that it is not possible to create a common identity artificially. there are a number of examples of this, including the state of belgium, which was created almost 180 years ago by the merger of flanders and wallonia. despite having a common royal house, flag, anthem and currency, belgium is falling apart. eu flags, national anthems and europe days may seem to be insignificant symbols. however, it is part of the european parliament's overall ambition, namely to build a united states of europe. the original eu constitution, which was rejected in referendums in france and the netherlands, contained references to the eu's national anthem and flag, among other things. this was overturned and eu leaders presented a 'new' treaty, the so-called lisbon treaty. the european parliament is now seeking revenge by introducing the use of eu symbols behind the scenes. we believe that european cooperation is in danger of suffering the same fate as the esperanto project, the artificial language which never became a world language but has largely died out. identity and cohesion are created by the people, not by the elite. we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. - i have voted against the report by mr carlos carnero gonzález on the use of the symbols of the union by parliament. the reason for this is that i do not think that the use of symbols needs to be written into any treaty or any rules of procedure. they're there anyway.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'water' in the context of the directive on the protection of consumers. however, we believe that information and training campaigns should be initiated primarily at local and regional level and not at the request of the eu.
in writing. − we share the concern of the resolution about the effects of climate change on the sustainability of the communities that inhabit the arctic area as well as the effects on the natural habitat, and we recognise the significance of the arctic for the global climate and therefore the arctic as a common concern. we cannot, however, support this resolution as its point of departure is first and foremost the interests of the eu. we believe that a policy which is not firmly rooted in the interests of the peoples of the arctic area is illegitimate. we believe that the starting point for all talks and initiatives concerning the arctic area must be the respect of the sovereign territory of the arctic nations and equally the inclusion of all the peoples of the arctic area as equal partners. finally, during the vote a majority rejected an amendment which stressed the need for the arctic to become a demilitarised and nuclear-free zone, thereby clearing the path for the arctic policy that this resolution calls for to include militarisation of the arctic and placement of nuclear weapons in the area. we cannot support this under any circumstances.

in writing. the legislation which alejandro cercas is dealing with is not without controversy. i think everyone agrees that it is in the interest of both truck drivers and the public that large, heavy vehicles are not driven by people who suffer from severe sleep deprivation. of course, rules are necessary in this area. at the same time, it must be clear that the truck strike that broke out on the öresund bridge in november 2007 was not an accident. the working time regulations that the eu has developed in many occupational areas have been widely criticised, both by employers and workers. it would therefore perhaps be more appropriate to ask the commission to carry out an impact assessment of the way the legislation has worked and been received, rather than to call for even stricter implementation. this type of legislation requires considerable flexibility to be able to operate in, for example, both romania and denmark. that's why i put my vote down.
for the pse group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to begin by thanking evelyne gebhardt for her good work, but i regret that we were unable to reach an agreement with sweden and myself. that is why i could not vote for the report in committee and i cannot vote for it in plenary. for me, as a swedish social democrat, this proposal is a step backwards for equality between women and men. therefore, these issues should continue to be resolved at national level. i think it would be enough to grant divorce only when the parties are in complete agreement. the weaker party, usually the woman, can now be forced by the man to make a decision, either by being first or by using force. the courts may thus be forced to apply laws that we strongly criticize, laws that are offensive and have an outdated and old-fashioned view of women, marriage and divorce. i'm more concerned with gender equality and women's safety than quick divorce verdicts. i will therefore continue to work and vote against this proposal until we find another solution.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. the aim of the proposal is to enable citizens in the event of divorce to exercise their rights and obtain the information they need, but the right to information and knowledge is not dependent on a common set of rules. a common regulatory framework does not in itself create more knowledge or make citizens better informed. in my home country, sweden, if you do not have small children in common, the only requirement for divorce is to file a notice. there are also examples of other countries within the eu where divorce is completely banned. this, if anything, illustrates the need for national legislation in this area! the lisbon treaty does indeed make some aspects of civil and family law supranational, but we do not have a lisbon treaty today. i question the commission's proposals in an area which is still a matter for national competence. my group will not vote for the motion. i also thought i had a very good argument from my social democrat colleague inger segelström.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking roberta angelilli for a very good report in which she followed up on the decisions taken by the children's strategy. last week i presented a report on young people and the media. it's a big study of young people's media habits, and we're also taking into account the views of their parents. the new thing is that a majority of young people in sweden have their own computer in their room and that the tv has been thrown out. there is a big difference between girls and boys in the way they use the internet. the girls chat, socialize, and text, while the boys play games. the girls are being approached for sexual contact. fewer girls are exposed to pornography sites than in the past, but they are very negative about online pornography. very few people meet strangers from the internet in real life. young people today have a healthy view of the media, but the high consumption is worrying both parents and us politicians. that group needs to be included in community programmes in future. we've defined a high consumer as someone who uses a media device for more than three hours a day. this is the case for about 8-9 percent of the total youth population. but in sweden today, 96 percent of all young people have a mobile phone: the same is true of 70 percent of the younger children. so the pace of development is at record speed. our job as members of parliament is to fight the avars.
rapporteur. − mr president, this report is a response to the global climate change alliance, which was launched by the european commission towards the end of last year. basically, the global climate change alliance is a very good initiative. it is, first and foremost, a recognition that low-income countries will be seriously at risk because of climate change. it is an irony that, only eight years ago, the millennium development goals were agreed upon in new york. there was hardly any mention of climate change, yet it was obvious – already then – that many low-income countries would suffer badly from the adverse effects of climate change. the way we are organised, however, in national organisations, in governments etc. – climate change on its track, development cooperation on another track – was already a real impediment or hindrance towards fully recognising the threat of climate change to development and poverty reduction. the challenge is, of course, great. in order to assist low-income countries in adaptation and risk reduction and in mitigation efforts, and to look for synergies between the two, there must be a special focus on deforestation and then, finally and most importantly, implementing all these concerns in the context of development planning and poverty reduction. it is crucial that we do not end up with a series of stand-alone adaptation projects. rather, we have to mainstream adaptation and risk reduction into development cooperation. the big question when we discussed this in the committee on development was how to finance this. the commission proposal entails only eur 60 million. it is a drop in the ocean. nobody knows how much adaptation risk reduction will cost; nobody knows what technological cooperation will cost in terms of mitigation. the world bank, oxfam, undp and others have come up with estimates ranging between usd 10 billion and, i would say, usd 100 billion yearly. some measures need not cost more: if you do development planning and poverty reduction strategies taking into account the adverse effects of climate change at the beginning, you may not end up with increased costs. but in many areas we know that there will be additional costs. farming practices, risk reduction for extreme weather events, sea level rise, health measures: you name it. the question is: where will the extra or additional funding come from? in the report we make a few suggestions. one of them, quite naturally, is to use some of the revenue from the expected auctioning of emission permits in the future. it is very important to somehow earmark funding for developing countries in this context. another suggestion is that member states should support the commission initiative and not embark on their own initiatives. this is a new area, where it makes sense to pool resources. finally, all that we do in this area must be seen in the context of next year’s climate negotiations in copenhagen. proactive action from annex 1 countries – in particular the eu – in this field is crucial for a global deal to be struck. the development report deals with the issues mentioned – and many others – in the spirit of supporting the european commission initiative and with the primary aim of strengthening it, both in terms of substance and finance.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? i hope the commission will be here soon. they haven't arrived yet. i shall begin by speaking in general terms about the report. in the european parliament we have on several occasions discussed the policy to be pursued in a globalised society. we're not going to compete for low-wage jobs, we're going to have good working conditions, we're going to invest in human capital, in people and investment and so on to be successful. we have also discussed many times the balance between open borders and a strong social europe and have concluded that this balance is important. we have also often discussed the issue and importance of equal treatment of people regardless of gender, ethnic origin and nationality and have stated that equal treatment and non-discrimination should prevail. the report is about having open borders. the committee is in favour of open borders without restrictions and transitional periods, but at the same time we want a social europe in which we do not compete with each other by forcing lower wages, worse working conditions and the like. the report also deals with the principle of equal treatment, i.e. equal treatment and non-discrimination of employees, irrespective of nationality. it must not be the case that a latvian, a pole, a german, a swede or a dane is treated differently in the same labour market. that is also the background to the proposals in the report. the main proposals concern the posting of workers directive, as three of the judgments concern posted workers. it is very important that we do not make the posting directive a minimum directive. it is true that the directive contains 10 minimum conditions which must be met. these conditions must be met, but the basic principle is equal treatment. that's why we need to be clear. equal treatment shall apply irrespective of nationality. in a labour market, such as that of the german state of lower saxony, the conditions which apply there must be applied to all workers, regardless of their origin. this is an important principle which must become even clearer after the judgments that have been handed down. the second is that we have different labour market models. all these models must be equally valuable in terms of implementation. some other things in the directive also need to be changed. we must also make it absolutely clear that the right to strike is a fundamental and constitutionally protected right which cannot be subordinated to freedom of movement. this is true of the new treaty, but also of other aspects of primary law. thirdly, ec law must not violate the ilo convention. the rüffert case concerns an ilo convention on public procurement. in that case, the working conditions applicable at the place of employment shall apply. that is the reason for the proposals that have been put forward. i will listen to the debate and at the same time i would like to thank everyone involved, including the shadow rapporteur, for the good cooperation we have had. (applause) i would like to thank you.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to emphasize what is in the report and what is not in the report. i would like to thank the rapporteur. he has been sympathetic to the differences of opinion in the committee. this means, mr spidla, that the report does not call for the posting directive to be repealed or amended. the report was initially full of condemnations and criticisms of the court, but this has been removed. that's what we're talking about now. to emphasize this, i would like to quote in english: i will quote the english text of paragraph 27: welcomes the commission's indication that it is now ready to re-examine the impact of the internal market on labour rights and collective bargaining; and: suggests that this re-examination should not exclude a partial review of the pwd meaning not exclude. madam president, i would like to thank you. this means that there is no requirement for change. however, the commission's review of how this works in the different member states is welcomed. if this review gives rise to changes, they should not be excluded. i would like to say this because the posting directive plays a very important role. a million people have opportunities to work in different countries. it is also about equal treatment, the same right to work in all parts of the union, even if you have a collective agreement from your country of origin. that's the question. as long as you comply with the rules of the posting of workers directive, you have the right to work throughout the eu. this was also the court's conclusion in the laval case, among others. mr commissioner, madam president, the criticism directed at the court of justice is no longer in the committee's proposal and there is no call to tear up the posting directive, it is important to remember this before the next debate.
madam president, i would like to thank you. thank you, mr andersson, for an important report. a lot of people are talking about the lavaldomen where the swedish union went too far. there are a lot of things in the report that i do not like. there is a tone in the interpretation of the ecj and several points point to what jan andersson originally wanted, to tear up the posting of workers directive. but that is not what the revised report says, as gunnar hökmark very rightly points out. the question now is not to exclude a partial revision of the directive, which is more in line with the opinion of the imco committee, for which i was responsible. the vote will hopefully further confirm that the posting of workers directive does not need to be repealed. please see proposals 14 and 15 from the alde group. - what? it is wrong to think that the swedish model is best preserved by going through brussels - it is the opposite! if we go through brussels, the swedish model of responsible partners can be threatened and we will have legislation and minimum wages in sweden. it cannot reasonably be in the interest of the swedish trade union.
madam president, i would like to thank you. one of jan andersson's main conclusions is that the labour market should be safeguarded by changing the eu's posting directive. the best solution for sweden would be to have the eu treaty clearly stipulate that labour market issues are to be decided at national level. if there is one thing we should have learned from the laval judgment, it is that our labour market should not be governed by eu legislation. the junilista advocates a swedish exemption from eu labour law. it would be interesting to hear what jan andersson thinks about this proposal. is eu law always the way forward? the law is the result of the fact that the european social democrats and the european parliament's political leaders have agreed to changes in the eu treaties, giving the eu and the ec court of justice more and more power over labour market policy. we will of course vote against mr andersson's praise of the lisbon treaty.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? a few brief comments: there is a difference between the court's role and our role as legislators. the court has spoken. now, as legislators, we will act if we find that the court has not interpreted the legislation as we want. in the report we say that we and the commission will act. we should not rule out changes to the posting of workers directive, which we also point out. there is no contradiction between freedom of movement and good social conditions, quite the contrary. a few words on the amendments tabled by the ppe-de group. unfortunately, they contain a number of contradictions where they run counter to the compromises. on the one hand, one criticises unilateral statements by the council, on the other hand, one welcomes statements. there are many contradictions in their amendments. i reject the exemption for certain countries because these are european problems which we must solve together. different labour markets should work side by side. we are in favour of the new treaty because the problems with the judges were in the old treaty. i am not saying no to action at national level. we need such measures in sweden and germany, for example, but we also need action at european level. finally, i would say that it is now up to the commission to act. if the commission is not responsive to parliament and, above all, to what the people of ireland, germany, sweden and other member states are saying, the european project will have a hard time. this is one of the most important issues for the citizens of europe. free movement - yes, but good social conditions and no social dumping. we must work for it, so listen to parliament! (applause) i would like to thank you.
madam president, i would like to thank you. two things are important. first, the financial crisis. it is important that we speak with one voice from europe. the last time we shared crisis management with the united states was when we were supposed to discuss terrorism. we imported a lot of rules that now feel very foreign to the european idea of personal privacy. in this crisis we need a european line and therefore common rules and standards are needed for things like transparency, soundness and what financial instruments are to be used in the european market. the stability pact is a great tool for this. secondly, the climate package. we have voted on the climate package in the two main committees and by a large majority have decided to support the commission's proposal. we will not back down on the goals and timetable. if the presidency is serious about emphasizing both the objectives and the timetable, we are actually waiting for information on when we can sit down and negotiate concretely. on the part of parliament, we will use the codecision and codecision procedure provided for in the interinstitutional balance. (applause) i would like to thank you.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? now we are late. we have many reports to consider and the report for which i am responsible is at the bottom of the voting list. since there is a risk that many members will leave the chamber, i would like to defer the vote on the andersson report until tomorrow. i would also like to hear whether the other political groups are in favour of this. (applause) i would like to thank you.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. we also support the main content of parliament's report on the commission proposal and have therefore voted in favour. however, we oppose the request that the budget should increase from eur 60 million today to eur 2 billion in 2010 to finance the climate change alliance. we are also against the proposal to earmark at least 25% of future revenues from the auctioning of emission allowances to finance this increase in the budget.
in writing. − the fisheries policy of the eu is not, and never has been, based on well-considered and solidary decisions. fish stocks in europe have been declining dramatically in recent years, and little is being done to change this fact. the fisheries policy for which the eu should be responsible must be characterised by a long-term and forward-looking approach. in many ways, however, the report by mr busk is a positive development. the justification for the proposal states that the recovery of cod is of the utmost importance and that a total ban on cod fishing would be the best solution, even if this measure is subsequently rejected. unfortunately, the amendments to the report do not reflect the concerns of mr busk in the explanatory statement. the proposals are too weak to have any real significance. it is very unfortunate that the already inadequate regulation of fishing effort is being reviewed when the cod stock has 'improved substantially'. the focus should reasonably be on the need to safeguard recovery to a greater extent than at present. only then can we start talking about a possible revision. the report therefore sends the wrong signal, namely that the problem will soon be solved and that we will then have to start reviewing the system. the reality is quite the opposite. i therefore voted against the report.
in writing. - the condition of the cod is very serious and requires immediate and vigorous action. the commission's proposal is not sufficient, however, and it is lacking in several respects. it is also interesting to see how parliament suddenly thinks that a matter should be dealt with at national level. it is clear that the aim is to weaken the commission's proposals in favour of industry. you put a sonic goat on a gardener. we have voted against the report for the reasons stated above.
in writing. - france has submitted an application to mobilise the fund following the impact of hurricane dean on martinique and guadeloupe in august 2007. the commission has therefore proposed to mobilise a total of eur 12 780 000 from the fund to support france. junilista welcomes both national and international solidarity and aid actions when a country is hit by disasters. firstly, however, we believe that, as past experience has shown, the eu is not capable of managing disaster aid effectively with community funds. secondly, we are talking about a contribution of fractions of a billion of france's gdp. it is an unreasonable idea that the eu should intervene and co-finance projects which a rich member state should be able to manage on its own. we have therefore decided to vote against the report as a whole.
the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide climate change alliance. unfortunately, the basic intentions of the report have been peppered with writings that junilista cannot support, including the call to link the union's environmental commitment with a common foreign and security policy and detailed proposals on how the eu should initiate environmental initiatives in third countries. the climate change alliance can also be seen, according to some of the report's lines, as an attempt by the eu to extend its powers in forestry and maritime matters. we are strongly opposed to this use of questions to build the european union. junilista is strongly in favour of eu cooperation covering cross-border environmental issues. however, poverty reduction and environmental action at global level should be carried out within the framework of the un. the junilista has therefore, after careful consideration, voted against the report.
in writing. the report calls for greater cooperation between national administrations. it should be remembered, however, that it is institutional competition that tests the best forms of governance and distinguishes them from the less suitable. europe's diversity of administrative structures and the exchange of experience between them should be a good example. the report is full of well-intentioned words, but it lacks mainly concrete proposals on how to improve the management of structural operations in order to overcome the major shortcomings in the control of the financial aspects of structural operations. it is worth recalling that eu structural measures constitute the largest single item of expenditure for the union for the period 2007-2013 and that the european court of auditors in its report for the financial year 2006 finds that at least 12 per cent of the money paid out for structural measures should not have been paid out. the report also makes reference to the treaty of lisbon. however, this treaty has been rejected in the democratic processes. referring to the treaty of lisbon is therefore an expression of unacceptable arrogance. the future of the treaty is at the time of writing so uncertain that references to its content should be avoided. for these reasons, the junilista has decided to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. - it is interesting that the report now (in the draft report, point 5) notes the absence of a general strategy for the establishment of bodies at eu level. new bodies are being set up on a case-by-case basis and there has been an impenetrable patchwork of regulatory authorities, executive agencies and other community bodies. it is even more interesting to note that the majority in the european parliament has been a constant encourager of new bodies and only now realizes that the whole has been lost. the junilista group supports the main points of the report, but we are critical of the way in which the european parliament is now trying to introduce new areas of supervisory authorities which will present annual reports to parliament and that the directors of the authorities may be invited to the competent committees of parliament before they are appointed. we are sceptical of those proposals. first, it is appropriate that the commission be responsible for the management of those bodies. secondly, partisan politics may influence the appointment of directors of public authorities, who are themselves to be civil servants.
in writing. joseph kony and the lord's resistance army have been guilty of horrific crimes over the past 20 years which is why the international criminal court now wants to put him on trial. the conflict in the great lakes region, uganda and sudan continues, with new civilian casualties being claimed on a regular basis. it is the responsibility of the international community to put an end to this tragedy. the junilista generally takes a negative view of foreign policy resolutions. in this case, we are dealing with an organisation and its leaders who have been indicted by the international criminal court for crimes against humanity. that is why we have chosen to support this resolution.
in writing. - we have voted against this report by the european parliament's committee on culture and education. we are not voting against the idea of the erasmus mundus programme as such, but because of some of the detailed proposals that have been put forward by the committee and the commission. we are not in favour of any specific erasmus mundus visa as briefly described in the proposal. each member state has its own right to issue visas and we hope that they will have a free visa for students who are part of erasmus mundus. we believe that such visas cannot be regulated at eu level. we also reject the idea of eu financial support for an alumni association for those who graduate from erasmus mundus programmes. student associations should grow out of the needs and efforts of individual people. they cannot be built from the top down by the eu institutions.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the proposed amendments to the directive should be adopted by the council in accordance with the procedure laid down in article 189b of the ec treaty. the aim is to prevent one spouse from trying to take the time to bring an action in order to have the case governed by the law of a particular country which would protect the spouse's own interests. this objective is, of course, commendable in itself. however, in my view, the disadvantages of regulation outweigh the benefits. sweden has one of the most liberal marriage laws in the world, and that is something to be proud of. the danger of the original proposal is that it would mean that in some cases swedish courts could be forced to rule under maltese, irish, german or iranian law when a party seeks divorce. it would, in the long run, limit the swedish unconditional right of a person to apply for and also obtain a divorce - an area on which i would never compromise. my first thought was therefore to vote against the report. however, an oral amendment was tabled during the vote, which refers to the principle of public order. i still believe that the swedish model should be defended, but to encourage improvement i chose to abstain instead.
in writing. - it is with great disappointment that we of junilistan note the rapporteur's eagerness to go ahead with a question which has only recently been rejected by the council. in fact, the current brussels ii regulation, despite its shortcomings, is much better legislation than the one proposed by the rapporteur. the fact that the spouses are deprived of their right to choose their own court or jurisdiction shows the arrogance of the commission, and especially of the rapporteur, in dealing with the practices of all the member states. we not only reject this ill-conceived report but call on all members to stand up for the freedom of choice of spouses in divorce. complicated eu rules are the last thing these people need in these difficult times.
in writing. - as a european, i usually see a clear added value in european legislation. often problems are better solved by having more people think of them. this report is a regrettable exception to that rule. we have every reason to be proud of legislation which allows people to go their separate ways if they so wish, and therefore every reason to protect the system we have today in sweden. i therefore share the government's view that the commission's proposal for harmonisation is going in the wrong direction, when it has to take account of the practice of malta, for example. the vatican cannot be allowed to impede an active gender equality policy at home. the european parliament took a different view. that is why i intended to vote against the report - the amendment is also in the minutes.
in writing. - the fisheries policy of the eu is not and has never been based on well-considered and solidary decisions. fish stocks in europe have been declining dramatically in recent years, and little is being done to change this fact. instead, the eu is trading its aid for fishing rights in third world countries, thus also depleting their seas of fish. the local population along the coast is deprived of its livelihood and is forced to live on welfare, which is also rarely adequate compensation or compensation for the loss of income. the fisheries policy that the eu should be responsible for must be characterised by a long-term and forward-looking approach. such a procedure does not include aid for the rearmament and modernisation of fishing fleets, the ultimate aim of which is to increase fishing capacity. on the other hand, i would be happy to support measures aimed at supporting vulnerable local populations in poor coastal regions whose only source of income is fishing and who suffer immensely from the decline in fish stocks - largely as a direct result of the misguided eu fisheries policy. the proposals in the pedro guerreiro report do not contain any such measures, and i therefore voted against the report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the map of europe has been changed by armed aggression and war. russia has thus demonstrated its readiness to use military force to achieve political ends. let us not allow this to be pushed out of the debate we are having today. of course, this must affect our view of russia, but also the different requirements that need to be set for future cooperation. i want to warn those who compare with other examples. hannes swoboda do not compare with iraq! iraq was a dictatorship of the most brutal kind, in conflict with the international community. we may have different views on what happened, but do not compare iraq with a european democracy and sovereign state. there is no excuse for what happened to georgia. don't try to excuse what can't be excused. russia or any other country has legitimate security interests in any other european country. this must be the starting point of all european cooperation, otherwise the basis of cooperation is undermined and the door is opened to further violence. we have important cooperation with russia in a number of areas which must be pursued together. it's about energy, it's about iran, it's about the climate. i believe that europe and the european union should be open to cooperation, but clearly with the requirements that must underlie that cooperation. we will welcome cooperation, but we will also be clear about the requirements that must be met. we can also thereby contribute to a development in russia that upholds democracy and respect for fundamental rules. this must be the basis of european policy towards russia.
madam president, i would like to thank you. in my country, sweden, we have unfortunately become well acquainted with this problem. tourists in a good holiday mood are tricked into buying shares in apartments they are not very happy with when they return home, if they have any with them when they return home! the new directive on timeshare will bring much better protection and the same rules for the purchase of timeshare wherever in the eu the purchase takes place. for people to enjoy the freedoms of the internal market with confidence, they need to be properly protected. consumers must feel strong, safe and secure. the seller is prohibited from requesting a deposit during the withdrawal period and the withdrawal period is also extended from 10 to 14 days. this way, consumers do not have to worry about losing the deposit if they are not satisfied or want to withdraw from the purchase during the cooling-off period. the directive is likely to force out unserious companies that do not meet the requirements of honest marketing and reasonable purchase agreements. i think that many people today are reluctant to buy such services in other member states precisely because they cannot feel safe and confident in consumer protection. the harmonization and measures of the directive will give consumers stronger consumer protection. this could help to encourage more people to take advantage of such shared-housing services outside their own member state, and we would naturally welcome that. in other words, this is an excellent proposal with broad support. sun-loving nordic citizens thank the commission, the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs!
