in writing. - we chose to vote for the report because it deals with several very important issues concerning the situation of women in the balkans. we would like to make it clear, however, that we are opposed to any requirement to introduce quotas. how political parties and national parliaments choose to organise themselves is not a matter for the eu to decide but is up to them.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment. the need to take action to address a number of problem areas appears to be considerable. we welcome several of the papers aimed at improving gender equality in the region, access to child and elderly care, the importance of combating stereotypes and discrimination and the need for the countries seeking eu membership to meet the copenhagen criteria. at the same time, we are critical of the european parliament's unrelenting attempt to gain influence and political power at the expense of the national parliaments. the report also clearly advocates far-reaching intervention in the internal affairs of the balkan states, a development which junilista strongly opposes. we welcome several of the intentions in the committee's report and in the alternative resolution. the junilista has therefore, after careful consideration, voted in favour of the alternative motion for a resolution.
for the pse group. i would first like to thank alejandro cercas for a very good job. is a common working time directive really necessary? unconditionally, because we have a common labour market and we must have minimum rules on health and safety. this is about health and safety. there are two issues which are the main differences between us and the council. one is the idle time on call. if there's a resemblance between us. the similarity is that we both say that flexible solutions can be found by the social partners agreeing at national or local level. the difference is the starting point of these negotiations, where the council says that this is not working time, but that it is free time. but we're saying the bottom line is that it's work time. of course, it is work time when you leave your home and are at the employer's disposal. but we are not against flexible solutions. the individual opt-out is a question of whether it should be permanent or phased out. we mean to phase it out. first of all, it is not voluntary. consider the present-day labor market situation, where many people apply for the same job with a single employer. what are their choices with regard to an employer when applying for a job? secondly, i wonder whether it is not a challenge in the present situation for some to be allowed to work 60 to 65 hours while so many are unemployed? it's challenging. thirdly, there is the question of equality. who works 60 to 65 hours? well, it's men who have housewives who do the groundwork. the criticism of the council's proposal by the women's lobby is strong and justified. this is about health and safety. we've been trying to get the negotiations started. it is the council that has not come to the negotiations. we are willing to discuss with the council and we have tried and we have continued to do so, but we have our positions with which we will start the negotiations.
for the pse group. i would like to thank mrs thyssen, my colleagues, the council and the commission for a very good cooperation. as mrs thyssen and many others in this house have said, we have improved the directive in a number of areas. for us social democrats, a consistently high level of security has been the highest priority. i believe that we have achieved a high level of safety without imposing unreasonable demands on industry. in some areas we would of course have wished we could have gone further, but i think that the compromise is for the most part a good text today. our group has wanted third party certification for certain toys but has not received any support for this, either from the other groups in the committee or from the council and the commission. we are naturally disappointed by this, but since we consider the new directive to be an improvement on the current one, we will support it in the plenary. i am particularly pleased that we will have stricter rules on the use of carcinogens, mutagens and substances toxic to reproduction, something the socialist group could not have compromised on. we have a much better basis for calculation than the council proposal and we have removed the derogation from the substitution principle, which will mean that these substances will be used to a limited extent. for the most dangerous heavy metals, we've cut migration limits in half and banned their use in areas where children can come in contact. the precautionary principle has also been one of the most important improvements. the principle is now in the article, and therefore the market surveillance authorities can refer to this principle if there is reason to believe that a toy is dangerous without scientific evidence. we have developed a better definition of how toys should be designed so as not to cause suffocation. asphyxiation is one of the most common fatalities involving toys, and we consider it a great step forward that we have clear rules in this area. we welcome the fact that the new directive requires that toys must not damage hearing. the commission has promised to draw up a new standard and i hope that it will be kept. we have wanted to see a stricter regulation of allergenic fragrances where all allergens were banned except in very specific cases. however, following parliament's consideration, the use has been restricted compared to the commission's proposal and we hope that the list will be kept up to date if other allergenic fragrance substances are used in toys. we also welcome the clarification of the rules on warnings and the fact that more types of toys will have the warning text on the toy itself, otherwise it is easy to forget it when removing the packaging. warnings relevant for the purchase of the toy must also be visible to the consumer, whether the toy is purchased in the store or online. i hope that after this week's vote we can now tie up the sacks and count on safer toys under the tree in the future, please.
for the gue/ngl group. time and again we hear about products being recalled because they are dangerous for our children. to investigate the swedish toy market, my colleague from the left party and i bought 17 different toys in stockholm, selected at random, and then had a laboratory test the 17 toys. one contained illegal levels of lead, five other toys contained brominated flame retardants. this is, of course, completely unacceptable. this shows that better regulation is necessary, but also better control. new toys should be subject to mandatory independent testing. children, as we all know, are resourceful and resourceful, and they do not use toys merely in the way that the manufacturer may have intended. it is difficult, if not impossible, to predict how children will use a particular toy. therefore, legislation must also apply to broken toys, as some dangerous substances are released. the safety and security of children is non-negotiable. there is no room for compromise. i welcome the directive, but the united left would have wanted to go further. a total ban on allergens, carcinogens, mutagens and substances that affect reproduction. the safety of our children is more important than short-term financial gain, please!
for the ind/dem group. for me and many others here tonight, it is obvious that toys should be safe, because small children cannot read warning texts or assess the risks that may arise. products must also not contain chemicals that may be harmful to health. the junilists therefore welcome an update of the legislation in force in this area, but i would also like to warn against ill-concealed protectionism. toys that meet the safety requirements should be able to be imported and sold in the eu regardless of whether they have been manufactured in the eu or in asia, thank you.
for the ind/dem group. yes, mr president, i am a strong supporter of the eu's single market, but defence equipment is not like other goods and services. when a country exports defence equipment, it also takes a stance on foreign and security policy and must be able to take responsibility for this. the commission's proposal for a new system for the transfer of defence equipment is justified by efficiency and security of supply and the rapporteur, mrs rühle, essentially supports the commission. this is misleading reasoning. if the treaty of lisbon is pushed through by the european power elite against the rules of democracy, as is likely to happen, the proposal we are discussing here will be a major step towards military union. say no to this! intergovernmental solutions in this area are the way forward compatible with an independent national foreign and security policy. that's the kind of thing that works. the nordic countries are currently negotiating these issues. thank you for speaking.
the market improvement that this will bring about will promote the defence industry's opportunities in the european market. as we have heard, previous licensing systems have been complicated and administratively burdensome, and have also made it difficult to distinguish between loyal partners in neighbouring countries and new operators from third countries. this is now changing by reducing the barriers to transfer and harmonising and simplifying the rules, which will of course benefit market-driven countries. my country has a strong competitive defence industry and we have a good reputation in international contexts for peacekeeping and democracy-building efforts. for that reason it has also been extremely important for us, for me, as well as for my government, to maintain 100% swedish control over exports to third countries. we cannot under any circumstances accept that countries with a more accepting attitude towards undemocratic and belligerent states buy arms from sweden and then pass them on, export them beyond swedish control. we want a guarantee that the defence equipment we sell does not fall into the wrong hands, and we have got that with the introduction of so-called end-user certificates. i therefore support with great confidence the compromise presented in plenary and thank all the parties involved for a good job.
in writing. - if it were not for morocco's actions in western sahara, i would have voted in favour of the resolution. i have nothing against an association and exchange between the eu and morocco. however, in order to receive my support, morocco must first respect human rights and stop oppressing the people of western sahara.
the report also calls for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the european parliament and the council are able to take part in the negotiations on the future of the euro. under current legislation, such transfer is prohibited. the junilista agrees that it is of the utmost importance to protect the euro against counterfeiting. we believe, however, that this issue should be dealt with by the countries whose currency is the euro. sweden and other non-euro area member states should not interfere in the euro area countries' handling of the matter. we have therefore decided to abstain on this report.
in writing. − it is positive that funds are reimbursed to member states when the implementation rate of the structural funds is low. the second part of this amending budget deals with disaster aid and the establishment of a rapid reaction mechanism to deal with rising food prices in developing countries. we are not convinced of the proposed budget of eur 262 million. the question is more complicated than that. there are many examples of how the eu has dumped food prices and pushed out local food producers in developing countries. the eu has thus impeded the local food supply in developing countries. so now they want to use disaster aid as a short-term solution. what is needed is to change the eu's common agricultural policy and the eu's export refunds for agricultural products. we cannot therefore support this part of the amending budget at this stage. as the reimbursement of unused structural funds to the member states constitutes the largest part of amending budget no 9/2008, we have chosen to vote in favour of the proposal as a whole. that does not mean that we support the disaster aid proposal in this amending budget.
in writing. - the report by jamila madeira on european coastal tourism is a difficult and complex one, without concrete and clear objectives, but with a clear and concrete top-down perspective. i cannot see how the european parliament could improve the conditions for the tourist industry in the union by calling on the member states to build cycle lanes (paragraph 7) or to reduce airport taxes (paragraph 32). the conditions for coastal tourism differ in countries such as greece and sweden. that is why i voted against the report.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the proposed amendments to the directive should be adopted by the council in accordance with the procedure laid down in article 149 (2) of the treaty. the tourism sector and investment for the development of industry is a matter which should be dealt with exclusively by the individual member states. the junilista group therefore considers that financial investments in infrastructure and fixed transport links, for example, should be managed by the member states concerned and not be a burden on taxpayers in other parts of the eu. the junilista also questions the rapporteur's call on the european parliament to 'promote efforts to maintain economic activity outside the high season of tourism', a proposal which can hardly be described as anything other than an attempt to establish a cross-border employment protection. unlike the rapporteur, we also oppose the committee of the regions' wish to establish a european coastal fund. the junist party members have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union's institutions. the report's proposals are contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. we do not want a european media education for all children in the member states. individual member states shall design their curricula according to national conditions. we also do not believe that parents and older people in general have poor media literacy.
in writing. the rapporteur wants to introduce european media training at different levels, targeting families, schools, homes for the elderly and the disabled. that is a good idea, of course, but it does not belong at eu level. as an advocate of a narrower but more inclusive eu, i believe that this should be something that each member state should organise itself. that is why i voted against the report.
in writing. - we believe that media literacy is a matter for the member states. the draft opinion before us in the european parliament does indeed contain some good advice (and some less good advice for that matter), but this is a matter exclusively for the member states. the proposal for a report also addresses the curricula of education in the member states. junilista has pointed out in this parliament before, and does so again, that the member states have exclusive responsibility for the organisation of education and the content of education systems. subsidiarity is the talk of the town, but the reality is different. the eu should deal with crossborder issues, but not with those that individual member states can decide on themselves or that are already regulated by other international treaties. we have voted against the motion for a resolution for these reasons.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. however, we do not believe that the eu should legislate on how to regulate trade in defence equipment within europe's borders. defence equipment cannot be considered as other goods and services. as with export policy, legislation in this area should be a matter for the member states alone. where there is a need for cross-border cooperation, it should be at intergovernmental level. as junilista strongly opposes any attempt to build up military capability within the framework of eu cooperation, we are strongly critical of the commission's proposal. the rapporteur's amendments do not make things any better. the junilists' vote against the report is therefore not only a vote against the committee's resolution, but also a clear rejection of any form of militarisation of eu cooperation.
in writing. - we believe that the european commission's strategy for a more competitive defence industry in the eu is going in the wrong direction. it is another step towards military cooperation in the eu, with the aim of building a common defence. it is a development which we fundamentally oppose. we are committed to an independent and militarily non-aligned foreign policy. the protection of international law, democracy and human rights is not mentioned in the european commission's proposal. we believe that peace, democracy and human rights are more important than creating a new market for weapons. it is also a security policy area. sweden has the power of decision under the eu treaty. on this point, we believe that the european commission's draft law is contrary to the eu treaties. for these reasons, we shall vote against the motion.
in writing. i do not want a single market for arms. any country should be able to stop importing and exporting weapons if they so choose. weapons must not be exported without hindrance, even within the eu. unfortunately, we lost the vote on the amendment which expressed this. i therefore abstained in the final vote, as there were also positive aspects of the report, such as a reinforced control to stop exports to third countries and increased transparency and access to information for people's movements and others.
in writing. − the junist mandate in the european parliament is based on a promise to voters to work for greater transparency and prudent management of the eu's financial resources. the council now proposes that eu operations of a value not exceeding eur 1 million and co-financed by the european regional development fund, the cohesion fund or the european social fund should continue to be exempted from monitoring and control. this is simply unacceptable. we understand that the administrative burden must be put in relation to the amounts involved. nevertheless, we are critical of the council's proposal to leave the field open to the misuse of eu resources in the way proposed. for these reasons, the junilista has decided to vote against the report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'commercial vehicle'. we have absolutely no objection to ensuring that the european parliament's assistants have reasonable salaries and working conditions. but just as little as in the case of the transfer of meps to a salary system in the eu with a favourable eu tax, we want to transfer members' assistants to the 'eu tax rescue'. members and their assistants must be rooted in their national reality. wages and benefits shall be linked to the conditions in their member state or, in the case of assistants, to the conditions at the place of work where they live and work. neither members nor assistants should be isolated in an 'eu bubble' of high salaries and benefits far removed from the reality of the citizens they are supposed to represent. we have therefore voted against this proposal for an eu charter for assistants. for us, this is a matter of principle, not of the financial conditions of assistants per se.
in writing. − the new harmonisation rules for parliamentary assistants (‘other civil servants’) are not satisfactory from a nordic employee perspective. we consider that the new rules will lower social standards as regards family benefits and social benefits for the care of sick children, etc. there are also serious concerns about how the new rules will affect pension rights, unemployment benefits and the protection of employees against dismissal. however, one should remember the background for the proposal. there are serious problems for assistants on unregulated contracts at the european parliament. they are at times working in appalling conditions. this new regulation will put an end to that practice. we voted in favour of the proposal in solidarity with assistants who are exploited by greedy meps/employers.
in writing. - we have voted in favour of this statement by the european parliament. however, we have doubts about the proposal in paragraph 13 to extend directive 2005/29/ec. it is good that the statement points to austria and belgium as good examples of how to deal with unserious catalogue companies. we think that it may be enough to highlight these countries in europe as good examples. the legislators of the member states are certainly competent enough to take note of the good examples of other countries and themselves decide on new laws addressing the problems facing entrepreneurs in their respective member states in this field. institutional competition between member states is essential to solving the very problems dealt with in this statement.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. − madam president, i agree that this renewables directive is the best part of the energy and climate package. i am particularly happy that they managed to vastly improve the sustainability criteria for biofuels. i think the rapporteur has done an excellent job. we should congratulate ourselves on this directive because other parts of the package leave a lot to be desired. when full auctioning is postponed to 2027 – a generation from now – and more than 60% of emissions reductions can be made in third countries, where is the necessary push and where are the necessary incentives for the transformation of energy production, transportation, industrial production, etc.? this is serious for our long-term efforts to cut emissions but also for industry. we need innovation. if i were you, commissioners, i would be a bit worried about the risk of a collapse of the ets market as a combined effect of the low requirements for domestic action and the recession. i think this renewables directive is an outstanding example. it will give the necessary incentives for technology development, it will create new jobs and it will reduce dependency on the outside world, all of which we desperately need to happen.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. − we have had an emissions trading system in place in europe since 2005. this debate has been about improving these rules. yet it has felt like being in the movie killing fields with fire and mortar from all sides. much of the negotiations have been conducted in the committee on industry and i would like to mention some of the points that are included in the final outcome of the negotiations. the priority of a global agreement is of the utmost importance. the fact that we are getting the list of the competitive industries after copenhagen is very important. we have got through simpler rules and less red tape for small businesses. the system has become more open and transparent with a ban on insider trading, which ensures credibility and efficiency. we have introduced incentives for industry to use surplus gas and heat for cogeneration. we have more shipping and we have the ability to negotiate with the countries around us on emissions trading. the structure is still there, the environmental objectives are there, the instruments have been weakened by the actions of the member states. i regret that, but i would still recommend that you vote yes, because the cost of not having a climate package in place is infinitely greater. thank you, mr president.
for the alde group. the swedish author and nobel prize winner selma lagerlöf begins one of her most famous novels with the exclamation finally!, and that is what all of us who have worked on these different issues in the climate package can say. a big thank you, of course, to the french presidency, to the commission and to commissioner dimas who has personally put so much work into this, to avril doyle of course, and to all colleagues, and not least to the staff who have worked so hard. there are many of us who would like to go further, but let us remember that there are also many who do not believe in the climate effect, who do not want any decision and who would gladly have postponed all the handling, all the handling and above all the vote that we are going to have tomorrow, and that is the situation with which we are going to compare. in the best of worlds, i would wish for more emission reductions in europe, for fewer emission allowances to be given away for free, but at the same time i can see that we have achieved a number of positive things such as increased transparency, simpler rules, exemptions for small businesses, carrots, incentives for energy efficiency in industry and a threshold for aid to developing countries. shipping will be entering the system in a few years. it is clear that more allowances will be sold at auction. unfortunately, there was no earmarking of the proceeds for important investments and projects. however, member states have promised to demonstrate through transparency that at least half of the revenue is used to help developing countries, invest in forests, invest in new research and invest in new technologies. it is a promise which the member states must keep, and we in parliament will keep a close eye on them, and those who fail to keep their promises here will be hunted down with flashlights. what we are voting on tomorrow is not a perfect agreement, but if we say no to this, we are voting yes to nothing. then we have nothing to start with at the copenhagen negotiations in december. if we do not start this work now, the costs will be enormous, and what we have to do now is to take our responsibility in the vote tomorrow, for the environment, for industry and for the people of europe. the alde group will support all the proposals contained in the package. thank you, madam president.
for the gue/ngl group. emissions are increasing at an unprecedented rate. the increase of 2.2 parts per million of co2 per year is the fastest increase in co2 emissions in 650,000 years. we have to stop this. we need to get down to 350 parts per million of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere. the ets is the eu's main instrument for reducing emissions. so how we design the system is absolutely crucial. that is why i am disappointed that as much as half of the eu's emission reductions will be made in other countries through so-called flexible mechanisms. this has never been the thinking of the climate commission, which speaks of the flexible mechanisms as complementary measures only. i am also disappointed that the auction procedure is taking too long to come into full effect and that energy companies with sufficient capital will be granted free allocation if they build pilot plants for carbon capture and storage, known as ccs. nevertheless, i and the left group will support the proposal. the proposal is in any case an improvement on the current badly functioning system. we have introduced a ceiling for cdm projects and quality criteria for them. after all, auctioning of emission allowances will become the dominant principle. the upper emission ceiling will also be gradually lowered. this means that europe's emissions must be reduced continuously. in conclusion, it is a step in the right direction, but i wish we had gone further. as a rule, industry lobbyists and conservative member states have been successful in their attempts to water down climate ambitions. it is the eu in a nutshell. a lot of talk, but a little work when it really counts. thank you, thank you!
madam president, i must say that in these years that i have been in the european parliament i have learned that things do not always turn out exactly as you want. everything is based on compromise, but it actually usually ends up pretty good anyway. there are many people complaining at the moment, both those who think we should have decided on more ambitious rules, but also those who think that these rules are too ambitious. however, i think that we should be very pleased that we actually manage to reach a compromise at all. this is a step in the right direction, and it's a step that at least makes me proud. i am proud that this confirms that sweden remains the country that takes the greatest responsibility in europe, but also that europe is the part of the world that takes the greatest responsibility overall. as moderates, we have worked hard to achieve this compromise, both in the council and here in the european parliament. there is a lot to comment on this, but if i were to comment on what i am most pleased with, i would like to highlight what we have done with cars, that we take into account the environmental benefits cars do, but also that we give car companies reasonable time to develop new car models. as i said, however, we must not be fooled into thinking that this is the final step in the process. this is just the prelude to the important work that needs to be done in copenhagen. i think we have a good base there. thank you so much!
i want to thank satu hassi, because she's really done her best but unfortunately the council is not cooperative. unfortunately, we have a council that is turning a blind eye to the fact that we in europe must take our responsibility to solve the climate problem. we cannot take 80 percent of our climate responsibility outside our own borders and let the poorest countries on earth, when they're going to do their climate shop, pay dearly for their climate work while we do the simple and cheap things. it's colonialism at its worst. we need to reduce our own emissions by 70 to 80 percent if we're going to meet the climate goals. with this policy we will be dealing with 7 or 8 percent instead. it is completely unsolidarist and inadequate, and the question is whether future generations will not hold the council of ministers accountable in the international criminal court in the hague for such an irresponsible policy which affects not only people but also the earth. thank you, thank you!
mr president, there is a great weakness in the directive we are now discussing. by allowing such a high degree of flexibility through reductions in third countries, i wonder what the effects will be. cost-effectiveness is an important principle and the compromise in front of us would be ok as proposed if the objective were to cut 20% of global emissions. then it would make sense to do what is now being proposed. however, science tells us that we have to reduce over the longer term by 80% to 95%. i do not understand how that will be possible if we postpone most of our efforts until 2020 and beyond. we need a thorough transformation of energy transport in industrial production, construction etc. by allowing 60% to 80% of reductions in the non-ets sector in other parts of the world, this is not going to happen.
for the gue/ngl group. since 1990, emissions from the european transport industry have increased by over 30%. as early as 1995, the european commission proposed the emission limit of 120 grams per kilometre for european cars. but what we're about to vote on is a lost opportunity. when this law comes into force in 2012, 35 percent of cars will be exempt. the so-called eco-innovations can help car manufacturers to emit even more. the fines are so low that it's cheaper to ignore the law than to cancel production. what will happen to parliament's demand for 95 grams per kilometre by 2020? i didn't say anything! i would like to vote in favour of amendment no 50 by the left group and the green group. then we have a real binding emission standard for cars: 95 grams of co2 by 2020. if it passes, we in the left group are prepared to support this, otherwise not. it is said that if you lock two company executives in a room, they immediately begin to discuss how to divide the market between them and form a cartel. unfortunately, this is also the case for the two major political groups in this parliament, the ppe and the pse. once again the conservatives and the social democrats have chosen to run the other side of the house. the big losers in this, yes, are the environment and social democracy. for the environment, this is a lost opportunity to take control of car emissions. as for the social democrats, this agreement shows that they are becoming more and more like their environmentally hostile political opponents in the ppe. it's not good.
mr president, i would like to thank you. who are we protecting by making the car industry a low-stakes business? we're talking about loopholes in the law. they're not crawl holes. it's a freeway with emergency exits for the auto industry, free of all liability and all demands. who benefits from that? are we going to continue to produce cars that no one in the world wants to drive? - i know. most cars in the future will be sold in india and china and other developing countries, and they want energy-efficient cars. mother earth wants energy-efficient cars, and european consumers want energy-efficient cars. so are we going to let the auto industry live in a fantasy world where they can continue to make cars that nobody wants? no, it is bad policy for the environment, for consumers, for the economy and for research. in other words, a complete failure. that is why we cannot support this motion!
writer. - mr president! - what is it? tomorrow parliament will decide on the various elements of the climate package. this will have meant that we have negotiated and decided on important elements of the future european energy and environment policy in a very short time. it also means that parliament and the council will have completed the climate package much earlier than we will have implemented and decided on the energy markets. it should have been the other way around if we had been able to complete the decision-making on the electricity and gas markets and the crossborder markets much for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. as usual, we are presented with a document that is exemplary in parliamentary craftsmanship by those who have prepared the budget. at the same time, we find ourselves in the unreasonable situation that what we are doing is something that we should not be doing. we're in the middle of a discussion about how to spend more money. this parliament does not represent the taxpayers, it does not want to withhold spending, but is concerned that not enough money is being spent. more than two thirds of the money goes to things that are not only something we should not be doing as a parliament, but are also being used for the wrong things. they still go to agricultural policy, to rural development and to regional policy, to all those things that belong on the countries' own table and that they have to pay for themselves. there is also, quite rightly, a lot of talk here about 2009 being a year of crisis in europe, in the united states, indeed, in fact, in the whole world, and then the question is asked: what are we gonna do? - right here? and i said, there's nothing we can do about it here. the eu countries spend perhaps 40 to 45 per cent of their money on public expenditure. one percent goes this way and to the wrong things. we are marginalizing ourselves with this approach. thank you so much. thank you.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you. as the rapporteur for the budgetary committee on industry, i have to say that we in the committee agreed at a very early stage, even before the crisis hit, that we must increase our efforts, particularly in the areas of climate and energy measures and small and mediumsized enterprises. we have now reached the same conclusion in the committee on budgets, and i would like to thank the rapporteurs for the unusually good cooperation on the budget this year. we also stress the need to focus on joint growth and infrastructure initiatives. i was listening to mr lundgren from the ind/dem a little while ago. he's got this all wrong. no one believes that we could have such a large eu budget that the eu itself could counteract the cyclical efforts. what we are going to do is common things that the member states cannot do, to build the eu together into a common market, a real common market. now that we have removed the barriers to trade, we must also have a common infrastructure, not least a common energy infrastructure, and a common research activity to become world leaders. that is what we are talking about, not taking anything away from the member states. i spoke for the first time in this budget debate in 1999. what was the conversation about? yes, about the same things as today, about jobs and growth, about simplification and efficiency, about more flexibility in the budget to improve the eu's ability to react quickly to new challenges. but unfortunately, too much is still rolling in the same wheels. the commission's initiative in the context of the recovery plan is therefore welcome, even if it came very quickly. in any case, i think the most important thing we can do now is to bring about a more substantial and stable change in the eu's budgetary policy. the results of the commission's open consultation on the long-term budget show what it is all about: a stable and long-term commitment to growth, environment and climate policies. it also means that we can move away from the situation we have with ad hoc operations with constant revisions of the budgetary plans. the long-term budget now needs a different structure. that is the most important thing the commission can do now. thank you, sir.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i speak for my whole group. we are opposed to the procedure now proposed. our president, francis wurtz, has already said at the meeting of the group chairmen that this is completely unacceptable. the vote on the climate package, one of the most important votes of the year, should be held in the same way as any other vote. it is undemocratic and it is terrible that we do not vote on each report separately. we must be able to vote on each report in its own right and we must have the right to table amendments which we vote on. i would like to see this proposal torn up and let us vote on each report separately and let us vote on the amendments that have been tabled, please!
i would like to say a heartfelt thank you to alejandro and all the others who have worked on this issue. i don't see the council here today. we have tried to open negotiations with the council, but they have not wanted to come to the table. we now have a large majority in this parliament. let us now come to the table so that we can have a good working time directive. we now know, by a large majority, what parliament thinks.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats decided to vote in favour of this report on the reform of the emissions trading system. in the main, however, we believe that the climate package as such is not ambitious enough. more will be required of the eu to meet the challenge of climate change. but we believe that this can be a very important part of the measures that must be taken. we are disappointed that the compromise between the council and the european parliament does not provide sufficient guarantees that part of the proceeds from the auction will go to climate action in developing countries. we also believe that the auctioning of allowances should have been more extensive and that the use of the cdm should have been further restricted.
in writing. the revision of the emissions trading system is a step forward from the current rules. emissions allowances will be gradually auctioned to industry, rather than being given away for free as is the case today. it commits the eu to adjust its climate target from 20% to 30% reduction by 2020 at a successful climate agreement in copenhagen next year. it also calls on member states to use their auction revenues for climate action in europe and beyond. unfortunately, the compromise is far from the level of ambition that the situation requires. instead of auctioning all allowances at the outset, auctioning will be phased in. this softening reduces the incentive to develop new and more low-carbon technologies. it also reduces the revenue that the eu needs to help developing countries invest in green technologies, adapt to climate change and protect tropical forests. at the same time, while up to half of the emission reductions can be achieved through reductions in third countries, the ets will remain a tool of limited effectiveness in the period up to 2020. despite its shortcomings, a 'no' vote would have been unthinkable. i do not want to take the risk of jeopardising the whole directive, which has many positive aspects compared to today.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats decided to vote no to this compromise on responsibility sharing because we believe it is completely unacceptable that not even half of the eu's emission reductions need to be done at home. we believe that this is the wrong signal to the rest of the world, which expects the eu to lead on climate change, and we are concerned that the eu is now failing to put clear drivers in place to develop new green technologies. we believe that the latter is absolutely crucial for employment and welfare in europe.
in writing. - i chose not to vote on the directive on the allocation of responsibilities. the main reason is that it sends the wrong signal to the outside world when the eu can do around 70% of the emission reduction by 2020 abroad. major efforts are needed to help developing countries invest in low-carbon technologies. however, this aid should not primarily be an alternative to domestic reductions but a complement. we don't have the time or the money to choose whether we're going to have a chance of preventing dangerous climate change. postponing the necessary domestic transition beyond 2020 is counterproductive. we have to start now if we're going to have any chance of getting to near zero emissions by 2050. industry needs strong incentives for the necessary transition in energy, transport, construction, industrial production, etc. the proposal for the non-accounting sector is far too weak in these respects. that is why i chose to abstain on this part of the package. a no vote would have put the whole package in jeopardy, and i didn't want to take that risk. the directive has a lot of good things in it, by the way, compared to the status quo. in particular, it is the first in the world to require binding reductions from all sectors not covered by emissions trading.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. we would have liked to see a more ambitious proposal with stricter fines, shorter transitional periods and a clearer long-term objective. we are also critical of the fact that the ethanol rebate was so small that it hardly justifies investment, even though ethanol contributes to emission reductions. however, we have chosen to vote in favour of the proposal as a whole, as we believe that a rejection would mean an even later introduction of environmental rules for the automotive industry.
in writing. the european council welcomed the commission's proposal to amend the directive on working time, but stressed that it was not enough to ensure that the directive was fully implemented. the council agreement contains an opt-out option which allows the social partners to depart from the weekly working time directive. this is how the swedish model of collective agreements is protected. this is also in line with the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions should be taken as close as possible to the citizen. the parliament's proposal is to exclude this opt-out option. that's why i voted against it.
in writing. - junilistan believes that it is first and foremost the social partners and secondly the swedish parliament that should determine our working time rules. this line has broad popular support and the successful swedish trade union movement has normally argued that the social partners should decide such matters in collective agreements without state interference. the trade union movement in sweden and other eu countries has now changed its mind and is therefore calling for major changes to the council's common position. the question now is to hand over power over the swedish labour market, not to swedish elected representatives but to brussels. the reason is concern that member states will gain competitive advantages and risk public health through long working hours and irresponsible regulations on standby time. but the eu countries are democratic states governed by the rule of law and are bound by the european convention on human rights. they have free trade union movement. countries that do not meet these criteria are not accepted into the membership. is there a problem? i would point out that the new situation that has arisen should be dealt with in a conciliation process between parliament and the council. i have therefore voted in favour of the amendments on the opening-up of the market and the uk's opt-out to promote such a political process.
in writing. − employment rights and working time are two of the pillars of the swedish labour market model. in today's vote on the working time directive, it therefore seemed natural to follow our swedish line and defend the collective agreement model by following the compromise reached in the council. unfortunately, there was no opportunity to support individual amendments which were good, as that would have overturned the whole compromise. the council compromise allows for the opt-out which ensures that we can maintain our swedish model. the choice of the social democrats to risk our swedish system in favour of more eu legislation is remarkable and calls into question their support for the swedish labour market model.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of workers from risks related to exposure to carcinogens and to the use of carcinogens in the manufacture of foodstuffs. we welcome the more effective crossborder follow-up of traffic offences and the establishment of an electronic information system as a means of increasing road safety. road safety will always be a high priority for us. the proposal lacks a legal basis, unfortunately, as it opts to treat traffic offences, which in sweden and several other member states are within the criminal justice system, as an administrative matter. this has the effect of making it difficult to apply the directive in sweden and in several other member states, but also of calling into question the legal basis of the directive as a whole. we have therefore decided to abstain.
in writing. the commission proposes to introduce, by means of the proposed directive, rules for the imposition of fines for certain traffic offences committed in member states other than the driver's own. the commission and parliament's proposal deals with the issue through a supranational decisionmaking process under the first pillar of the eu. however, like the swedish government, we believe that the proposed directive, since it has a criminal law purpose, should be dealt with on an intergovernmental basis within the framework of judicial cooperation under the third pillar of the eu. we have therefore decided to abstain.
in writing. - this report proposes the creation of a specific electronic system for the exchange of information on drivers between member states. it will be a simple and easy way of asking those who drive too fast, without a seatbelt or at a red light to pay a fine in the country where the traffic offence was committed. the idea behind this proposal is certainly a good one. but unfortunately the proposal leaves much to be desired. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. the technical equipment for traffic controls and the methods used for traffic controls are also to be harmonised. there are also questions about the legal basis of this proposal, as sweden has pointed out in the council. the junilist has therefore voted against this proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr giusto catania, both for the report and for your unwavering commitment and work to respect fundamental rights. you can never compromise on basic rights. there can never be any political or cultural reason to compromise fundamental rights. and to mr szymański i would say that the fundamental rights of course also include reproductive rights. they also include the right to their sexual orientation. what is important now is that all the eu institutions work to ensure that the fundamental rights, the charter, are not just words on paper, but that we now have to ensure that concrete action is taken. we must ensure that fundamental rights are respected and that applies to all groups in society, please!
mr president, i would like to thank you. in the eu member states and institutions and in the western world in general, there are serious problems of discrimination against lgbt people. in many parts of the world, the problems are bigger than that. there, one even risks imprisonment or execution for his sexual preferences. this is completely unacceptable and should not be happening in 2008. i believe that human rights are universal and not relative. you can't invoke old customs and customs to defend hate against lgbt people. no, human rights apply to all people in the world and these rights should be inviolable. every person who calls himself a democrat must take responsibility and fight intolerance in all its forms. i am afraid that there are many members of this parliament who have hostile views towards homosexuals. this was particularly evident before and after the exhibition i hosted in brussels last week. i was very appalled when i read the comments, but unfortunately i am not very surprised. the fight against intolerance and for human rights must be waged everywhere with our friends, nationally, in the eu and globally through the un. i therefore welcome the initiative of the french presidency. i actually had a minute and a half. thank you, thank you!
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. first, a brief word on the value of vocational training. it is important that vocational training in each eu member state develops. it is important for the eu itself, for competitiveness and for our ability to assert ourselves in the world, but it is also important for the individual that we have a high quality vocational training in the eu. that's the general framework around this. we know that vocational training is organised very differently in the individual member states. it is not that this proposal for a reference framework threatens the way in which member states organise vocational training throughout europe, but that it can build on the traditions that exist, whether they are organised locally, regionally or nationally. there has been a common framework for quality assurance and vocational training in the past, but the member states have now wanted to develop this framework, not least in view of the increasing globalisation and the need for higher quality in education. in this work we have been doing on the quality framework, i would like to thank the commission and the council for the excellent cooperation that has enabled us to come up with joint solutions. what is it then? the member states have been very keen that we should not be giving them stickers, but something which they see as adding value to themselves. we need to exchange experiences. i have myself visited pilot projects on quality assurance in motor vehicle training for young people back in my country where there is a collaboration going on that is very, very rewarding. we have a common labour market, we have common objectives and therefore it is important that we have this kind of exchange of experience and a common framework within which this exchange of experience takes place. we have also been careful, together with the member states, to say that the indicators presented here should not be seen as a means of control but as an asset for the member states. they shall be able to be used when they are assuring quality and developing their systems. i hope and believe that this step, that we are now reaching an agreement, will mean that vocational training will develop in the internal market. i am quite sure that it does, and i am also quite sure that the exchange of experience and the tools now being made available to the member states will be of benefit both to the member states themselves, to the eu and to individuals. thank you, thank you!
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? mr takkula is not here anymore, but he asked whether there is a contradiction between having ambitions at eu level and the fact that education systems are still national in nature. i see no contradiction in that. i think that this reference framework is based on ambition at eu level, while at the same time we have confidence in the member states. we give them opportunities and what we call a toolbox to use. i don't think there is any contradiction. i would then like to stress what mr figeľ said about the fact that in the current economic and employment crisis it is even more important to invest in education. this is what will help the eu to be competitive, but also to give individuals the tools in their lives to move on and invest in further education throughout their lives. i would like to conclude by thanking the commission again, but also all the shadow rapporteurs. we have had a very good cooperation during the journey and i too would like to wish you all a merry christmas and a happy new year.
for the pse group. mr president, commissioner and vice-president barrot and members. i would like to begin by thanking javier moreno sánchez and the committee, because we have had a good discussion about frontex and eurosur this time, but i would also like to thank them for following frontex's work so closely on an ongoing basis. it is necessary because so many people are concerned about how frontex will develop, if it is to become the eu's bulwark against the rest of the world and the poor globally. it has recently emerged that frontex did not interpret it as having a mission to combat trafficking in human beings. i am glad that i have the support for this now, and that we are clarifying this. i think everyone here agrees on the importance of doing everything we can to help stop people getting into trouble when they apply for eu membership. they are not criminals but they are poor, and they seek a better life for their family. i am also pleased to receive support for the deepening of the rules applicable to the seas, and in particular the mediterranean, in ec law and in international law. it is unacceptable that fishermen who take refugees should be suspected of smuggling, and that there is a lack of clarity as to whether they should be able to release the refugees, for example, in the nearest port or what rules apply. it is therefore good that in future programmes for eu asylum, refugee and migration policy we now also address how we can make better use of frontex in future work. before i close, i would like to wish all my colleagues, our wonderful libe chancellor, president barrot and commissioner barrot a merry christmas and a happy new year, thank you!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i appreciate the parts of the report that talk about the sanctity of human life and the right of return of refugees, that they are not sent back to an unacceptable situation. i am very concerned, however, that frontex will become part of the construction of fortress europe. i feel that our solidarity with each other to close our borders is greater than the solidarity with the people who come to us. we have some kind of false image that europe should have some kind of disproportionate share of the world's refugees. that's not true at all. it is the poorest countries on earth that receive by far the most refugees. we're only taking in a stream of these people. moreover, when we talk about integrating return agreements into all agreements with other countries, i think we are completely wrong. first, we'll create development through fair trade agreements, and then maybe we'll have fewer refugees, too. it's a much better policy for us and for other countries.
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. thank you, gianluca susta. it would have been easy for us to agree to a good deal here. but parliament's rules force us to table separate resolutions without the possibility of voting on individual amendments. this makes it difficult to reach a compromise where the will of the majority in parliament has a chance to be expressed correctly. this is very unfortunate because it means that if, for example, the green resolution is not supported tomorrow, we will be voting here tomorrow on a proposal to control the internet and the content on the internet and to make the distributors responsible for it. that would be very unfortunate, because that is not even the will of parliament. consumers and people are threatened in two ways by piracy and counterfeiting. you can be exposed to environmentally harmful goods, you can be exposed to health-friendly counterfeit medicines and thereby be directly affected. but we can also be threatened by excessive measures to protect trademarks, especially copyright. it's all about finding a good balance. i think that parliament's clear message to the commission and the council, as they continue the negotiations, is that whatever the outcome of the vote tomorrow, we will be clear that personal use without profit should not be criminalised. acta will not provide access to private computers, music players, etc. that is a clear message from parliament. as regards criminal law, we must vote for the green alternative proposal if we do not want to have some kind of image that we could suddenly introduce criminal law at european level. we have no mandate whatsoever to do such a thing. the question is whether it would work and how it could balance a scale of punishment in one country when the scale of punishment in another country would actually go completely wrong. so doing it on an international level does not seem to be working at all. that is why the green proposals are better. the original proposal states that no exemptions should be made for travellers. it is not reasonable to equate a traveller who brings goods worth up to eur 400 with a businessman who can bring 50 containers. in particular, it is not reasonable to vote against the rules on qualitative content online, qualitative statistics regulating content and, in addition, secondary liability and liability of intermediaries. in order to get more people to vote for the green resolution, we will table an oral amendment to delete article 15, which was apparently a bit controversial, and i hope that many will support our proposal, thank you!
madam president, i would like to thank you. this is a very, very broad topic. it's about everything from, as i talked about earlier, medicines, to car parts, to designer products and illegal downloading. it is clear that counterfeiting is a major problem in this area and that it threatens valuable assets and even security. but there is a lack of clarity about the actual value of these products and how much of it is available on the european market. that is why i think the commission's inquiry is a very good one. i have chosen to focus on acta, the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement, the trade agreement currently being negotiated between the united states, japan, the european union and other countries. that is a case of too much closeness. we all react to rumors about what's going on. there are legitimate concerns, ranging from the need to search computers at border controls to mp3 players. we've heard rumors of a ban on region-free dvd players. i would argue that this uncertainty, these rumors themselves, are damaging the fight against piracy, the fight against counterfeiting. that is why i think that we all here in the house are calling for more openness. we need to know more clearly what mandate is really behind this, what the commission wants to achieve and what it is unable to accept. i myself focused in my amendments to the report, which i was fortunate to have heard, on what we do not want to see in acta. i pointed out in particular that we must not have measures which restrict personal privacy, that we must not go beyond the existing legislation in this area and, last but not least, that it must not inhibit innovation and competition. it is a pity, however, that in such an important area we have to define what we should not do when there is so much important work to be done. but the reason for that is precisely the secrecy, the uncertainty that secrecy creates. we must not get ourselves into a situation where the fight against this, the tools we take to our aid are bigger problems than the piracy itself. that's what i'm worried about. thank you so much!
mr president, many of our colleagues want to vote in favour of the idea in the green resolution not to make internet providers and telecom providers responsible for the content on the internet and so on, but many of those colleagues who want to vote in favour of the green resolution do not like paragraph 15, which is related to another subject. in a spirit of christmas compromise, we would like, in an oral amendment, to withdraw paragraph 15 from our resolution, in order to enable more people to vote for it.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to reduce the number of member states' contributions to the eu by half. the bulk of eu money goes to unnecessary or socioeconomically harmful activities, including agricultural policy, the cohesion fund, fisheries policy and support for various forms of information campaigns. in addition, there are the costs of the european parliament's commuting between strasbourg and brussels and of institutions such as the european economic and social committee and the committee of the regions, which should be closed down immediately. agricultural policy is particularly reprehensible. the money goes from consumers to often very wealthy recipients. farmers in the world's poorest countries are losing out to competition from eu subsidised farmers. the member states are constantly being reminded by various eu institutions of the importance of reducing public spending. at the same time, this house is constantly demanding increased expenditure at eu level. this is baroque. public expenditure by member states is directed at schools, health care, research, infrastructure and support for vulnerable groups in society. the bulk of eu spending is spent on a crazy agricultural policy, misguided structural funds and funding of institutions ripe for closure. our rejection of the budget proposal should be interpreted as a demand for a sharp cut in eu budget expenditure and a halving of the member states' eu contribution.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the proposed amendments to the draft regulation should be adopted by the council on the basis of the opinion of the european parliament. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament and the council. the report contains many good recommendations, not least on measures to facilitate the mobility of students and workers within the member states. however, we do not consider that the recommendations which are intended to influence the curricula of the member states are compatible with the principle of subsidiarity. the number of hours of physical education per week of study and the possible inclusion of media literacy in national curricula are best decided by the member states themselves.
in writing. the committee on culture and education wants to make a fresh contribution to the field of education. we in the junilist group would like to point out once again in this house that education policy is a policy area for which the member states have political responsibility. as always, the european parliament's committee on culture is very imaginative in its reports. this report again addresses the question of school sports. the proposal for paragraph 4 takes the view that the curriculum should provide for at least three sports lessons per week. this is yet another example of how eu politicians and officials go into all areas and all levels of detail in their eagerness to centralize political power. subsidiarity is celebrated in the party talk but never respected in the political practice. we believe that the european parliament has nothing to do with this area and have therefore voted against it.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. we share some of the concerns people have about the developments that frontex is opposing. we do not think that frontex should be militarized and therefore voted for amendment no 2. frontex should not mean that the eu is building higher walls against the outside world, but it is important for us that the eu states promote a generous refugee and migration policy. however, we welcome the discussion on frontex to which this case has contributed in the european parliament. it is positive that the european parliament has instructed frontex to include the fight against trafficking in human beings in its mandate and also to ensure that ec law is consistent with the international law otherwise applicable in this area, in order to enable the eu to intervene as effectively as possible to help people in need.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' in the definition of motor vehicles. the committee's report and the alternative motion for a resolution, however, unjustifiably advocate far-reaching legislation at eu level to tackle the problems of counterfeiting. the junilist is opposed in particular to the proposals for the coordination of the activities of the judicial and police authorities and the harmonisation of the criminal law of the individual member states. for these reasons, we consider it necessary to vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. the responsibility for these challenges lies with the un. we are strongly critical of all the texts in the report which advocate the continued development of the eu's military capability and have therefore voted against the report.
